#5919. FP’s 2026 Holiday Gift Guide

Roundup: FP’s 2026 Holiday Gift Guide

It’s been a rough year for travel. Oil prices skyrocketed after Iran closed the Strait of Hormuz at the start of the U.S.-Israeli war on the country, leaving many people thinking twice about the steep cost of airfare. More recent disruptions, including to U.S. air traffic control, made us determined to curate a list of holiday gift recommendations that will make you feel globally connected from the comfort of your own home.

So Brick your phone, set tea to steep with a gooseneck kettle, put on a Mongolian cashmere hoodie, and cozy up to complete a British paint-by-numbers kit while munching on Spanish jamón chips. (Fear not: Those still inclined to travel will also find our favorite bags and gadgets.)

It’s been a rough year for travel. Oil prices skyrocketed after Iran closed the Strait of Hormuz at the start of the U.S.-Israeli war on the country, leaving many people thinking twice about the steep cost of airfare. More recent disruptions, including to U.S. air traffic control, made us determined to curate a list of holiday gift recommendations that will make you feel globally connected from the comfort of your own home.

So Brick your phone, set tea to steep with a gooseneck kettle, put on a Mongolian cashmere hoodie, and cozy up to complete a British paint-by-numbers kit while munching on Spanish jamón chips. (Fear not: Those still inclined to travel will also find our favorite bags and gadgets.)

I’ll be the first to admit that I have a big problem—er, a bag problem. My closet is practically overflowing with thrifted purses, and my FP colleagues know that I come into the office carrying not one or two sensible totes, but three or four. So trust me when I say that I know bags, and this one stands above the rest.

Baggu’s Medium Nylon Crescent Bag is a workhorse, ideal for a friend or loved one looking for a trusty companion for weekend errands, jet-setting adventures, and everything in between. I’ve previously recommended Baggu’s packing cubes for their durability and cute patterns. This product has those same qualities.

Sleek and roomy, the crescent bag is available in a range of colors, materials (mine is leather), and sizes, although I’ve found that the medium-sized pouch is perfect for my needs.

JisuLife Portable Handheld Fan Ultra2 ($72.67, currently on sale)

I’ve traveled to a bunch of countries over the years, but my luggage tag collection is not the largest at FP—not by a long shot. Many of my colleagues have been to more destinations and stayed longer, just one of the things that makes working here so delightful. In the office, everyone has a travel tip.

Mine: The gadgets you take with you should be multifunctional. The exemplar of an all-purpose gadget is this freakishly powerful fan that’s also a power bank, flashlight, and handy-dandy keyboard cleaner. I keep it in my carry-on, fan myself before takeoff, charge my phone over the Atlantic, and light up the seat pocket on arrival to make sure I didn’t leave my passport behind. As for my keyboard—it’s reliably crumb-free.

Etnodim makes high-quality, gorgeous interpretations of classical Ukrainian clothes, from embroidered shirts and dresses to traditional kerchiefs. On a trip to Kyiv this fall, I got a traditional shirt, known as a vyshyvanka, for my daughter from their brand store in Podil, Kyiv’s equivalent of Brooklyn. Fortunately, you don’t have to travel 30-plus hours by plane and train to get their items, which are also sold online.

While I’d heartily recommend Etnodim’s wildly colorful designs, their more basic items—like this merino wool scarf—go with anything. Best of all, you’ll be supporting a Ukrainian brand that celebrates the country’s unique identity, in opposition to Russia’s best efforts to stamp out such cultural expressions.

Breville Milk Café milk frother ($199.95)

After realizing just how much money I was spending per month on lattes at my local (international chain) coffee shop, I decided to look into making them at home. But I didn’t want to spend thousands of dollars on some behemoth machine that requires a specialist degree to operate, either.

Enter the Breville Milk Café milk frother. It’s super easy to use and clean, it doesn’t take up much counter space, and it reliably makes the perfect steamed milk to my desired temperature and amount of foam. I’ve used it at least once—if not twice—a day for the last five months, and it hasn’t let me down yet.

But that’s just the milk part of the equation. Now on to the coffee part…

Breville Nespresso Original Essenza Mini Espresso Machine ($174.99)

The Nespresso Original Essenza Mini Espresso Machine is basically a Keurig for espresso. You don’t have to grind beans or fiddle with a million buttons and levers to make a single shot of espresso—just pop in a Nespresso pod; make sure the little, easily removable water tank in the back is full; and hit a button.

This machine is also compact, so it doesn’t monopolize your kitchen. Best of all, the espresso tastes just as rich and creamy as it would if you went to the corner coffee shop—and you don’t even have to put on pants to enjoy it.

Ortlieb Vario Lite pannier backpack ($195)

FP recently moved offices to a space farther away from the D.C. Metro’s Red Line—and so I became a bike commuter.

Biking has long been my preferred mode of transportation on the weekends, when I shuttle between yoga and Trader Joe’s with the help of a storage basket. But I quickly realized that commuting is an entirely different ball game. Laptop plus lunch is a heavy—and valuable—combo. I did not trust my basket, however adorable, to keep these items secure.

Enter: a pannier backpack. Rather than settling either for a backpack (which would strain my back while biking and probably make me sweaty) or a pannier (which would be difficult to transport after parking my bike), I decided to invest in this brilliant product from Ortlieb, a German brand that happens to be—like me—Franconian. It has transformed my commute.

The Vario Lite allows me to toggle seamlessly between pannier and backpack modes. It has an interior laptop pocket and outer compartment where I store my keys and phone for easy access. Its rolltop structure means that its size is flexible, and its backpack function is ergonomic. Did I mention that it’s waterproof, too?

The Vario Lite is the little brother of the Vario, a slightly larger pannier backpack from Ortlieb. I tried on both and found the Vario Lite to be a better fit on my 5’1” frame. But anyone can rest assured that there’s an entire line of high-quality pannier backpacks—in a range of colors—to choose from.

Brick Phone Distraction Blocker ($56.80, currently on sale)

The Brick—a magnetic square you can use to curtail access to your phone—is an invention I resisted for a long time. Its very existence was proof that we were living at capitalism’s nadir: You’re telling me I need to buy a little plastic brick to blunt the pernicious effects of this other little plastic brick?

And yet. The Brick has made my life better, and here are the numbers to back up that claim. Before acquiring the device, I was averaging 10 hours and 40 minutes on my phone per day. Now that number is down to just under eight hours. Sometimes, when there isn’t an international spy scandal all over the news, it has been known to hit seven.

That I have been wasting this much of my life scrolling is mortifying enough. What’s worse is how easy it was to break the habit. The minor inconvenience the Brick depends on is that once you block certain apps you can only unblock them by tapping your phone to the cube. Tapping the cube is very easy. But most of the time, it’s simply too much of a bother. So instead of doomscrolling, you put down the phone and do something else instead, such as engaging with your children or animals.

Turns out you can throw money at seemingly intractable problems. Capitalism, you win this round.

Scotch Precision Ultra Edge Scissors ($11.39)

I, too, initially recommended the Brick (above) but voluntarily scrapped my write-up lest attention spans wane while reading too many words about a plastic square. Instead, I’d like to recommend something analogue to fill your newfound free time: paper collage.

I recently turned to physical collaging as a cathartic supplement to the digital work I sometimes do for FP. It’s the perfect hobby for the world traveler with piles of ephemera or magazine readers with a backlog of vintage Foreign Policy and National Geographic. Pull together a gift collage kit with paper bits of your choice and these sharp, precise scissors.

These superior snips come recommended secondhand by Ray Orr, a collage artist and former visual journalist and co-founder of Living Room DC, a creative space in D.C.’s Mount Pleasant neighborhood. Consider gifting a workshop or drop-in session for collage or artmaking in the cozy spot.

—Lori Kelley, executive creative director

Cocinare FLOW B9 Gooseneck Electric Kettle ($55.99, currently on sale)

You know what they say: A warm cup of tea a day keeps the geopolitical crises away. Or maybe two cups, given the present outlook. Actually, scratch that—might be best to have three.

World leaders may have decided to shirk responsibility for confronting our warming planet, but you can still control the temperature of water at home with this luxurious electric kettle. No software required; this baby does not run on Outlook. Just plug the kettle in, set it to the optimal steeping temperature for your beverage of choice, and hit the button. A quiet moment with a perfectly warmed cup of tea is just minutes away. (For a slightly less precise but more budget-friendly option, consider this alternative.)

Everyman’s Library Pocket Classics ($16-$28)

If you’re like me, packing for a trip is a calculation of how much reading material you can bring to fill time spent in transit without losing precious real estate in your bag. “They’ll have bookstores there,” a wise friend once reminded me. But a smart traveler comes prepared!

Short story collections from Penguin Random House’s Everyman’s Library Pocket Classics Series are a compact travel companion and a great gift for someone you know who’s getting excited for an upcoming trip (or still reminiscing about their last one).

From India to Russia to Ireland, these anthologies compile literary short stories and book excerpts related to the featured country, continent, or city. Their contents can introduce you to a new destination, deepen your appreciation for a place you already love, or even inspire a change of itinerary.

I’ve found the anthology format to be ideal for the random stretches of time to kill when traveling between destinations. The stories are just the right length to digest on a plane, bus, or train ride, and can even make the inevitable delay more enjoyable.

Zeedog Ruff Leash ($15.20, currently on sale)

This year, I became the owner of a young dog—and also the continued owner of pair of aging arms. After having my shoulder wrenched by my puppy’s eagerness to chase D.C.’s rats a few too many times, I switched to this leash from Zeedog.

Through a simple shock-absorbing design, which looks like a stretchy wave at the end of the leash, the Ruff Leash absorbs the force when your dog yanks. It also comes, like puppies, in a stylish variety of colors. Strongly recommended for anyone foolish enough, like me, to get one.

Sony WH-1000XM6 Noise-Canceling Headphones ($348, currently on sale)

Living in a loud city like New York, I’ve long been a believer in a good pair of headphones. Even before advances in noise-canceling technology, hearing the Talking Heads instead of the roar of an oncoming subway was an amazing way to mitigate the sounds of crowded urban life.

Recently, I upgraded from my older Sony headphones to the new WH-1000XM6s. What they lack in catchy brand naming, they make up for in incredible sound quality and call clarity. From Zoom calls to catching background noise in video edits, they sound incredible while blocking out everything I don’t want to hear.

I’ve long admired the bold colors and vibrant, travel-inspired scenes painted by Rhi James, the U.K. artist behind Hebe Studio. Her paint-by-numbers kits offer a delightfully nostalgic way to slow down and create beautiful artwork of your own. With these kits—which are designed to be beginner-friendly and include high-quality paints, brushes, and canvas—it’s easy to feel confident as you bring your masterpiece to life. James’s enchanting landscapes are a unique gift that will add a personal touch to someone’s home while also giving them a creative escape.

—Sara Stewart, senior creative director, product

Quince Mongolian Cashmere Pullover Hoodie ($99.90)

Whether or not you’re comfortable thinking of yourself as the kind of person who wears a cashmere pullover hoodie, you will feel comfortable wearing a cashmere pullover hoodie. Quince cashmere is responsibly and sustainably sourced, so you can also feel comfortable about the conditions of the goat whose soft, soft hair you are wearing. This makes a great gift for anyone whose head gets jealous when they put on a sweater.

Schoolhouse x Pendleton Abington Hall Throw ($139.99-$238.99, currently on sale)

As winter approaches, there’s nowhere I want to be but snuggled under a blanket and sitting by a crackling fire. I grew up in the woods of the Pacific Northwest and, to me, Pendleton is the height of luxury. Since 1909, this Oregon-based mill has been producing wool blankets and textiles to outfit lumberjacks and the Beach Boys alike. Pendleton’s collaboration with home goods brand Schoolhouse marries its heritage product with modern style. This 100 percent wool, “Made in USA” blanket is sure to elevate any cozy space and keep you warm all winter long.

A confession: I have long known what the perfect holiday gift is, but I’ve kept it to myself. No more! It’s a puzzle from Liberty Puzzles, a wooden jigsaw puzzle made in Boulder, Colorado. (Full disclosure: I worked at Liberty Puzzles long ago, but unlike my friends at fast-food joints, who came away swearing off the menu, I left charmed having seen how the product gets made.)

What sets a Liberty Puzzle apart from the typical cardboard variety? For starters, the shapes. Each one is unusual, and thick, laser-cut pieces fall into place with a satisfying click. They also have a faint smell of burnt maple. Best of all, if you ever lose a piece—say, in the stomach of a mischievous furry friend—the company will replace it for free.

The catalog is quite global, including landscapes, art, maps, and vintage posters, so there’s something for everyone. I’ll make it easy for you: A gastronomical map of Italy for the couple fresh back from their honeymoon! A woodblock print of Japan’s snow-dusted Mount Haruna for the burgeoning snowboarder! A painting of Diana and her nymphs for the Roman history buff in your life! They even make kids’ puzzles.

Torres Selecta Iberian Ham Gourmet Potato Chips ($49)

Give the candy canes a break this year and stuff your stockings with chips!

I first encountered these pork-flavored potato chips at my neighborhood wine bar years ago, and they quickly became a cult favorite. They’ve been on my mind again, having recently returned from Andalusia, Spain, where jamón ibérico seemed to show up at every meal.

Salty, savory, and downright decadent, these chips would pair great with a dollop of creme fraiche; a dab of fig jam, pickled shallots, or salmon roe; and a glass of cava. They’re the perfect treat for the adventurous eater on your list, or anyone who’s still dreaming of their last trip.

Mens Soft Fleece Drawstring Elastic Waist Cargo Pants ($16.97)

Yes, you read that right: I’m recommending sweatpants.

My travel frequency has gone up a lot this year (not a complaint!), and I’ve put a lot of thought into what I wear on long flights. Cargo sweatpants have been a game-changer for comfort and convenience. The additional pockets are useful for carrying chargers, chewing gum, and other paraphernalia without constantly having to open the overhead bin. And the pockets have buttons to close them, so I avoid the constant fear of losing my passport. The sweatpants are also possibly the most comfortable item of clothing I own.

While I purchased my pair at a brick-and-mortar store in India (shout-out to Marks & Spencer), I’m sure this equivalent on Amazon is just as good. Having closed pockets is the key. And these sweatpants cost less than some Manhattan cocktails, so you can pick up a few to keep in rotation.

Bic 4-Color Original Retractable Ballpoint Pen ($4.38, currently on sale)

One of the dangers of having kids is the constant temptation to indulge in nostalgia for the objects of one’s own youth. But sometimes nostalgia can be an invitation to genuine rediscovery.

Such was my experience with the classic Bic four-color pen. I bought one for my fifth-grade son during this year’s back-to-school run-up and watched as his newfound power dawned. You’re writing in blue, and with a flick of the thumb: Boom! Green. If the mood strikes: Boom! Back to blue—or red?

I soon bought a four-color pen for myself and quickly came to a renewed appreciation for its simple design and small daily offer of variety. But reading about the pen’s history also gave me a deeper understanding of its geopolitical significance. I hadn’t known that Bic is a French company, and that the iconic four-color pen has always—since its original release in 1970—been a product proudly made in France. The factory in the town of Montévrain still closely guards its manufacturing secrets.

The Bic four-color pen isn’t just a writing implement—it’s a precisely engineered machine, an enduring symbol of Francofuturism. All of which makes it the perfect stocking stuffer for the status-conscious middle schooler in your life—or the wonky Europhilic aesthete.

Although I have never been much of a video gamer, I would be lying if I said that the 1990s classic Oregon Trail did not eat up hours of my childhood. Fighting to survive bandits, broken axels, and (of course) dysentery was a favorite activity of mine when the days were cold, the nights were long, and I needed an escape from our chaotic world.

So when I was gifted a physical version of the iconic game, I was hooked. The story is the same: Make your way from Independence, Missouri, to Willamette Valley, Oregon, without succumbing to drowning, starvation, or a host of other calamities. My friends and I have spent hours around my coffee table attempting to cross the treacherous 2,000-plus-mile passage without incident. There have been screaming matches, thrown cards, and more typhoid outbreaks than we can count.

#5918. The Model Middle Power

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

ANDREW YEO is a Senior Fellow and SK-Korea Foundation Chair at the Brookings Institution and Professor of Politics at the Catholic University of America. This essay is adapted from his forthcoming book South Korea’s Grand Strategy: Narratives and the Rise of a Global Nation.

What South Korea Gets Right About Geopolitics

In the ten months since Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney gave his widely discussed speech at the World Economic Forum in Davos, the question of whether, and how, the world’s middle powers can help rebuild a broken international order has become increasingly urgent. In his remarks, Carney stressed that although the old U.S.-led alliance system was collapsing, middle powers—states that do not have the ability to reshape global politics but that still possess substantial might—were well positioned to pick up the slack. But to do so, Carney argued, they would need to build up their “strength at home” and “act together” to harness their collective power, even in the absence of backing from the world’s hegemon.

Although the Canadian prime minister’s bold vision was widely applauded, some experts thought his agenda was overly ambitious. They noted, correctly, that it would be very difficult for middle powers to build a grand alliance composed of countries as diverse as Brazil, Canada, South Africa, and Turkey. “The path to unity among middle powers is fraught because this group includes rich and poor countries, as well as small and large ones,” wrote the economist Eswar Prasad in February. Carney, however, is right that middle powers have an opportunity to thrive going forward. Even when aligning with great powers, these countries can still exercise greater autonomy by developing national capacity and diversifying external partnerships.

South Korea offers a prime example of this flexible approach. For decades, Seoul has pursued a middle-power strategy centered on building domestic strength and forging partnerships with democracies everywhere while deepening its alliance with Washington. South Korea has been adaptable, recalibrating its security alignments, economic strategies, and diplomatic roles as regional and global conditions require. This has enabled a country smaller in size than most of the world’s states to punch far above its weight, exercising substantial influence in Asia and beyond.

Not every government can follow Seoul’s path, and South Korea’s strategy has its limits. As Seoul itself acknowledges, middle powers cannot escape great-power competition. But the country has begun to show what middle powers can accomplish when they leverage their partnerships and their own strength to protect their interests and deter larger threats. And by maintaining and even building its ties to the United States while asserting greater autonomy, South Korea has proven that middle powers can strengthen themselves without building whole new networks—contrary to Carney’s suggestion.

According to classic international relations theory, grand strategy is the exclusive provenance of great powers. The United States’s effort to contain the Soviet Union during the Cold War is a paradigmatic example; China’s pursuit of global economic primacy is another. Middle powers, by contrast, have little say in such strategies. Instead, these countries are seen as primarily reactive in their foreign policies, navigating around the decisions of the great powers rather than setting the agenda themselves.

But recent history suggests that the division between great and middle powers is less clear-cut than has been assumed. In practice, middle powers very much have their own affirmative visions that they pursue in the world. They have the leeway to decide which threats matter most to them, which partnerships to prioritize, and where to invest scarce resources. Japan, concerned with the threat posed by China, has been quietly exercising leadership in the Indo-Pacific by improving its ties with neighbors, increasing investment and aid to the region, and strengthening its own defense capabilities—all while remaining closely allied with the United States. India, worried about China, Pakistan, and growing economic headwinds, has successfully bolstered partnerships with the United States and other democracies but maintained its relationship with Russia.

Much like Japan and India, South Korea is worried about its neighbors. But for Seoul, this is nothing new. South Korean leaders have been concerned with the very thing Carney fretted about in his Davos speech—becoming collateral damage in conflicts between great powers— since at least the late nineteenth century, when it was caught between China, Japan, and Russia. The country has a well-known proverb, “When whales fight, the shrimp's back is broken,” that captures the sentiment that great-power feuds can harm countries caught in the middle.

To safeguard against this threat, Seoul has long sought to increase its self-reliance through industrial policy. The country transited from light to heavy export industries in the 1970s, turning it into a major player in the chemical, steel, and shipbuilding sectors. State investments helped it become a major global producer of batteries and semiconductors in the present era. South Korea takes great pride in what historians call “the miracle on the Han”—or the country's rapid rise from a war-torn and impoverished state to a rich one during the late twentieth century. They now use the state’s wealth to promote public goods globally, contributing to international development, global health, climate policy, and multilateral bodies.

Seoul has also built up a formidable military, one that experts consistently rank as among the strongest in the world. Today, many South Korean analysts argue that Seoul needs a nuclear arsenal for self-defense, even though the country officially remains under Washington’s nuclear umbrella. The state has simultaneously used its armed prowess to deepen relations with U.S. allies. Seoul, for example, has inked multibillion-dollar arms deals with European buyers, making it a key partner in rebuilding NATO’s industrial capacity. In 2025, it was the second largest supplier of weapons to European NATO members (after the United States). South Korea has also forged defense partnerships in the Middle East and Southeast Asia, widening Seoul's options and reducing its dependence on any single market. South Korea, in other words, has been able to convert its economic resources and bureaucratic capabilities into greater influence by expanding its partnerships, strengthening its voice in international bodies, and helping shape global rules and norms.

But even as it charts its own path, Seoul has no plans to abandon the United States, at least in the near future. In fact, the sanctity of the U.S.-South Korean relationship is a significant historical Korean narrative. Many South Koreans see the alliance as a bond inked in blood, thanks to the sacrifice both countries made during the Korean War, when they worked to stop North Korea, backed by China and the Soviet Union, from overrunning the south. Most South Koreans correctly believe that the continued U.S. military presence in the country—currently, 28,500 American troops are stationed on the peninsula—has played a major part in deterring North Korean aggression over the past seven decades.

At first glance, South Korea’s strategy may seem contradictory. On the one hand, the country wants to deepen its cooperation with the United States, on which it still depends for security. But on the other, it seeks greater autonomy.

Yet these twin goals are not actually at odds. By cultivating ties with Washington, Seoul has gained access to advanced military capabilities and technologies as well as a larger diplomatic platform to expand its regional and global interests. It is in part because of this relationship, for example, that South Korea has had such success at selling weapons to multiple NATO members, including Croatia, Estonia, Finland, Norway, Poland, and Romania. (Seoul has also built up a formidable diplomatic apparatus in its own right.) At the same time, South Korea helps make this compact worthwhile for the United States by providing it with valuable technology, including semiconductor chips. Seoul has also partnered with the Washington to help revive the American shipbuilding industry, agreeing to invest $150 billion in the U.S. sector.

Still, South Korea’s growing push for more independence in its foreign policy does result in some tensions with the United States that cannot easily be resolved. For instance, the current government’s push to give Seoul, not Washington, operational control over both states’ forces on the peninsula in the event of war has been rebuffed by the U.S. military. American commanders instead favor a conditions-based transfer after South Korea has proven it has the capabilities to lead a joint defense, rather than setting a fixed date. The two sides also have not reached a consensus on what role South Korea would play if Taiwan is attacked or blockaded by China.

Yet so far, Seoul has done an effective job at managing these differences. For example, in his second term, U.S. President Donald Trump has repeatedly accused Washington’s allies, including South Korea, of leeching off the United States and not paying their fair share. To extract more from Washington’s partners, Trump has implemented punishing tariffs. In response, South Korea could have been defiant and slapped on retaliatory restrictions, as other U.S. allies did. Instead, South Korean President Lee Jae-myung avoided rebuking the president and worked to meet American demands in ways that heightened the two states’ mutual dependence. For instance, Lee pledged to invest $350 billion in the United States to advance American economic and national security interests, including by purchasing more U.S. military equipment, and to increase South Korean economic support for locally stationed American forces.

Seoul’s strategy has not been a uniform success. The South Korean economy remains exposed to American and Chinese pressure despite its growth and diversification. North Korea is expanding its nuclear and missile forces and deepening military ties with Russia, creating new security headaches for which Seoul still needs U.S. assistance. The country is politically polarized, resulting in sharp policy shifts that can undermine long-term strategic planning. It also suffers from the lowest birthrate of any country in the world, threatening its tax base and the size of its armed forces. Beyond that, South Korea may not provide the ideal template for every middle power. Not every country in this category has similar economic resources, networks, or ties to the United States.

But on the whole, South Korea’s grand strategy has been effective, and it thus carries broader lessons. For one, it shows how adaptive middle powers can draw on their respective national narratives to find their strengths and to chart a path forward. Perhaps more important, it is proof positive that middle powers can exercise influence and achieve a measure of autonomy even without working together to build a neutral third pole, as Carney urged. It also illustrates that middle powers can remain firmly allied with the United States while developing their own independent capabilities. South Korea, then, demonstrates that states can reduce their vulnerability and widen their political options.

An earlier version of this article incorrectly stated that the South Korean government was known to be considering developing nuclear weapons.

With Its Allies, Seoul Has Enough Leverage to Push Back

Stephen E. Biegun and Emma Chanlett-Avery

#5917. Don’t Let China Hawks Exploit the Nvidia Scandal

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Argument: Don’t Let China Hawks Exploit the Nvidia Scandal

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

Amid the flurry of commentary over Nvidia and Jensen Huang’s “gone today, here tomorrow” act, there is one question that remains undiscussed: Has U.S. policy toward China succeeded in its aims of maintaining American preeminence and security?

The overwhelming reaction to Huang’s reinstatement has been bipartisan outrage at U.S. President Donald Trump’s interference in a private company, coupled with demands for stricter enforcement of U.S. export controls on the sale of Nvidia’s chips to China. As Sen. Tom Cotton said, “I have warned the administration from the start about Communist China obtaining our most advanced chips through both smuggling and remote access.”

Amid the flurry of commentary over Nvidia and Jensen Huang’s “gone today, here tomorrow” act, there is one question that remains undiscussed: Has U.S. policy toward China succeeded in its aims of maintaining American preeminence and security?

The overwhelming reaction to Huang’s reinstatement has been bipartisan outrage at U.S. President Donald Trump’s interference in a private company, coupled with demands for stricter enforcement of U.S. export controls on the sale of Nvidia’s chips to China. As Sen. Tom Cotton said, “I have warned the administration from the start about Communist China obtaining our most advanced chips through both smuggling and remote access.”

That sentiment is widely shared. But should it be?

There was a point sometime in the mid-2010s when U.S. policy shifted decisively from engaging China to containing it. This began during U.S. President Barack Obama’s second term, and then it accelerated with the first tariff war and the campaign against Huawei during the first season of the Trump show. It was cemented as consensus policy during the U.S. President Joe Biden’s interregnum and has been maintained during the second season of Trump.

This shift was driven by a growing consensus that engagement had failed. China’s entry into the World Trade Organization in 2001 was supposed to make it more democratic and open. It did not. China under President Xi Jinping instead turned away from political reforms, silencing any hint of opposition to the Chinese Communist Party (CCP). Internationally, Beijing adopted a more confrontational stance toward the United States while continuing to steal American intellectual property.

In this context, Washington embraced export controls on high-end technology that could enhance the Chinese military or give Chinese companies an edge in the race for preeminence in artificial intelligence. But instead of meaningfully containing or slowing China, these export controls have only succeeded in raising tensions and increasing the potential of a future war.

By most informed accounts, the restrictions in place since 2022 have delayed or impaired the ability of Chinese companies to build AI frontier models on the scale, scope, and speed of Anthropic or OpenAI. The restrictions on semiconductor manufacturing equipment sales to China have also created supply constraints that have pressured the Chinese military.

As a result, however, China has become far more self-reliant and aggressively built out its own domestic chip design and manufacturing. Though these domestic efforts lag behind the United States, Nvidia, and Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company by months or even a few years, this has not prevented dramatic breakthroughs such as the unveiling of China’s AI model DeepSeek.

What’s more, China has managed to evade export controls and obtain high-end chips, either through third parties and other countries in Southeast Asia or through good old-fashioned theft and copying. If Nvidia knowingly and purposely evaded those controls with the acquiescence of some in the Trump administration, that remains a profound scandal. But the reality is that even without this kind of corruption, sanctions have consistently failed to prevent countries from accessing things that are available worldwide, even highly controlled military equipment. A country as large and as intertwined with the global economy as China simply cannot be cut off for long.

By trying to control China’s chip access and only partly succeeding, Washington has pushed Beijing into a more unequivocally hostile stance. The CCP and Xi perceived, rightly, that the United States had no intention of allowing its peaceful rise and concluded that confrontation was inevitable.

Especially in the past two years, Washington also began to cut off China’s access to aspects of the domestic U.S. market. The Trump administration has blocked Chinese attempts to purchase U.S. companies and prevented thousands of Chinese students from studying at American universities. Students and investments both made China more dependent on and integrated with the United States.

But many Americans, drawing the wrong lessons from the brief period of Japanese ascendency in the late 1980s, saw these developments as a sign of American weakness, not strength. Japan’s investment in Rockefeller Center and Pebble Beach actually bound the country more closely to the United States and in the process demonstrated how attractive and singular U.S. properties and capital markets were (and are). Instead of recognizing this, policymakers simply carried their aversion to foreign investment over to China.

In the past year, both the Trump administration and Xi seem to have established what was termed “mutually assured disruption.” China can withhold rare-earth exports that are vital to tech companies everywhere, while Washington can use trade restrictions to squeeze the China export machine. The result has been a period of relative calm in U.S.-China tensions.

Now, with the Nvidia drama, that modus vivendi is in question on Capitol Hill. Congress sees the complicity of the White House as a political opening that it can exploit to double down on its preferred strategy of strangling Chinese technological growth.

But the calls for greater export controls and more scrutiny of trade will not make the United States economically or militarily more secure. They will instead push China ever deeper into its adversarial stance. In this new climate, any vestiges of bilateral engagement will be harder to sustain, aggravating the relationship that will determine the stability of the global system over the next decades.

There is still time to pursue a better policy. From the outset, Washington should have loosened controls on high-end chip exports, which could have helped ease Xi and the CCP’s iron grip on China and soothe bilateral tensions over time. The refrain that the United States can’t afford to let China win the race for artificial general intelligence is grounded in the same fallacy that any country could contain such a technology and that there is some magical first-mover advantage. There certainly wasn’t with any prior technology, nuclear weapons included.

A more sensible and open policy on chip exports remains the best path going forward. Congress can still pursue it, even as it seeks to hold Trump accountable for any corrupt acts—and Huang for any mistakes he may have made. Nvidia’s scandals do not prove that engagement was wrong and should not preclude the United States and China from developing a more cooperative relationship. Global peace should not be sacrificed on account of Huang’s actions, or Trump’s.

This post appeared in FP's The Reading List newsletter. Sign up here.

Zachary Karabell is the author of the Edgy Optimist Substack and an opinion contributor at the Washington Post.

#5916. Avoiding Colombia’s Escalation Trap

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

ELIZABETH DICKINSON is Deputy Director for Latin America at International Crisis Group.

How a U.S.-Backed Offensive Could Help Bogotá Reclaim Its Conflict Zones

On September 21, José Luis Pérez Villanueva, better known as “El Cholo”—the second-in-command and financial architect of the criminal group Autodefensas Conquistadores de la Sierra Nevada—was killed during a Colombian police operation on the rural outskirts of Santa Marta. Within hours, militants from that organization launched a dramatic campaign of terror. The group, known colloquially as Los Pachenca, demanded that businesses across the city shutter. Some 90 percent of shops did, as videos circulated of gunmen shooting up storefronts that stayed open.

For two decades, Los Pachenca has been the unquestioned criminal authority in the beachfront city and the adjacent Sierra Nevada mountain range, charging extortion payments from businesses and residents and controlling the flow of drugs out of the Port of Santa Marta, en route to Europe and beyond. But the level of control the group exhibited during the strike was particularly jarring, given that Santa Marta is the vacation home of many of the country’s elite and a common destination for international visitors. Colombia’s government responded by militarizing the city, sending in hundreds of soldiers and police officers, who rolled into town on the main highway with tanks and air support. Many shops still stayed closed until the morning of September 25, when the group announced that it was ending the strike. For locals, Los Pachenca’s reaction was a message about who remains in charge.

Calm has since returned to Santa Marta, but the incident may be indicative of risks to come. Colombia’s newly elected president, Abelardo de la Espriella, has promised a steep military escalation against illegal groups, which have grown precipitously in recent years. As he does so, armed and criminal organizations will undoubtedly respond. Often, their target will not be the state. Instead, they will likely direct their violence against the civilian populations, whom they have spent years coopting and coercing. That is because armed and criminal groups across the country have learned that their true leverage is their ability to govern local communities—and to intimidate and punish them into silence. Even when they do attack state forces, their use of weapons such as improvised explosives and makeshift drones means that bystanders often suffer. In one recent incident in the Pacific coast department of Valle del Cauca, four drone attacks launched by an armed group in a single day left five civilians injured.

These dangers are particularly acute as de la Espriella’s pledge of a zero-tolerance crackdown against armed and criminal groups has won enthusiastic support from Washington. Both countries want big and visible victories against these outfits. They triumphantly announced a new era of cooperation when the president took office in August, including a promise of upgraded military ties. (The United States has not yet publicly pledged new weapons, but it is already providing greater intelligence and logistical assistance on some operations.) After two years of tensions and threats between President Donald Trump and leftist former President Gustavo Petro, the political realignment means that Colombia is once again the United States’ closest defense ally in South America. This is likely to continue, despite major Democratic wins in the U.S. Congress, as the relationship’s focus on security has a long history of bipartisan support.

This week, during U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth’s visit to Bogotá, the de la Espriella administration signaled that it would seek congressional approval for joint military operations with the United States in Colombia. This cooperation will likely yield initial results. The Colombian government has said that it aims to ramp up attacks against armed and criminal groups and to go after leaders who enjoyed protection as counterparts in peace talks during the past administration.

Yet Colombia’s true challenge is not how to carry out attacks but what to do afterward. Surgical strikes, which see the military enter and exit quickly, leave civilians more vulnerable in the aftermath, as armed groups retaliate against alleged informants and tighten their grip in areas they control. Bogotá must ensure that every operation includes contingencies to anticipate criminal groups’ reactions and build on successful offensives by weakening their grasp on communities. The government will need to strengthen the police’s ability to patrol contested areas, a costly but necessary process that the United States and other partners should support.

The stakes are high. Bogotá has a chance to leverage its increased military pressure to open the door for the state, rewriting the story of who governs conflict areas. If the government misses this opportunity to consolidate state control, however, Colombia risks entering an escalatory spiral in which civilians pay a mounting price for a campaign intended to protect them.

Armed and criminal groups have spent the last decade expanding their reach across Colombia. According to the local think tank Pares, the number of municipalities with an armed group presence rose from 212 in 2018 to 518 last year—or about half of all municipalities. This deterioration reversed dramatic and hard-won improvements in security. After the historically violent decades of the 1990s and 2000s, military pressure and negotiations yielded a 2016 peace agreement demobilizing the former Revolutionary Armed Forces of Colombia (FARC), once the country’s dominant rebel group. Colombia’s countryside breathed a sigh of relief as the FARC pulled out of areas where it had been in control, which represented a quarter of the country’s land.

The FARC’s exit, however, did not pave the way for Colombia’s highly centralized and bureaucratic state to claim control of those vast geographies. Instead, old armed groups such as the National Liberation Army and newer ones such as the Gaitanista Army of Colombia (also known as the Gulf Clan) saw a chance to expand their access to trafficking routes, coca crops, illegal mines, and, ultimately, vulnerable rural populations. Kidnapping for extortion and mass displacement began to resurface, shattering optimism that progress would move in one direction. Over the last four years, membership in illegal groups has nearly doubled, to some 27,000 fighters today.

As a candidate, de la Espriella successfully harnessed public frustration by promising mano dura, or iron-fisted tactics, including aerial attacks and increased incarceration, facilitated by the construction of mega-prisons. He blamed his predecessor’s administration for accelerating insecurity. The first leftist to be elected president in modern Colombia, Petro had entered office in 2022 vowing to de-emphasize the military’s role and instead hold peace talks with various armed organizations. But not every group participated, and many of the ones that did sought to take advantage of cease-fires to cement their control over communities. Midway through his term, Petro pivoted back toward deploying the armed forces. But much of the harm was already done.

Colombia’s new president has been in office only a little more than 100 days, but the shift is marked. De la Espriella carried out three bombing raids in his first weeks. He has extradited dozens of individuals to the United States, including senior leaders, and the administration has killed or captured many others. The president is doggedly pushing to restart aerial fumigation of coca crops, which the state carried out in the early 2000s to try to reduce the number of hectares under cultivation; the practice has since been heavily restricted by court rulings over concerns about health and environmental consequences. Both in private and in public, military leaders laud the government’s new support for their operations.

The Trump administration has also welcomed these developments, which align with a regional trend favoring Washington’s approach on counternarcotics. For Colombia, the new era of security cooperation with the United States represents a return to the status quo ante of the last quarter century, which was briefly disrupted by the Petro administration. U.S.-Colombian military ties were forged through antidrug efforts and deepened in 1999 with Plan Colombia, a decadelong $10 billion aid package that funded, among other things, the expansion and professionalization of the security forces and fumigation. The Colombian police and military are deeply integrated with their American counterparts. They use U.S.- and NATO-compatible systems, travel by the hundreds to the United States each year for instruction, and receive American intelligence. In nearly every operation, at least one step (and often far more) in the logistical chain is either coordinated with Washington or relies on U.S. support. That relationship never fully stopped under Petro, but the two sides were notably wary of each other. Now bilateral trust appears to be restored.

Even before the elections, de la Espriella impressed U.S. officials by outlining his plans to reclaim security momentum for the state. The Colombian president is now acting on several explicit U.S. priorities, including extraditions and legislation to designate numerous groups as terrorist organizations. Meanwhile, Bogotá’s coca eradication targets are even more ambitious than Washington’s. At a personal level, relations are strong, too. Two of Colombia’s top military officials previously served as envoys to U.S. Southern Command.

Bogotá says that it intends to deliver results for Washington’s antidrug goals and has explicitly asked for additional support in the form of funding, aircraft, surveillance technologies, and maintenance. Although Colombia’s military remains among the best trained in the region, its logistical needs are critical. Only 13 of Colombia’s 53 Black Hawk helicopters are in working order, and defense spending has shrunk as a share of the national government’s budget, from 22.4 percent in 2003 to just 14.7 percent in 2025, with a growing proportion of those funds spent on salaries and pensions.

How much the United States will provide remains to be seen. In August, U.S. Southern Command’s General Francis Donovan discussed ways to shore up the deteriorating air fleet during a visit to Bogotá. The United States has also redeployed surveillance drones from Africa to Colombia and Ecuador, where they are already aiding intelligence collection. But Secretary of State Marco Rubio was reluctant to pledge specific aid while in Colombia in September, and most of this assistance is likely to be in kind or to stay in the hands of the U.S. military and contractors, with little entering the Colombian budget. Much of it appears to be focused on short-term offensive wins.

Colombia has returned to tactics such as aerial bombardment because they arguably worked against the FARC. Founded as a Marxist rebellion in the 1960s, the group behaved like a conventional army, dressed in uniforms, and sought out combat with the state. This meant that the Colombian military could bomb encampments and that capturing leaders left the organization at times rudderless. Slowly chipping away at the FARC’s hierarchy and control helped push the group to finally negotiate its disarmament, beginning in 2012.

But today, those same strategies are yielding diminishing returns. The current generation of armed and criminal groups has little interest in confronting the state. Instead, they behave like businesses, seeking to safeguard access to natural resources, supply chains, and trafficking routes. They live within the civilian population and rarely wear uniforms. Leaders are easily replaced and hierarchies are fluid. Critically, Colombia’s armed groups have learned that wars with the military are costly but that controlling local populations—through intimidation and violence, but also by offering economic incentives, jobs, and services—is comparatively cheap. The battlefield has evolved from a conventional conflict to a contest over who can govern territories and the people living there.

Santa Marta offers an example of how this new model of warfare works. On most days, an outsider in the city would never perceive the presence of Los Pachenca. The group’s members do not patrol or identify themselves. Yet residents know them well from the weekly or monthly extortion payments they are forced to make. Local companies and government officials essentially view the group as an inescapable reality. Pushing back carries a violent price, but paying a protection tax, taking a bribe, or facilitating illegal commerce keeps business running smoothly.

The criminal governance of rural areas is even more suffocating. Groups that emerged from splinters of the former FARC, known as dissidents, often force local residents to hold identity cards, issued so that militants have a full census of who lives in the area and can detect possible enemy fighters. In the Amazon region, these groups keep a land registry more complete than that of the state so that they know exactly whom and how much to extort.

With this intense surveillance over society, armed and criminal groups frequently deploy the civilian population as human shields against military offensives and police patrols. The most egregious example is the widespread use of forcibly recruited children as bodyguards for senior commanders. De la Espriella’s initial bombardments, which killed at least six children, were even temporarily halted by a court over concerns about minors. Senior officers admit that these types of casualties are inevitable around high-value targets, and that the groups know that the military is far less likely to proceed if it means killing children.

Armed and criminal groups will also try to use the military operations to their political advantage. When children are killed in bombardments, for example, FARC dissidents spin the local narrative into a story about how the state cares little for rural populations. The argument sticks because there is rarely a credible state presence that can serve and protect these same communities and earn their trust.

There are more immediate consequences, as well. In the aftermath of bombardments and high-level captures, armed and criminal groups often undertake what amounts to a purge of the population, killing or exiling anyone they believe to have collaborated with the state. And when the state takes out a leader, intra-group violence frequently ensues as former associates compete to take his place. Many residents shelter inside to avoid being caught up in the fighting, leaving children without school and parents without income. If new splinter groups emerge, the combat for control over neighborhoods can place even more civilians in the crossfire. This fallout widens the gap between immediate tactical results and long-term security gains. Taking out a senior leader wins applause, but conditions may well worsen on the ground.

De la Espriella’s administration has rightly identified winning territorial control, permanently dislodging criminal groups, and restoring state governance as its ultimate goals. The dilemma it now faces—exacerbated by U.S. support that focuses on attention-grabbing wins—is how to both achieve short-term results and ensure that these do not unleash new waves of violence, retaliation against civilians, and, in turn, criminal consolidation.

The challenge is twofold. First, the military needs to anticipate groups’ violent reactions and to safeguard the population. In the case of Santa Marta, for example, the government could have increased police presence in the city in the hours after El Cholo was killed. Before the next bombardment or high-level capture, the state should ensure that a ground team of soldiers or military police is ready to hold the ground, protect the community, and prevent the armed group from swiftly stepping back in to reclaim territory.

The second challenge—establishing lasting territorial control—is trickier. A permanent rural security presence might offer the best chance for Colombia’s government to change the direction of the conflict. The country’s military leadership controls only the tip of the spear, not the rest of the state. Time and again, governments “send only the military to the hardest areas,” a retired senior officer told Crisis Group. Moreover, soldiers are operating under heavy pressure for quick wins, which are measured in the number of combatants and especially high-value targets that are killed or captured. There is a real risk that by focusing largely on these statistics, the state will overlook more comprehensive policies necessary to achieve durable security.

Pressure to deliver fast results has in the past incentivized unacceptable practices. In the early 2000s, intense political demands for combat kills fueled elaborate schemes in which soldiers murdered thousands of civilians and counted them as guerrilla fatalities, a scandal known as “false positives.” These crimes deeply compromised public trust in the security forces, and the military has worked for years to build a culture and procedures that ensure they do not happen again. Today, the country’s senior military leadership is likely the first and best brake on any attempts to use excessive force. Commanders say that they are cognizant of the need to guard against rules being slowly stretched until they are broken. “We need a stronger security approach, but it also worries us when we hear there is pressure for ‘results,’” one officer said. But U.S. rhetoric and behavior, including its decision to sink what it claims are drug-smuggling boats on the high seas, compound these risks.

Success, then, may demand a strategy that is ultimately anchored in a much stronger and larger police force, fit for the job of safeguarding civilians rather than merely carrying out offensive strikes. Military operations can generate a window of opportunity by shuffling dynamics on the ground or removing certain dangerous players. Yet those reconfigurations will turn out in the state’s favor only if the resulting vacuum is eventually filled by reliable law enforcement. Take, for example, an operation that temporarily dislodges a group from a rural hamlet. If the military acts alone, it will likely enter and leave in a matter of days or even hours, allowing the armed group to quickly return. If, however, the offensive is followed up with permanent police patrols, access to the justice system, and social interventions, criminals will have a harder time regaining control.

This, of course, is easier said than done, given the government’s resource constraints. To create effective and permanent patrols, police departments operating in the countryside need more officers and equipment to maintain a presence in the most remote communities. Colombia has roughly 180,000 uniformed police officers, a figure that, per capita, puts it on the lower end of OECD countries, many of which are far more urbanized and therefore easier to patrol. In Colombia’s conflict-affected areas, outgunned officers must often stay inside their local stations for their own safety, limiting their ability to build trust and channels of communication with the population. Other state institutions, such as the justice system, say that they cannot establish a durable foothold in villages without guarantees of protection in the medium term—exactly what rural policing should strive to provide. The U.S. State Department’s Bureau of International Narcotics and Law Enforcement Affairs has funded the building and equipping of rural police stations in the past; it should expand this work. International partners with long-standing expertise in rural policing, such as Canada and France, could provide critical support, as well.

In cities, the challenge is different. In Santa Marta, for example, the state is present, but it is weak and vulnerable to the pressure and cooptation of the same groups that it must confront. The police need both more officers and better working conditions—including salaries, benefits, access to education, and social support for families—that could help them resist bribes. Regular vetting and counterintelligence checks can help ensure that police are fighting on the side of the state and not its adversaries.

Expanding the police will be an expensive, long-term undertaking. In the interim, Colombia could prioritize more personnel for critical areas and ensure that officers have a strong grounding in community policing, allowing them to understand the most pressing risks facing civilians. That means regular outreach to communities to build trust and make certain that officers are seen as having a stake in local well-being. The United States, meanwhile, must continue to help Bogotá strengthen its police and courts with direct financial and technical assistance.

Colombia, backed by renewed U.S. support, cannot afford to repeat past mistakes or ignore how the conflict has changed. Bogotá must therefore plan for and capitalize on military pressure to do something no government in Colombia’s history has done: govern well in conflict areas.

#5915. The Party Shaping Spain’s Election Isn’t Even on the Ballot

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: The Party Shaping Spain’s Election Isn’t Even on the Ballot

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

Catalonia’s independence movement is moving to the right, and Spanish Prime Minister Pedro Sánchez might pay the price in snap parliamentary elections on Nov. 29. Junts per Catalunya, the separatist party that supported Sánchez to form a minority government in 2023, turned against him last year to fend off a new far-right rival—ultimately forcing the early vote.

Sánchez called elections after failing to get emergency legislation aimed at tackling Spain’s housing crisis through parliament in October. The conservative People’s Party (PP), the far-right Vox party, and the center-right Junts all voted against the package.

Catalonia’s independence movement is moving to the right, and Spanish Prime Minister Pedro Sánchez might pay the price in snap parliamentary elections on Nov. 29. Junts per Catalunya, the separatist party that supported Sánchez to form a minority government in 2023, turned against him last year to fend off a new far-right rival—ultimately forcing the early vote.

Sánchez called elections after failing to get emergency legislation aimed at tackling Spain’s housing crisis through parliament in October. The conservative People’s Party (PP), the far-right Vox party, and the center-right Junts all voted against the package.

It was the latest example of Junts going against the minority government’s initiatives. When party head Carles Puigdemont announced that Junts would revoke its backing and move into the opposition last year, he blamed Sánchez’s Socialist Workers’ Party for broken promises. But the pro-independence party is also facing a growing threat at home in Catalonia.

That threat is Aliança Catalana—a far-right, separatist party led by Sílvia Orriols, the mayor of the small town of Ripoll. Orriols founded Aliança in 2020 after breaking with another separatist party; its platform combines a demand for Catalan independence with hard-line hostility toward immigration and Islam. She pushed for a tougher approach to immigration after the 2017 Barcelona attacks, carried out by a terror cell based in Ripoll.

Unlike Junts, Aliança has elected not to stand in the Nov. 29 national elections, refusing to take part based on its Catalonian separatism. “We are not thinking about influencing Madrid, and that’s part of the foundation of Aliança,” said the party’s press chief, Eduard Berraondo.

Polling currently points to a right-wing majority. The PP leads with 31.2 percent of the vote, with the Socialists at 29.3 percent, and Vox at 18 percent as of Nov. 24, according to newspaper Ara’s polling average. Together, the PP and Vox are projected to win around 190 seats, above the 176 needed for a majority in the 350-seat Congress of Deputies. Only the state-run Center for Sociological Studies pollster, which critics say favors the government, puts the Socialists ahead.

So, although Aliança isn’t on the ballot, its rapid rise is reshaping Spanish politics.

The party’s hard line on immigration and Islam has pushed Junts to the right, making the party ever less likely to partner with the Socialists. Aliança’s voters could help decide where Catalonia’s right-wing vote goes: back to Junts, across to Vox, or nowhere at all. Finally, the party’s growing popularity suggests a deeper shift among Catalan independence supporters, away from progressivism and toward a more exclusionary view of who counts as Catalan.

Aliança has risen rapidly in Catalonia. The far-right party won just two of 135 seats in the Catalan parliamentary elections in 2024, but the latest survey from the Catalan government’s polling agency, the Centre d’Estudis d’Opinió (CEO), projects that the party would capture between 23 and 25 seats if regional elections were held now. Junts, which holds 35 seats, would fall to between 18 and 20.

Catalonia’s next regional elections must be held by late June 2028. Almost 1 in 5 people who voted for Junts in 2024 now say they would back Aliança—but a majority of those wavering respondents still ranked Junts as their second choice.

Aliança’s message lands with voters anxious about rapid population growth, crime, and stretched public services. Around 18 percent of its supporters say they would vote for Vox on Nov. 29, according to the CEO. This matters in Madrid, where each seat that Junts loses weakens the leverage that once made it a kingmaker.

Junts is responding to Aliança’s rise by shifting to the right. The party has demanded that Madrid hand Catalonia control over immigration, backed bans on the burqa and niqab in public spaces, and pushed for tougher rules on repeat criminal offenders. The party has also proposed making immigrants wait 10 years after registering as residents before they can apply for subsidized housing, more than three times the current maximum.

Catalonia elects 48 members of Spain’s national parliament; Junts holds seven of those seats. In return for the party’s support after the 2023 election, the Socialists pushed through an amnesty law covering those involved in the 2017 Catalan independence push, including Puigdemont, who at the time served as Catalonia’s president and fled to Belgium to avoid arrest. The Supreme Court lifted his arrest warrant in October, clearing the way for his return.

Junts has ruled out backing either Sánchez or the PP’s party leader for prime minister, though it has left the door open to supporting a different Socialist candidate. Competing with Aliança for allegiance in Catalonia, it has little room to prop up a left-wing government. With a PP-Vox majority, that might not matter. But if the right-wing vote falls short, Spain’s government could once again hang on a handful of Catalan votes.

The CEO expects Junts to win just four or five parliamentary seats in the snap elections, while Vox could more than double its share of Catalonia’s seats—from two seats to five or six. Some of those votes might come from Aliança supporters.

“From Nov. 29, a dual voter will emerge: someone who votes Aliança in municipal and Catalan elections, and Vox in Spanish ones,” said Xavier Torrens, a professor of political science at the University of Barcelona.

Vox and Aliança are opposed when it comes to the question of Catalonia’s independence, but on immigration, they speak the same language.

“Many people vote Vox because they reject immigration, feel unsafe, or believe immigrants take a large share of public benefits. Aliança says the same,” said Xavier Rius, a Catalan journalist and the author of a book on Aliança.

Berraondo rejected a direct comparison between his party and Vox, insisting that their positions overlap only on immigration and law and order and that Aliança does not share Vox’s opposition to abortion and gay rights.

Still, Aliança might see a PP-Vox victory as good news for its future. “Imagine you think that if the PP and Vox win the elections in November, they could start the dirty job of changing the immigration rules,” Berraondo said. “Then you can take advantage of it if you are in government in Catalonia in 2028.”

This political convergence would once have been unthinkable. The Catalan independence movement defined itself against the Spanish nationalism that Vox represents and long prided itself on being progressive, inclusive, and pro-European. Center-right parties joined the independence push in the 2010s, but Aliança is the first far-right separatist party to gain traction in Catalonia.

So, how did a far-right party emerge from the movement, and why now? Part of the answer lies in 2017. That October, Catalonia’s pro-independence government held a referendum that Spain’s Constitutional Court had declared illegal. Puigdemont signed a declaration of independence and then days later announced that he asked parliament to suspended it. Weeks later, Madrid suspended Catalonia’s autonomy, and the movement’s leaders were jailed or fled abroad.

For many Junts supporters, the promise that independence was within reach proved hollow. Puigdemont’s about-face and rush to negotiate with Madrid seemed to reveal that the party prioritized its political leverage over independence—a perception later reinforced when Junts backed Sanchez’s government and demanded amnesty for its leaders rather than any move toward independence.

Orriols, the Aliança leader, has built her appeal on that sense of betrayal, casting the referendum moment as a deception and Puigdemont as a leader who lost his nerve. Voters who have switched allegiance from Junts to Aliança are, on average, around 56 years old, with nearly 89 percent supporting independence, according to the CEO. Their top concerns are immigration and insecurity. These are disappointed separatists—not newcomers to the cause.

“Part of Junts’s electorate realized that the whole narrative about independence was just fluff, that they weren’t serious about it,” said Massimo Maoret, a professor at IESE Business School in Barcelona and a longtime observer of Catalan politics. Aliança, he added, has tapped into that feeling “together with anti-immigrant, and especially anti-Islamic, sentiment.”

Further, as the cost of living rises, the concerns over affordable housing that brought down Sánchez’s government have also boosted the far-right party. Aliança “is winning an anti-system, anti-establishment vote, driven by people being fed up with the rising cost of living, the high cost of housing, and the poor functioning of public services such as rail transport,” Torrens said.

Aliança has drawn a connection between those issues and immigration, from pushing a debate over who counts as Catalan. For decades, the independence movement embraced a slogan popularized by Jordi Pujol, a former Catalan president: “Anyone who lives and works in Catalonia is Catalan.”

Aliança takes a different line. “Aliança considers that only some of Catalonia’s inhabitants are Catalan, while the rest are immigrants settled in Catalonia but not Catalan,” Torrens said. Orriols has said that Muslims could not be Catalan because “they don’t integrate with the country’s culture and values.”

Vox could unseat Pedro Sánchez in snap elections. How do you beat such an unusual party?

Why Spain’s Sánchez has succeeded while Britain’s Starmer floundered.

The question is the extent to which Aliança is shaping the independence movement. Junts has already shifted toward some of Aliança’s positions on immigration—but Jordi Turull, Junts’s secretary-general, has warned that the far right “will do nothing but take us further from our goal of a prosperous and complete Catalan republic.”

Josep Vila, the president of the Catalan National Assembly, a grassroots pro-independence organization, declined to say whether Aliança belonged to the movement. The group was “not concerned by the diversity of political options,” Vila wrote in an email, as long as they shared a strategy for independence.

Meanwhile, Aliança’s base is broadening beyond the independence movement. According to the CEO, only 53 percent of its supporters now back independence, down from 62 percent late last year. Taken together, the data suggest a shift in priorities. For disillusioned separatists and newcomers alike, concerns about immigration, security, and the cost of living increasingly outweigh the question of independence.

In some ways, Catalonia seems to be following a familiar European script of an emergent far right—this time from within a separatist movement. Torrens makes a distinction between national-populist parties that took decades to break through, such as France’s National Rally, and those that do so within a few years, such as Italian Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni’s Brothers of Italy, Portugal’s Chega, and Vox itself.

Aliança, he said, is on the fast track. The party’s first test comes in this week’s snap elections, when its supporters could shape the outcome. The bigger tests come later: municipal elections in May 2027 and the regional election by 2028. For now, Aliança seems to have shifted the Catalan independence movement’s center of gravity, as a movement that prided itself on building a Catalonia for all has absorbed an us-versus-them view of who belongs.

The Nov. 29 vote will offer the first signs of how far that shift reaches beyond Catalonia. If Junts loses seats in Madrid, and especially if the PP and Vox win a majority on their own, then Catalan separatists could lose their leverage over Spain’s government for the first time in years. The irony is that some of Aliança’s own supporters might help sweep the nationalists in.

Blake Evans-Pritchard is a journalist based in Spain. Previously he spent eight years in Hong Kong, covering politics and finance across Asia.
