#5914. Trump pardons turkeys but Hill influence isn’t all gravy after midterm roasting

President Donald Trump, first lady Melania Trump and Speaker Mike Johnson are all smiles at the annual Thanksgiving turkey pardon at the White House. (Finn Gomez/Getty Images)

President Donald Trump on Tuesday celebrated what he called one of the “immense powers” of his office, pardoning two turkeys ahead of the Thanksgiving holiday. But lawmakers say his influence on Capitol Hill has already changed since Republicans’ midterm losses.

Joined by first lady Melania Trump and Speaker Mike Johnson, R-La., set to lose power in January after he hands the gavel to a Democratic successor, Trump for the sixth time spared the long necks of two American turkeys — this time “Feathers” and “Freedom.”

“In a few moments, under the immense powers vested in me by the Constitution of the United States of America, I will grant a full, absolute and unconditional pardon — meaning they will live forever — to two magnificent Thanksgiving turkeys, special turkeys,” Trump said, suggesting he could extend his power to spare the birds from the holiday dining table to somehow grant them immortality.

While voters punished his party’s candidates for doing too little to address high prices and his decision to join Israel in going to war with Iran, Trump on Tuesday remained focused on another area of largely unchallenged authority — his White House projects. He began the event by touting the yearly event, for the first time, being held on the helipad he had built on the White House’s South Lawn. Later, he announced plans for another new location next year.

“I'm told that per the long-running tradition, these exquisite animals stayed overnight at the nearby Willard Hotel. Unfortunately, for 200 years, the White House lacked appropriate accommodation to host such distinguished guests,” he said of the turkeys. “I'm pleased to announce that next year we'll be able to have a special location right over there in that rather small ballroom being built. It's going to host a lot of people and a couple of turkeys next year. We're going to do it inside.”

Trump also highlighted that the two bright, white turkeys were raised in West Virginia — which he carried more than 40 points when he was on the ballot. The turkeys, now spared, will spend their remaining years in Texas, a state where Democrats scored two of their most high profile wins in the House and Senate races — something Trump left unmentioned.

The pre-holiday vibes were lighthearted on Tuesday, with the president slated to later travel to South Florida for a Thanksgiving weekend of turkey and time at one of his golf clubs. But when he returns, lawmakers will continue assessing how much sway he still has on Capitol Hill as he begins what, for his predecessors, were their lame-duck eras.

One Republican Senate ally acknowledged his influence with lawmakers already has changed.

“I think it's going to be policy-to-policy on that,” said Senate Republican Conference Vice Chair James Lankford.

“But he's the president of the United States all the way to the very end. So he still has his veto pen,” the Oklahoman added. “He still has the ability to be able to direct agencies to engage. We're passing legislation. It takes three to tango: House, Senate, and the White House.”

Senate Commerce Committee Chair Ted Cruz of Texas said Trump’s influence, once the Democrat-controlled House and Senate are seated on Jan. 3, “will, obviously, be different.”

“I don't think the incoming Democrat majorities are interested in passing productive legislation and passing pro-jobs legislation,” said Cruz, a potential 2028 GOP presidential candidate. “I think, instead, they're just going to attack the president all day long.”

Still, Cruz predicted “the president will continue to lead.”

Trump on Monday showed how he likely intends to use his lame-duck influence on and off Capitol Hill when he successfully pushed for the reinstatement of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang. During an interview with CNBC’s Eamon Javers, Trump made an admission that would be stunning from any other sitting American president about a private company, saying he had been “speaking to the board directly and indirectly.”

In a Monday statement, Nvidia said its board had “received input from senior levels of the United States government, and based on that input, the board is confident that Mr. Huang’s reinstatement poses no national security risk.”

Such unprecedented moves are a big reason why Sen. Rick Scott, R-Fla., last week said he expects Trump to be Trump — despite overseeing a midterms campaign he insisted be about himself and his unpopular second-term record.

“Any president is going to have a lot of influence because they get to run the executive branch,” said Scott, a former Sunshine State chief executive. “And they have the biggest pulpit in the world. So he's going to continue to have a lot of influence.”

Still, some Republican lawmakers have criticized Trump and his team for what they call recent stumbles.

Sen. Jim Banks of Indiana last week told reporters he had “a lot of questions” about how Trump and his administration handled upgrades to the country’s air traffic control system that prompted a nationwide ground stop on Nov. 13.

“Somebody needs to be held accountable for what happened,” he said, raising concerns about the president demanding $50 billion in additional taxpayer monies to build a new system. “I’m not fully convinced that throwing out more money is going to solve the fundamental problems.”

Trump also has taken ample friendly fire on social media from GOP members over his interference in the Huang scandal.

North Carolina GOP Rep. Pat Harrigan took to X to say the Nvidia founder’s reinstatement was “a mistake.”

“For the past two years, I have privately said there is no way Jensen Huang can argue for the exportation of advanced American chips to China without being compromised. Turns out, well, we all know,” Harrigan wrote after Trump contended an administration probe had “exonerated” Huang of any potential wrongdoing. “There may come a time when forgiveness is warranted, but there are still too many unanswered questions and too much unmitigated risk. Today’s coordinated action between the company and this administration is likely to whitewash this risk.”

Harrigan, a rare Tar Heel State Republican incumbent to win another term, echoed other GOP lawmakers in calling for an in-depth congressional probe in the Huang scandal and his affair with an alleged Chinese intelligence operative.

Next week could offer an early test of other Republicans’ willingness to break more publicly with Trump on certain issues, as Huang is scheduled to appear before multiple congressional committees.

Even before Trump threw his political weight behind Huang’s return, Democratic Sen. Tim Kaine of Virginia said he had sensed a GOP president who had lost clout with his party’s Senate conference.

“I think the Nvidia issue has caused some major concerns about the Trump China policy, obviously,” Kaine said. “That is definitely creating some anxiety.”

Trump has given clues that he is as concerned with his influence among the incoming Democratic majorities.

“If they want to get things done, I want to get things done, too,” Trump said during a Friday press conference at the White House. “You know, there are those that say we might get more done because we had some Republicans that were just impossible to deal with.”

#5913. A look at how the Trumps spend their Thanksgiving holiday through the years

Melania Trump delivers a powerful message of gratitude to military families during a visit to Fort Rucker ahead of Thanksgiving. She honors the profound sacrifices made by military spouses and children while their loved ones are deployed across the globe. She expresses deep appreciation for their strength and resilience in keeping their homes full of love.

In keeping with tradition, President Donald Trump marked his sixth Thanksgiving in the executive office with the White House’s annual Turkey Pardon ceremony on Tuesday.

His plans for Thanksgiving Day this year remain unannounced — but previous holidays offer a preview of how the president likes to spend the day honoring those who can't be at home for the uniquely American holiday due to service to the country.

Last year, Trump spent Thanksgiving at Mar-a-Lago participating in his favorite pastime — golf. But he also used the time in what is known as the "Winter White House" to call Maj. Gen. Dave Gardner, commander of the 101st Airborne Division and Joint Task Force–Southern Border, by video conference and talk with troops deployed along the southern border.

TRUMP AND DANA WHITE HONOR MARINE CORPS BAND WITH SIX-FIGURE CHECKS IN FIRST EVENT ON NEW HELIPAD

President Donald Trump delivers remarks to U.S. troops in an unannounced visit to Al Asad Air Base, Iraq, Dec. 26, 2018. (Reuters/Jonathan Ernst)

First lady Melania Trump spent the days ahead of the holiday in 2025 visiting Camp Lejeune and Marine Corps Air Station New River with second lady Usha Vance on Nov. 19. They met with military families and made Thanksgiving crafts with children.

In 2020, during the final Thanksgiving of his first term, Trump remained at the White House and made calls to six deployed military units representing every branch of the armed forces, including troops in Kuwait and sailors aboard the USS Winston Churchill in the Red Sea.

Trump famously took a secret trip to Bagram Airfield in Afghanistan on Nov. 28, 2019, where he served turkey, posed for photos and thanked American troops for their sacrifices amid the ongoing war in the Middle East, which not even two years later, in August 2021, was brought to an end by then-President Joe Biden.

"There's nowhere I'd rather celebrate this Thanksgiving than right here with the toughest, strongest, best, and bravest warriors on the face of the Earth," Trump told the troops at the time.

"And you don't even know how much the people of our country love and respect you."

President Donald Trump and first lady Melania Trump greet military personnel during an unannounced visit to Al Asad Air Base, Iraq, Dec. 26, 2018. (Reuters/Jonathan Ernst)

TRUMP SAYS US MAY RECOVER ALL THE EQUIPMENT LEFT BEHIND IN AFGHANISTAN BY BIDEN ADMIN

The visit came as roughly 12,000 American troops remained in Afghanistan more than 18 years after the U.S. invasion.

During the visit, Trump met with Afghan President Ashraf Ghani to discuss efforts to end the war and told American troops that the Taliban wanted to reach a deal with the United States. He said his administration was reducing America's military presence in Afghanistan while pursuing a political solution to the more than 18-year conflict.

President Donald Trump visits with personnel at U.S. Coast Guard Station Lake Worth Inlet in Riviera Beach, Florida, on Thanksgiving Day, Nov. 22, 2018. (Mandel Ngan/AFP via Getty Images)

Trump spent Thanksgiving 2018 in Florida having a traditional dinner with his family, including Melania and his children and grandchildren.

He also held a video call with deployed troops before visiting Coast Guard members at Station Lake Worth Inlet with his wife, where the first couple thanked them for their service and hurricane rescues.

After that holiday in 2018, Melania and then-second lady Karen Pence assembled comfort kits for deployed troops at American Red Cross headquarters.

MOSCOW, TALIBAN FORGE MILITARY ALLIANCE IN POWER GRAB AFTER US AFGHANISTAN EXIT: REPORTS

President Donald Trump speaks to members of the military via teleconference from his Mar-a-Lago resort in Palm Beach, Florida, on Thanksgiving Day, Nov. 22, 2018. (Mandel Ngan/AFP via Getty Images)

Trump spent his first Thanksgiving as president at his Mar-a-Lago estate for a traditional Thanksgiving dinner with his family. It came after a day of video-calling deployed troops.

Donald and Melania Trump also visited Coast Guard Station Lake Worth Inlet in Riviera Beach, Florida, where they handed out food and greeted service members.

Ashley J. DiMella reports on politics for Fox News Digital.

#5912. Trump Is Desperate For a Ukraine Deal, but the Window Is Closing

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: Trump Is Desperate For a Ukraine Deal, but the Window Is Closing

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

The clock is ticking. Sources in the White House say U.S. President Donald Trump wants a Ukraine peace deal by early January—before the Democrats, riding high after their stunning midterm election win this month, come sweeping in to take control of Congress. “The Americans are pushing hard and there’s some concern that it would need a great deal of Ukrainian concessions to get anywhere near a deal,” one European official told the Financial Times. “The Americans are in a hurry, Putin is in no hurry,” another said.

But wait—what’s the rush? Why exactly does Trump have to get a deal before the new Congress convenes? Surely he can find plenty of allies in the House and Senate who would be happy to support a just and equitable peace in Ukraine. After all, his goals are purely humanitarian; as he keeps telling us, he just wants to stop the killing.

The clock is ticking. Sources in the White House say U.S. President Donald Trump wants a Ukraine peace deal by early January—before the Democrats, riding high after their stunning midterm election win this month, come sweeping in to take control of Congress. “The Americans are pushing hard and there’s some concern that it would need a great deal of Ukrainian concessions to get anywhere near a deal,” one European official told the Financial Times. “The Americans are in a hurry, Putin is in no hurry,” another said.

But wait—what’s the rush? Why exactly does Trump have to get a deal before the new Congress convenes? Surely he can find plenty of allies in the House and Senate who would be happy to support a just and equitable peace in Ukraine. After all, his goals are purely humanitarian; as he keeps telling us, he just wants to stop the killing.

If saving lives were really his priority, there are plenty of things that Trump could have done by now to achieve that end. He could have increased humanitarian and financial aid to the Ukrainian government. Along with other weapons to bolster Kyiv’s defenses, he could have sent Patriot missiles to protect Ukrainian civilians from ballistic missile attacks, which Russia has been steadily ramping up in recent months. And, even more importantly, he could have pressured Russian President Vladimir Putin to end the war, rather than gutting sanctions passed by Congress and allowing Moscow to sell diesel fuel on global markets (which hasn’t helped U.S. consumers but has given Russia more funding for its war).

“The United States government is actually trying to get a very good deal for Russia,” as military historian Phillips O’Brien recently told a Ukrainian newspaper. “That’s its starting point.” He dismisses Trump’s professed humanitarian concern, arguing that Trump’s ideological affinity for Putin-style authoritarianism and persistent contempt for Kyiv are driving him to accommodate Russia’s interests while treating the Ukrainians as an afterthought.

The urgency with which Trump is pursuing this agenda also has a tangible financial component. He thinks that Russia is a potential gold mine, and he wants to wet his beak. The Kremlin understands this perfectly well, and the Russians have been working hard to service his expectations. Indeed, the list of potential business deals Moscow is offering Team Trump seems to be growing by the day. Putin has tasked Kirill Dmitriev, the head of Russia’s sovereign wealth fund, to pitch tempting proposals to Trump’s negotiating team, led by son-in-law Jared Kushner and real estate tycoon Steve Witkoff. The deals on offer include oil and gas investments and sales of aluminum and critical minerals; many of them would require the lifting of current Western sanctions before funding can flow, a huge incentive for Putin. Dmitriev has claimed that the total value of the projects under discussion amounts to $14 trillion—wildly fanciful but just what Trump wants to hear.

Trump and his cronies stand to benefit personally from these deals. A Texas financier named Gentry Beach, who has close ties to the Trump family, has already signed an agreement with Russian natural gas company Novatek. Dmitriev has suggested that Elon Musk’s Boring Co. could take part in a project for a tunnel underneath the Bering Strait; it’s a nonsensical notion but demonstrates just how deftly the Kremlin knows how to cater to the Trump World mindset. Trump, of course, wants a cut of his own. In October, the New York Times reported that one of the multibillion-dollar oil deals under discussion with Russia would benefit Middle Eastern investors with links to Kushner and Witkoff. And these are only the projects that we know about.

Hence the need for speed. Trump is clearly hoping that a peace deal will continue to yield dividends to himself and his friends long after he leaves office. Said differently, the White House is holding a fire sale of U.S. foreign policy to the highest and most likely bidder, which in Trump’s eyes is Putin. In that sense, Trump’s Ukraine policy is merely an extension of his broader program of replacing the U.S. national interest with his own—what the Atlantic’s Anne Applebaum has aptly called an “empire of grift.”

A post on Trump’s Truth Social account on Nov. 19.Donald Trump/Truth Social

But first, he has to get a deal signed, and that is looking less likely by the day. The president’s much-ballyhooed trilateral summit in Shenzhen, which was supposed to make Trump look like a global titan, ended without any sort of palpable progress on the Ukrainian front. The terse final communique didn’t mention either Iran or Ukraine (although it did offer up a vague swipe at the International Criminal Court). Neither Chinese President Xi Jinping nor his vassal Putin seems to have regarded the war in Ukraine as worth mentioning.

That has left Trump, the man who repeatedly claimed that he’d stop the war within 24 hours of taking office, sounding increasingly desperate. On his way back from the summit in China, he assured European leaders that the Kremlin was keen on peace: “Putin wants to end the war. He wants the deal done. He is getting tired of this.” Shortly thereafter, Putin demonstrated his peace-loving credentials by raining missiles down on Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky’s hometown, killing a 7-year-old boy and a 20-year-old paramedic and wounding 52.

As that example shows, Putin has yet to show that he’s actually interested in peace on any but maximalist terms. The Russian leader thinks he’s winning and that all he has to do is stay the course. In October, the Russians launched a record 153 ballistic missiles and 4,000 jet-powered attack drones into Ukraine. Rather than softening his negotiating position, Putin—encouraged by Trump’s permissiveness—has actually expanded his list of conditions for peace to include completely revising the current European security framework, including a rollback of NATO to its 1997 state. When Kushner recently claimed that Russia wouldn’t oppose Western security guarantees for Kyiv, a Putin aide immediately slapped him down.

It’s not just a failure of tactics. It’s an entirely mistaken worldview.

Three diminished leaders produced symbolism without substance, but their ideological alignment is historic.

Whether Putin actually knows how bad things are or bases his intransigence on rosy assessments from advisors is hard to say. But the Ukrainians aren’t letting up. Keen to demonstrate that the great powers can’t afford to exclude it, Kyiv prefaced the Shenzhen summit with a stunningly effective attack on the Kerch Bridge, a crucial lifeline connecting Russian-occupied Crimea with the Russian mainland. That was a carefully calculated middle finger aimed at Putin, who has turned the bridge into a symbol of Crimea’s allegedly indelible link with Russia.

There was a message for the Americans folded into that attack as well. In recent months, the Ukrainians have been experiencing slowdowns in their access to Starlink and Starshield, the Musk-owned satellite networks that Kyiv has used throughout the war to target Russian positions. Neither Musk nor the White House has been willing to admit responsibility for the lags, but many in Kyiv see the restrictions as a form of tacit U.S. pressure to comply with any deal the White House deems acceptable. (There are also rumors that Russia has threatened to attack the satellites.) So the Ukrainians decided to send a signal of their own—by attacking the bridge, one of Moscow’s most heavily protected assets, without using Starlink at all. The military planners in Kyiv came up with an entirely indigenous solution to the problem.

This isn’t the only way that the Ukrainians have been showing that they still have cards to play (to use the phrase wielded by Trump in his notorious Oval Office bullying of Zelensky). Ukraine has effectively stymied Russian efforts to capture territory this year; Russia actually lost ground in September and October, not least thanks to daring Ukrainian offensive operations incorporating remotely controlled ground vehicles. Ukraine’s General Staff claims that the Russians incurred 54,400 casualties (killed, wounded, or missing) in October alone, which would make it the bloodiest month of the war. When Trump urged the Ukrainians to cease their attacks on Russian oil refineries, they simply ignored him. Those strikes are one of their most effective ways of inflicting pain on the enemy, and they’re in no mood to capitulate.

Trump’s efforts to engineer peace are also running up against the stubbornness of the Europeans, who for all their dysfunction aren’t ready to give up. Given all these adverse conditions, it’s hard to imagine how the president and his feckless negotiating team will be able to get their way before the new crop of Democrats moves into Congress. But you never know. As a great American philosopher once said: “It ain’t over till it’s over.”

#5911. Can Shenzhen Summit Reduce Nuclear Risk?

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: Can Shenzhen Summit Reduce Nuclear Risk?

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

The recent summit between U.S. President Donald Trump, Russian President Vladimir Putin, and Chinese President Xi Jinping promised to “conduct a dialogue on reducing nuclear risks and preventing the proliferation of nuclear weapons.” But what that means is unclear, especially when arms control itself has become a politically fraught term across multiple spheres.

The expiration of New START, a 2011 treaty that built on past arms control efforts, in February left the United States and Russia without legally binding limits on their deployed strategic nuclear forces. Some North Korea watchers worry that arms control negotiations would mean abandoning denuclearization as a policy objective, even though interim measures could contribute to the longer-term goal. Meanwhile, China has long resisted trilateral arms control talks, citing the disparity between its arsenal and those of the United States and Russia.

The recent summit between U.S. President Donald Trump, Russian President Vladimir Putin, and Chinese President Xi Jinping promised to “conduct a dialogue on reducing nuclear risks and preventing the proliferation of nuclear weapons.” But what that means is unclear, especially when arms control itself has become a politically fraught term across multiple spheres.

The expiration of New START, a 2011 treaty that built on past arms control efforts, in February left the United States and Russia without legally binding limits on their deployed strategic nuclear forces. Some North Korea watchers worry that arms control negotiations would mean abandoning denuclearization as a policy objective, even though interim measures could contribute to the longer-term goal. Meanwhile, China has long resisted trilateral arms control talks, citing the disparity between its arsenal and those of the United States and Russia.

The Shenzen trilateral declaration nevertheless represents a modest but potentially meaningful diplomatic opening. China’s willingness to discuss nuclear dangers is noteworthy given its resistance to negotiations on nuclear arms limitations. However, the declaration provides no timetable, no delegation, and no agreement on specific measures. It remains an open question whether the three countries can translate that commitment into practical cooperation.

Arms control does not have to begin with a treaty. Arms control and nuclear risk reduction are distinct but overlapping approaches to managing nuclear competition, reducing the dangers of miscalculation, and building confidence among adversaries. Measures such as missile launch notifications and crisis communications can be pursued independently of a formal arms control treaty.

A trilateral arms control treaty remains a distant prospect because of China’s reluctance and Moscow’s demands for changes in broader U.S. policy regarding military support for Ukraine and NATO’s role in European security, and its insistence that multilateral arms control negotiations include the nuclear forces of the United Kingdom and France. But Washington, Beijing, and Moscow do not need to wait for a treaty before taking steps to reduce nuclear risks.

Two days before the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit, I suggested at the Track 1.5 Stockholm China Forum that a positive outcome of a Trump-Xi-Putin meeting would be if they reaffirmed that a nuclear war cannot be won and must never be fought (also known as the 1985 Reagan-Gorbachev principle) and that they should begin talks on nuclear risk reduction. That was the language the leaders agreed to in Shenzhen.

However, neither the principle nor the idea of risk reduction talks is new. In 2022, the P5—the five permanent members of the United Nations Security Council (China, France, Russia, the United Kingdom, and the United States)—had reaffirmed the same principle. Reaffirming it again was easier than agreeing on concrete measures that reduce nuclear dangers.

At least three obstacles could undermine the Shenzhen declaration: geopolitical crises, disagreements over the sources of nuclear risk and their appropriate measures, and incompatible conditions in other areas for progress on risk reduction. Take geopolitical tensions first. Beijing could suspend talks with Washington because of a confrontation over Taiwan or tensions from U.S. arms sales or military activities. A prolonged war in Ukraine and disagreements over European security could also hinder sustained dialogue and cooperation. The trilateral format creates an additional vulnerability: a crisis between any two participants could jeopardize the dialogue even if the third party wants to continue it.

The three countries may also disagree on what constitutes nuclear risk reduction and how best to achieve it. Beijing has tended to emphasize U.S. policies and military capabilities that it considers to be key sources of nuclear risk while Washington has placed greater emphasis on operational measures like establishing crisis hotlines to prevent misunderstanding, miscalculation, and unintended escalation.

Therefore, China might favor a discussion on strategic intentions, nuclear doctrines, or mutual no-first-use commitments while Washington would likely prioritize concrete measures such as missile launch notifications, reliable crisis communications, and greater transparency. These approaches are not mutually exclusive, but they reflect different assessments of what makes nuclear conflict more likely and how to manage those risks.

Some risk reduction measures require greater transparency, which China has historically rejected. Meaningful first steps in a risk reduction dialogue would be to provide advance notice of each other’s ballistic missile launches and create a crisis communications channel. However, Washington and Beijing currently do not practice advance notifications in the way that Washington and Moscow or Beijing and Moscow do.

China’s submarine-launched ballistic missile test in July illustrates the problem. While Beijing said that it notified relevant countries in advance, the notification fell short of the 24-hour standard urged by the United States and other countries.

Washington has already proposed this year that the P5 work on ballistic missile launch notifications and establish a P5 secure communications network, but it had “not yet gained consensus.” Therefore, the Shenzhen declaration raises the question of whether the three largest nuclear powers can make progress on proposals that have encountered resistance in the broader P5 process.

As for crisis communications, the challenge is not simply establishing a direct link between Washington and Beijing. Some channels already exist, but their effectiveness depends on whether urgent messages reach the appropriate decision-makers and receive timely responses. Existing models include the Washington-Moscow Direct Communications Link, which was established in 1963 at the leadership level after the Cuban missile crisis, and the Nuclear Risk Reduction Centers, which were established in 1987 to facilitate routine notifications and messages under arms control and risk reduction arrangements.

A similar arrangement simply does not exist between the United States and China. The limitations of existing channels became apparent in February 2023, when Beijing refused to answer a call from U.S. Defense Secretary Lloyd Austin, who was using a defense phone line established in 2008, to explain the U.S. shootdown of a Chinese surveillance balloon and ease tensions. The establishment of an institutionalized channel would require agreed procedures, designated authorities, and a commitment to maintain reliable communications even during periods of heightened tension.

Progress could also stall if governments make nuclear risk reduction conditional on concessions in other disputes. Beijing could seek changes in U.S. policy toward Taiwan or missile defense, while Moscow could press Washington on broader bilateral relations or on French and British nuclear forces. Such demands could hold nuclear risk reduction hostage to broader geopolitical disagreements.

Despite these obstacles, all three countries have an interest in reducing nuclear dangers. An initial trilateral dialogue could accommodate their competing priorities without requiring the three nuclear powers to agree on the exact causes of strategic instability.

They could begin by exchanging views on strategic intentions, nuclear doctrines, and behaviors that each considers destabilizing, while simultaneously exploring practical measures including missile launch notifications and crisis communications. They could also examine how the integration of artificial intelligence into military and nuclear-related systems might introduce new risks and what safeguards could help mitigate them. The immediate goal would be to identify risks that all three governments have an interest in reducing, even if they disagree on their origins.

The implications of nuclear risk reduction and prospects for eventual arms control extend beyond the United States, China, and Russia. U.S. allies are watching how Washington manages nuclear competition with Beijing and Moscow, how it responds to North Korea’s advancing nuclear capabilities, and how it maintains the credibility of its security commitments. The Shenzhen declaration’s silence on North Korea’s nuclear program raises questions about how the three nuclear powers intend to reconcile their commitment in preventing nuclear proliferation with the security concerns of the United States’ Asian allies.

The United States will need to negotiate measures with its two nuclear competitors in ways that reduce the risk of great-power conflict without weakening regional deterrence or creating new incentives for nuclear proliferation. But that requires more than a trilateral treaty. Washington will need to consider how its allies assess the implications of any future trilateral agreement for their own security, rather than treating their concerns as secondary to great-power negotiations.

Duyeon Kim is a research fellow at the Asan Institute for Policy Studies and a long-time foreign-policy and national security expert who has held senior positions at leading Washington, D.C. think tanks.

#5910. Nepal’s New Leader Reaffirms Ties With India

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

South Asia Brief: Nepal’s New Leader Reaffirms Ties With India

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Welcome to Foreign Policy’s South Asia Brief.

The highlights this week: Nepali Prime Minister Balendra Shah makes a notable visit to India, former Prime Minister Sheikh Hasina announces a return date to Bangladesh, and senior Pakistani opposition leaders are detained.

Welcome to Foreign Policy’s South Asia Brief.

The highlights this week: Nepali Prime Minister Balendra Shah makes a notable visit to India, former Prime Minister Sheikh Hasina announces a return date to Bangladesh, and senior Pakistani opposition leaders are detained.

Nepali Prime Minister Balendra Shah’s two-day visit to New Delhi concluded on Monday. Standing alongside Shah, Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi announced a $1.6 billion assistance package for Kathmandu, most of it earmarked for reconstruction after August’s catastrophic floods.

The two sides also pledged cooperation on satellite communications and urban development, and India announced that it will open a new Indian Institute of Technology campus in Nepal. Shah affirmed the strength of the Nepal-India partnership, saying that he hoped it would “be one where trust stands as tall as the Himalayas.”

Shah, 36, is a highly unconventional leader, known for his love of rapping, dark sunglasses, and video games. After taking office in March, he took a defiant approach to diplomacy, refusing to meet with visiting foreign dignitaries unless they were of similar rank. His unwillingness to meet Indian Foreign Secretary Vikram Misri prompted New Delhi to call off Misri’s scheduled visit in May.

That Shah made India his first trip abroad as prime minister speaks to the importance of the relationship. It is also a reminder of India’s regional influence at a moment when some commentators argue that India is on the back foot relative to China.

China has grown increasingly present in Nepal in recent years. In late 2024, the previous Nepali government, led by K.P. Sharma Oli, inked a framework deal with Beijing on the Belt and Road Initiative (BRI). Progress has been slow, and Nepal remains one of the only South Asian countries today with no BRI projects on its soil.

However, Nepal, like other smaller South Asian states, relies heavily on Indian development and humanitarian assistance. This gives New Delhi leverage, even as most regional capitals scale up ties with China.

The Maldives offers an instructive parallel. One of President Mohamed Muizzu’s first foreign-policy moves after taking office in November 2023 was to expel India’s relatively small military presence. Shortly thereafter, he signed new defense deals with China and signaled a desire to deepen ties further.

Yet months later, he visited New Delhi, where Modi announced a new financial assistance package at a critical moment of economic distress. In 2024 and 2025, Muizzu repeatedly called India his country’s closest ally.

India is not taking its neighbors for granted, and Shah’s visit showed it. The assistance package was generous, among the largest provided to Nepal since the floods. Modi’s announcement that India will create new innovation hubs and start-up incubators in Nepal will help boost the country’s shaky economy—but it is also likely meant to preempt Beijing from doing the same.

None of this means the relationship is in great shape. India and Nepal face several entrenched challenges entirely unrelated to China, including territorial disputes and public perception problems. Many young Nepalis see India as a bully, a view reinforced by India’s unofficial blockade of Nepal more than a decade ago.

Shah’s visit nevertheless shows that the two capitals can get substantive things done despite their disagreements, and both walked away with wins. For India, it’s a useful pushback against China. (Shah could, after all, have gone to Beijing first.) For Nepal, it secured badly needed reconstruction aid and pledges of additional economic support, which are hard to come by in a constrained global donor environment.

Hasina announces return date. Former Bangladeshi Prime Minister Sheikh Hasina, who has resided in India since being ousted by mass protests in 2024, says she will return home on Dec. 16, her spokesperson told Reuters on Tuesday. The date is symbolic, marking the anniversary of Bangladesh’s 1971 victory over Pakistan.

Getting back will not be simple. Hasina was stripped of her passport after her ouster, and Bangladeshi officials have refused to issue a new one. A Bangladeshi court also sentenced her last year to death in absentia for her role in the violent crackdown on protests. Still, Hasina says she wants to return to challenge the legitimacy of the charges and rally her party, which was banned last year.

Dhaka repeated its standard response: Hasina is a fugitive and cannot return unless India extradites her—something New Delhi, which was close to Hasina during her time in power, is unlikely to do.

Hasina, through her spokesperson, also asked the government to arrange her trip back on Bangladesh’s national carrier, Biman Bangladesh Airlines. Bangladesh’s state minister for foreign affairs called the request “delusional.”

If Hasina does return, she will almost certainly be arrested. That would inflame both supporters and opponents, likely sparking violent protests. But if she chooses not to, or tries but is blocked, her base could react violently as well. With major economic, energy, and public health challenges already on its plate, the government can’t afford fresh instability.

Pakistani minister visits India. Pakistan’s climate change minister, Musadik Malik, also traveled to New Delhi this past week, representing Pakistan at an arts and culture forum organized by the Shanghai Cooperation Organisation (SCO). It was the first visit by a Pakistani minister since 2023.

On one level, the visit went as expected. Malik held no bilateral meetings with Indian counterparts, and both governments emphasized that he came in his SCO capacity. With India-Pakistan ties ice cold since last year’s brief but intense conflict, any official bilateral engagement would have been stunning.

What stood out was Malik’s performance. He won admirers in Pakistan, and some in India, with a speech (delivered in Urdu) that emphasized the countries’ shared heritage and praised India’s rich cultural history. He also responded calmly to Indian journalists who peppered him aggressively with questions about Pakistan-sponsored terrorism.

Malik’s trip won’t thaw a frozen bilateral relationship, but it underscored the potential for people-to-people engagement.

Sri Lankan oil spill. Authorities in Sri Lanka moved quickly to respond to an oil leak at Colombo’s main port on Nov. 21, which the Marine Environment Protection Authority said occurred when a pipeline connected to a fuel storage tank ruptured unexpectedly.

The spill polluted nearly two miles of coastline, including the city’s main drainage canal, a fisheries harbor, and an area where local fishers dock their boats. Fortunately, the port authority stopped most of the leak within an hour. It is unclear how much oil escaped, but the tank reportedly held about 1,000 metric tons of marine gas oil.

This is the second such incident at the port in under a year. In December 2025, a pipeline sprang a leak while crude oil was being unloaded from a nearby tanker.

For more than a week, one of Pakistan’s most prominent opposition leaders has been detained, with no details released about his condition or location. All that is known is that on Nov. 17, Taimur Jhagra and two other senior leaders of the Pakistan Tehreek-e-Insaf (PTI) party were taken into custody in Peshawar.

The three were already facing legal trouble: The government links them to attacks on a state radio facility in May 2023, though their lawyers say there is no evidence tying them to violence.

Jhagra served as finance and health minister in Khyber Pakhtunkhwa province when Imran Khan was prime minister. He oversaw a series of notable health reforms, including the introduction of a universal health insurance program. Many Pakistanis—and not just PTI supporters—view him as thoughtful, clean, and committed to public service.

His arrest, the information blackout that followed, and the lack of a widespread public outcry illustrate the extent of state repression in Pakistan today.

Human rights activist Glenfrey De Mel, writing in the Daily Mirror, praises Sri Lanka’s new anti-terrorism legislation: The proposed Anti-Terrorism Act “represents a critical juncture in the nation’s pursuit of a framework that effectively addresses extremist threats while upholding the fundamental pillars of human rights and the rule of law.”

Student Taiba Akhter Mim warns in the Daily Star of the dangers of Bangladeshi children spending too much time on their devices: “Short, supervised viewing of age-appropriate material is unlikely to ruin a child. What should concern us is endless autoplay. … Before pressing play, we should ask what the video gives a child besides a reason to keep watching.”

Writer Akbar N. Shad explains in the Express Tribune why Pakistani society holds back from protests: “Pakistan’s political and institutional landscape has, at times, made dissent a difficult undertaking. Concerns about pressure, restrictions and the personal cost of challenging authority can discourage ordinary citizens from raising their voices.”

Michael Kugelman is the writer of Foreign Policy’s weekly South Asia Brief and a senior fellow for South Asia at the Atlantic Council. An analyst of the region for nearly two decades, he previously directed the South Asia Institute at the Wilson Center. X: @michaelkugelman

#5909. The Paradox of American Credibility

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

JOSHUA A. SCHWARTZ is Assistant Professor of International Relations and Emerging Technology at the Carnegie Mellon Institute for Strategy & Technology.

How Fighting Weakens Washington’s Reputation—and Backing Down May Strengthen It

In mid-February, after the United States failed to get Iran to renounce its nuclear ambitions, U.S. President Donald Trump issued an ultimatum. The Iranian leadership had to make “a meaningful deal” with Washington. “Otherwise,” he said, “bad things happen.” Barely eight days later, the United States and Israel began the war against Iran that continues today.

That war has been criticized on many grounds, but analysts have often overlooked the basic assumption about U.S. credibility that it has tested. There is a long-held understanding that the United States’ willingness to use force to stand up for its interests is essential to deterring future adversaries—and convincing them to give in to the kind of sweeping demands the Trump administration made of Tehran. As Elbridge Colby, U.S. undersecretary of defense for policy, said in the early stages of the conflict, “I think both our friends and potential foes are seeing the prowess of the joint force and that also contributes to deterrence.”

Going back to the early years of the Cold War, the concept of credibility—a state’s perceived capabilities and its willingness to use them even at high cost—has played a central role in the decisions that led to and sustained many of the United States’ largest military campaigns. The need to bolster U.S. credibility was a key force driving Washington’s strategy in Korea and Vietnam in the mid-twentieth century, and it played an important role in Iraq and Afghanistan during the so-called war on terror. American strategists today continue to assess foreign policy decisions based on how they might strengthen or weaken the country’s reputation.

But as the results of the Iran campaign have shown, a decision to use force does not always strengthen deterrence. After nearly ten months of war, and the expenditure of billions of dollars of munitions, the United States appears little closer to achieving its objectives of definitively ending Iran’s nuclear threat and ability to project power across the region, let alone securing regime change in Tehran. Meanwhile, the war has badly depleted U.S. military resources that could be used elsewhere and has further eroded the American public’s willingness to support military interventions. Rather than sending a strong signal of U.S. resolve, the Iran war risks conveying the opposite message. Vice President JD Vance argued that the reason the Republicans lost the midterm elections was the Iran war, and government officials in Tehran have gloated that “Americans said no to war.”

In fact, the conventional wisdom that standing firm in a military conflict enhances a state’s credibility misses something important. As the U.S. wars in Vietnam and Iraq showed, a prolonged military campaign may leave the country more cautious about using force, and less willing to bear the costs, in the years that follow. Adversaries notice this. Given that prior efforts by the Trump administration to compromise with Tehran were criticized as “appeasement” by figures like former U.S. Vice President Mike Pence, Trump may be tempted to continue or even expand the Iran campaign. But by taxing U.S. capabilities and deepening war-weariness among the American public, escalation in Iran could instead lead Beijing and Moscow to question Washington’s appetite for future interventions.

Policymakers should be far more skeptical about using military force for the sake of demonstrating U.S. resolve, and they should be less averse to backing down. The Cold War competition with the Soviet Union convinced the United States to intervene in costly proxy wars that consumed enormous human and military resources for questionable gain, and now the strategic competition with China and Russia may do the same. These kinds of conflicts are precisely the ones that are likely to harm, rather than help, U.S. credibility. Instead of obsessing about its reputation for resolve, which it cannot control, Washington must refocus its attention on maintaining the military strength and capabilities it needs to deter its major adversaries. Sometimes the best way to preserve both the capability and the willingness to act in the future is to walk away from conflict in the present.

For decades, policymakers and scholars have treated a country’s credibility as the dark matter of international politics—a hard-to-measure attribute that is nonetheless enormously important for maintaining deterrence. In the 1960s, nuclear deterrence theorist and Nobel Prize winner Thomas Schelling went so far as to say that acquiring a reputation for resolve is “one of the few things worth fighting over.” In keeping with this, it has long been assumed that whereas standing firm communicates to adversaries and allies alike that a country is willing to run risks and bear costs to uphold its interests, backing down or seeking to compromise conveys the opposite. As a result, many U.S. leaders and strategists have drawn on the logic of credibility to justify the most hawkish policies toward adversaries: making maximalist demands, using force, and remaining in wars long past the point where their costs exceeded their benefits.

Vietnam was a case in point. When debating whether to send the first group of U.S. troops to Vietnam in 1965, U.S. Secretary of State Dean Rusk told President Lyndon Johnson, “If the Communist world finds out we will not pursue our commitments to the end, I don’t know where they will stay their hand.” Later, as the United States became bogged down in the war, Assistant Secretary of Defense John McNaughton went further, arguing in a formerly top-secret memo that the primary reason for continuing the Vietnam War was to maintain America’s “reputation as a guarantor” and “avoid a humiliating U.S. defeat.” According to this logic, 58,000 U.S. soldiers and likely over a million Vietnamese people were killed not because the outcome of the war was itself crucial to U.S. interests, but because Washington worried how others—especially the Soviets—would perceive it if it withdrew.

Since the Cold War, the same justifications have been used to push the country into, and to sustain, many other conflicts. This was especially true during the so-called Forever Wars the United States was drawn into after the 9/11 attacks. On the night of 9/11, U.S. Secretary of Defense Donald Rumsfeld said, “We need to bomb something else [other than the relatively weak Taliban] to prove that we’re, you know, big and strong and not going to be pushed around by these kinds of attacks.” The logic of credibility not only played a role in Washington launching the Iraq war in 2003 but also locked it into the conflict, even as the costs rose and the raison d’être for the war—weapons of mass destruction—turned out to be false. As President George W. Bush said in 2006, “If we were to leave before the mission is complete, it would hurt U.S. credibility.”

Conversely, strategists have been quick to criticize U.S. leaders who backed down. During the Syrian civil war, many were concerned about the reputational damage to the United States when President Barack Obama declined to respond to the Syrian government’s use of sarin gas to attack a rebel-held suburb of Damascus, thereby violating a red line that Obama had previously declared. In 2021, many foreign policy hawks reprimanded President Joe Biden for withdrawing from Afghanistan and for waiving some U.S. sanctions on Russia’s Nord Stream 2 pipeline project. At the time, Michael McCaul, the top Republican on the House Foreign Affairs Committee, said, “U.S. credibility from Kyiv to Taipei cannot withstand another blow of this nature.” More recently, Trump himself has been accused of backing down against China on tariffs, which some have decried as another example of “Trump always chickens out,” or “TACO.” In each of these cases, critics argued that Washington’s failure to act has compromised the perception that the United States is willing to run risks and bear costs to achieve its objectives.

Despite the persistent assumption that U.S. credibility requires using force and refusing to back down, that thinking has been repeatedly challenged over the past decade. For one thing, experience has shown that a country’s past behavior may be a poor indicator of its present-day resolve. In April, for example, The New York Times reported that Trump believed that Iran was unlikely to attempt to close the Strait of Hormuz based on its past actions—such as its failure to mount a robust response to the U.S. and Israeli bombing of its nuclear facilities in the 12-day war in 2025. In reality, however, Iran has been far less restrained than expected in the current conflict and moved quickly not only to close the strait but also to attack U.S. allies and U.S. military bases across the region. In other words, Tehran’s failure to stand firm in 2025 had little bearing on its resolve in the present war.

In any case, it is far from clear that a country’s reputation for resolve is something that it can control by its actions alone. As Keren Yarhi-Milo has explained in Foreign Affairs, “credibility is in the eye of the beholder.” Even if a state acts decisively to signal strength, an adversary may not interpret that action as reason to reconsider its behavior. For example, despite Washington’s intention to communicate high resolve by bearing tremendous costs in the Vietnam War, the North Vietnamese Communist Party Central Committee concluded in 1965 that “the domestic situation in the United States, however, will not allow them to fully utilize their economic and military power in their war of aggression.” In other words, Hanoi ignored the intended signal from Washington and refused to back down.

Other pitfalls are associated with demonstrating resolve by standing firm and fighting. At the extreme, a state that leans too much on military strength can gain a reputation for aggression in ways that undermine long-term deterrence. Napoleonic France and Nazi Germany both had strong reputations for resolve, but their offensive actions ultimately provoked other countries rather than deterring them. There is certainly a risk that U.S. actions under the second Trump administration—such as decapitating the Iranian government and threatening to annex Canada, Gaza, and Greenland—could similarly backfire. For example, there is growing evidence that U.S. allies and partners in Europe, the Middle East, and Asia are rethinking their reliance on the United States and are seeking other sources of security. Far from conceding to Trump’s demands, Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney has described a “rupture” in relations between Washington and Ottawa and advocated for middle powers to band together to protect their sovereignty.

Even if using force bolsters a country’s reputation for resolve, it may harm the country’s broader credibility. For instance, Russia’s 2022 invasion of Ukraine and its willingness to absorb hundreds of thousands of casualties since then has sent a strong signal about its resolve. Yet its dramatic underperformance on the battlefield against a far smaller and less well-equipped army has likely diminished the international perception of its military effectiveness. The war in Iran could similarly tarnish the United States’ military reputation. Since February, Washington has depleted at least half of its precision-strike missiles and advanced air defense interceptor missiles. It will likely take years to replenish these stockpiles given slow manufacturing times for so-called exquisite platforms. In the meantime, the United States has fewer advanced systems to draw on if China makes a move against Taiwan or if Russia attacks Poland or Estonia. For both Russia’s and the United States’ military campaigns, then, the net effect has been to reduce rather than enhance the countries’ reputation for military capability in the eyes of their respective adversaries.

Quite apart from the negative consequences that often come with decisions to use force, there is an even more fundamental problem with conventional thinking about credibility. In practice, backing down can actually be more helpful to a country’s reputation for resolve than fighting. This becomes especially clear in looking at the long-term effects of engaging in military interventions. Although the United States was victorious in World War I, the conflict produced a lingering war-weariness among the U.S. public and key policymakers. In the years after the Armistice, Americans expressed strong support for the idea that the United States should stay out of any future European war, a sentiment that was ultimately codified in the Neutrality Acts of the 1930s. Only after Japan attacked Pearl Harbor in December 1941, more than two years after war began in Europe, did the United States enter World War II. Fighting in World War I sent a signal of strong resolve to adversaries, but Americans’ war-weariness and resurgent isolationism in the years that followed conveyed that the United States would be less resolved to fight.

This dynamic has been even more apparent in the aftermath of major U.S. wars since then. Following the Vietnam War, President Jimmy Carter declared that “young Americans should never again be sent to die in battle unless our own nation is endangered.” Consider how this kind of thinking affected adversaries’ view of the United States: In deciding to invade Kuwait in 1990, Iraqi dictator Saddam Hussein assumed that the United States was intensely casualty averse and might not intervene or would back down quickly. General Wafiq al-Samarrai, Iraq’s head of military intelligence during the Gulf War, said in a 1996 interview that Saddam “thought that America’s involvement in Vietnam had badly damaged its willingness to use military power.” Although the Vietnam War was primarily fought to buttress the United States’ reputation for resolve, it had the opposite effect in practice.

And again, during and after the U.S. war in Iraq, Americans became skeptical of costly military interventions. As a result, recent administrations have been especially hesitant to commit ground troops in combat operations. Iran is undoubtedly aware of this so-called Iraq syndrome, which may embolden Tehran to act more aggressively without having to fear a large-scale American ground operation.

There is also experimental evidence that backing down may strengthen a country’s long-term reputation for resolve. In a survey I conducted in 2021 with 100 British parliamentarians, respondents were randomly divided into two groups. The first group read about a situation in which a hypothetical country backed down from a prior conflict. The second group read about a hypothetical country that stood firm in a prior conflict but displayed the same signs of war-weariness or “syndromes” that emerged in the United States after the Vietnam and Iraq wars. Both groups were then asked to estimate the likelihood that the country in question would stand firm or back down in a current dispute with the United Kingdom. On average, parliamentarians believed that the country that backed down in the prior dispute would have greater resolve in the current dispute. In other words, if fighting leads to war-weariness, then backing down can actually be better for a state’s future credibility.

As the U.S. campaign in Iran has demonstrated, choosing to fight to maintain a reputation for resolve is an incredibly risky gamble and may backfire. To avoid repeating the same mistake, U.S. policymakers and strategists need to rethink the relationship between credibility and fighting. First, the United States should avoid fighting altogether when the primary objective is shoring up reputation rather than defending a core national issue. If a conflict directly involves key security and economic interests, as did the U.S. Revolutionary War and World War II, then fighting can be a logical choice, assuming the United States has sufficient military capabilities to win the war at reasonable cost. But if the primary argument for the war relates to future credibility, as in Vietnam, then policymakers should be wary.

Second, if Washington determines that an adversary must be confronted with military force, then it must focus on limited, achievable objectives. Wars that drag on because they have much larger aims, or are prolonged simply for reputational reasons, may erode the country’s credibility by draining munitions and making Americans more reluctant to fight in the future. The two U.S. interventions against Saddam Hussein’s Iraq illustrate this point well. In the limited 1991 Gulf War, the United States quickly expelled Iraqi forces from Kuwait at low cost and before war-weariness could set in among the U.S. public. But the U.S.-led invasion of Iraq in 2003, in which Washington pursued the far more expansive goal of regime change, continued for years at high cost and made many U.S. policymakers and ordinary citizens alike less supportive of using force in the future.

Finally, policymakers should focus less on the U.S. reputation for resolve, which they cannot control, and more on the country’s military capabilities, which they can. To deter Beijing and Moscow, Washington needs to convince both adversaries that it has the ability to prosecute a modern great-power war. The problem is that, although the United States continues to have the most advanced military capabilities and weapons systems in the world, they are limited in quantity, extremely expensive, and take a long time to replace. In the course of just a few months of fighting against Iran, the United States depleted huge percentages of its advanced and expensive stockpiles of offensive and defensive missiles.

This alarming shortfall raises new questions about whether the United States could sustain a longer war of attrition of the type Russia and Ukraine are fighting. Washington urgently needs to replenish its stockpiles and rethink its dependence on a small number of exquisite systems. It also needs to avoid costly conflicts that distract it from the principal threats it faces from China and Russia. Rather than making U.S. credibility stronger in the eyes of the world, continuing the war in Iran could well undermine it, just as fighting in Vietnam and Iraq did.

#5908. Johnson banks on GOP unity as permitting bill vote nears

Reps. Andy Harris, R-Md., on left, and Chip Roy, R-Texas, speaking, could be key to Speaker Mike Johnson's vote count on the GOP side. (Bill Clark/CQ Roll Call file photo)

The House will take up a Senate-passed bill to overhaul the federal permitting system under a rule when it returns next week, a House aide said on Tuesday. The bill, which passed the Senate on Nov. 19 in a 62-33 vote, has the endorsement of President Donald Trump and a wide range of industry groups that are generally GOP allies. Adoption of the rule, typically a party-line affair, would almost certainly lead to final passage and Trump's signature.

But it may come down to GOP attendance, with many departing members not showing up to vote in the days following the midterms. Speaker Mike Johnson, R-La., in a Nov. 20 letter to colleagues, implored them to come back to work next month to finish out the lame-duck session, citing the permitting bill vote among other things. But it remains to be seen whether defeated lawmakers will take him up on that.

And several Republicans, led by House Freedom Caucus members, have used their leverage in rule votes to extract last-minute changes to legislation from leadership.

Last December, the House considered a bill to overhaul the National Environmental Policy Act from Rep. Jared Golden, D-Maine, and House Natural Resources Chairman Bruce Westerman, R-Ark. Ahead of the vote on that rule, Republicans including Freedom Caucus Chairman Andy Harris, R-Md., sought last-minute changes to portions of the bill they were concerned would allow offshore wind projects in the Atlantic Ocean to move forward. Westerman agreed, which in turn caused the American Clean Power Association to withdraw support for the bill.

Harris hasn’t commented yet on the Senate-passed permitting measure. Nor has Freedom Caucus Rep. Chip Roy, R-Texas, who has called for permitting generally to be a lame-duck priority but was another one of the holdouts on Westerman’s bill last year. Still, the Freedom Caucus is generally on board with permitting legislation and specifically cited it as a priority for the session's waning days.

Republicans are generally expected to support the bill, which enjoys backing from industry groups like the American Petroleum Institute, National Mining Association and Edison Electric Institute, which represents investor-owned utilities. The bill’s supporters go beyond the energy sector, including groups like the American Road and Transportation Builders Association and National Association of Home Builders.

And two Democrats — Golden and Rep. Marie Gluesenkamp Perez of Washington, both members of the Blue Dog Coalition — have taken the rare step of crossing the aisle to support Republican rules this year in order to move legislation they support. Both have issued statements that speak favorably of the permitting bill.

Senate Environment and Public Works Chairman Shelley Moore Capito, one of the bill's lead authors, was enthusiastic about the bill's chances in the other chamber.

"I think there’s a real will there," the West Virginia Republican said Tuesday after presiding over a pro forma session. "We’ve got to get enough Republicans there to vote, and I think that’s what the speaker is hoping to do. I think he wants to do it under a rule, it sounds like, but I’m very excited about it because I think it’s very, very doable."

With the path through the House yet to be fully illuminated, a vote under suspension of the rules, which requires two-thirds of members present and voting to pass, remains an option. But that's a trickier path given the split among Democrats.

The Senate-passed bill emerged from that chamber in a weaker position than advocates anticipated earlier this month, when 73 senators backed advancing it on an initial procedural vote.

After opponents got through with the measure — including an amendments blockade by Oregon Democratic Sens. Jeff Merkley and Ron Wyden that left backers unable to incorporate changes necessary to shore up support — there were just 62 "yes" votes, although there were four absent GOP supporters.

Just 15 out of 47 Democratic caucus members voted for it, including only one member of the Environment and Public Works panel — Rhode Island’s Sheldon Whitehouse, who wrote the bill with his GOP and Democratic counterparts on EPW and Energy and Natural Resources.

If there is full attendance on both sides, the threshold for passage on suspension would be about 290 votes. Permitting bill supporters would need about one-third of House Democrats to vote in favor — 71 with current vacancies — similar to their level of Senate Democratic support, assuming all Republicans were on board.

That doesn’t seem out of the question given that the leadership of the 112-member-strong New Democrat Coalition has expressed support led by the current chair, Brad Schneider of Illinois; the incoming chair, fellow Illinoisan Nikki Budzinski; and Scott Peters, D-Calif., chair of the coalition’s Environment, Climate and Clean Energy Working Group. So has the centrist Blue Dog Coalition.

Clean energy groups like the Solar Energy Industries Association and American Clean Power Association back the bill, as do key labor unions including the AFL-CIO, Laborers’ International Union of North America and International Brotherhood of Electrical Workers. The AFL-CIO sent a legislative alert on the bill, which indicates it may include the measure on its annual legislative scorecard.

But Rep. Jared Huffman, D-Calif., ranking member of the House Natural Resources Committee, is a firm “no” on the current bill. He offered a series of changes on Monday needed to win his support — which have negligible chance of acceptance by the bill’s authors or by Trump, who last week said the bill would put money “back in the pockets of hardworking Americans” and lead to “tremendous amounts of new building.”

Huffman wants to roll back proposed limits on Endangered Species Act reviews as well as the bill's 150-day limit on judicial challenges and “project certainty” protections intended to bar future administrations from revoking approved permits. Huffman said the certainty protection would legislate “a third term for Trump” by preventing future administrations from revoking permits for projects that could have detrimental environmental impacts, such as fossil fuel or mining projects.

Meanwhile, the official position of the nearly 100-member Congressional Progressive Caucus is opposed. And the House Sustainable Energy and Environment Coalition said it couldn’t support the Senate bill without changes, arguing the measure “includes environmental rollbacks that are concerning to us.”

Two influential Democrats on energy and climate issues came out against the measure in its current form this week — Reps. Sean Casten of Illinois and Mike Levin of California, who are among the SEEC’s vice chairs. Casten and Levin have worked together on legislation intended to overhaul the transmission permitting system and address energy affordability.

In a statement Casten said the bill would make it “impossible” for a future administration “to undo the damage being done by the Trump Administration’s willful disregard of current environmental law and judicial precedent,” adding he would vote "no" on the Senate-passed bill “with confidence in our ability to craft a better version in the 120th Congress.”

Casten, who co-founded a clean energy startup before coming to Congress, is currently challenging House Financial Services ranking member Maxine Waters, D-Calif., for the gavel next year, in a bid resting in part on his environmental bona fides, including sustainable investment and climate-risk regulation.

Levin, an environmental lawyer by trade, praised the bill’s provisions making it easier to build interstate transmission lines, a key bottleneck barring the development of more wind and solar power generation.

“These grid reforms are real, and there’s no guarantee a better bill comes along that can actually become law soon,” Levin said in his statement. But he said it wasn’t worth the potential environmental costs, adding that “taking the current deal would mean giving up protections we may never win back in exchange for gains that aren’t guaranteed either.”

Major environmental advocacy groups like the Sierra Club and Natural Resources Defense Council have long been opposed to the measure, as have Native American tribes and representative organizations like the National Congress of American Indians.

Another factor affecting the debate in the House is how to handle a bipartisan package of Senate changes agreed to on both sides of the aisle, but blocked on the floor by Merkley, a staunch opponent of the bill.

That amendment included language making it more difficult to circumvent National Historic Preservation Act rules; giving landowners and state regulators more time to resolve disputes before eminent domain is invoked for transmission projects; and closing loopholes in the bill’s language aimed at making data center developers pay for their own electric-grid upgrades.

If that amendment had been included, the Senate vote count may have been higher. But attaching it in the House would send it back to the Senate for another round of votes, which may not be practical given the dwindling legislative session. An alternative would be to tuck the amendment into another must-pass bill, like government funding legislation or the National Defense Authorization Act.

The New Democrat Coalition’s statement, while overall supportive, said the group “will look for opportunities to negotiate changes that reflect environmental and Tribal concerns.”

Senate Indian Affairs Chairman Lisa Murkowski, R-Alaska, and ranking member Brian Schatz, D-Hawaii, worked on changes in the amendment to address concerns tribes and Native American organizations raised about the NHPA section.

“I'm not going to abandon it just because the bill has been passed now. So working it on the House side, looking for other opportunities to advance it yet this Congress,” Murkowski said Nov. 20. “So we're not done.”

Capito told reporters Tuesday that the permitting bill's fate has been invading her dreams.

"My husband Charlie wakes me up, I’m like in sleep going ‘huh, huh, huh.’ He goes, ‘What’s wrong? What’s wrong?’ I said, ‘I’m just dreaming about permitting reform,'" Capito recalled. "That’s kind of a sad state of affairs. It was our 50th anniversary.”

Lauren Green, Jackie Wang and Jacob Fulton contributed to this report.
