#5907. Do Democrats Disagree?

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Review: Do Democrats Disagree?

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

A World for All begins with a foreword from Sen. Bernie Sanders—who, being Bernie Sanders, does not pull any punches. U.S. foreign policy, he declares, has been “plagued by militarism, corporate power, and the selective application of international law.” His solution? The “United States should be pro­moting peace, not endless war,” he suggests, as well as the principle of “enlightened self-interest.”

What’s striking about the foreign-policy debate within the Democratic Party right now is that almost everyone involved would agree with Sanders’ statements. But where more traditional Democrats believe this critique applies mostly to Republicans, many progressives would apply it with equal vehemence to Democratic foreign policy as well. And where one side largely believes that the Barack Obama and Joe Biden administrations tried, however imperfectly, to advance these goals amid a host of challenges, the other believes they could have done much more.

A World for All begins with a foreword from Sen. Bernie Sanders—who, being Bernie Sanders, does not pull any punches. U.S. foreign policy, he declares, has been “plagued by militarism, corporate power, and the selective application of international law.” His solution? The “United States should be pro­moting peace, not endless war,” he suggests, as well as the principle of “enlightened self-interest.”

A World for All: New Visions of U.S. Foreign Policy, ed. Matt Duss, OR Books, 288 pp., $19.95, November 2026

What’s striking about the foreign-policy debate within the Democratic Party right now is that almost everyone involved would agree with Sanders’ statements. But where more traditional Democrats believe this critique applies mostly to Republicans, many progressives would apply it with equal vehemence to Democratic foreign policy as well. And where one side largely believes that the Barack Obama and Joe Biden administrations tried, however imperfectly, to advance these goals amid a host of challenges, the other believes they could have done much more.

A World for All, edited by Matt Duss (who previously served as a foreign-policy advisor to Sanders), bills itself as a “progressive blueprint” for U.S. foreign policy. It consists of 18 chapters, all written by different authors from the academic and think tank community covering a wide range of foreign-policy topics. At a time when a small but vocal group of Moscow apologists and Hamas enthusiasts has tried to put their stamp on the movement, this book shows that many thoughtful and principled voices still dominate progressive foreign-policy thinking. And as interest grows in the Democratic agenda after the midterms’ blue wave, it also serves as a welcome reminder of how much the entire party has in common when it comes to foreign policy.

Children wave flags and flowers as Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin attend a ceremony in Beijing on May 20.Maxim Shemetov/China Pool via Getty Images

Russia, China, and the Middle East: If there are three areas where Washington struggles in pursuing peace, it is these. So what do the contributors to A World for All propose?

The greatest continuity appears in the case of Russia. Here, Michael Kimmage, a Cold War historian, suggests four pillars for U.S. policy:

[M]aintaining and expanding meaningful channels of communication with Russia geared toward desired de-confliction and the avoidance of escalatory spirals; aiding Ukraine and containing Russian military power in Europe; monitoring Russia’s global activities and resisting them in a restrained and targeted, not reflexive or messianic fashion; and keeping an open mind as to Russia’s future.

It is hard to imagine most Biden officials finding anything here to disagree with. In fact, they might only object to the extent that this framing implies Biden himself was being reflexive, messianic, or close minded about Russia’s future.

The reason for this congruity, of course, is that the biggest obstacle to a cooperative relationship with Russia is Russia itself. As Kimmage writes, Washington “has been unable to find an enduring partnership, and it has been repeatedly surprised by aggressive Russian acts, from the invasion of Georgia in 2008 to the massive invasion of Ukraine in 2022.”

He might have added that U.S. President George W. Bush initially sought to work with Russian President Vladimir Putin in the so-called war on terror, or that Obama came into office in search of his famous “reset” with Russia. But because of Russia’s actions, even progressives are now considerably more pessimistic about the possibility of a better relationship with Moscow than both their Republican and Democratic predecessors.

In the case of China, think-tankers Jake Werner and Sandy Shan offer a more pointed contrast with Biden’s approach. With the aim of building a more cooperative relationship with Beijing, they call for the next administration to “[s]et a course to ending economic warfare against China” and normalizing “the presence of China in the US economy.” The two powers, they argue, must work together to “reform global economic governance” and “advance global climate action.”

Call them the restrainers, the riders, and the racers.

Meet the potential foreign-policy and national security big hitters of the next Congress.

The difference in their recommendations appears less in the stated goals than in what compromises a new president might make to achieve them. In responding to previous progressive critiques along the same lines, several former Biden officials have insisted that they too sought a cooperative relationship, but the problem came from Beijing. For example, Thomas Wright, who was on Biden’s National Security Council, argued in the Atlantic in July:

Biden did seek to cooperate with China on issues of mutual concern, such as AI. But he insisted that this had to be done without preconditions. That’s because those of us who worked on these policies inside the administration knew the Chinese playbook well. Beijing had little interest in cooperating on climate or anything else. It would stall by demanding that the U.S. make a concession on some matter of strategic importance, such as Taiwan and export controls.

While export controls or other economic measures might not be a concern for Werner and Shan, it is harder to tell how they think progressives should respond to demands for concessions on issues such as Taiwan. Here A World for All seeks to telegraph a different approach without necessarily spelling out the details.

Werner and Shan write that Chinese aggression against Taiwan would be a “cataclysm.” They argue that U.S. “measures to deter Chinese adventurism should therefore continue” but also be “balanced by stronger and more sincere efforts to reas­sure China that the United States is not pursuing permanent sepa­ration.” That is the sole reference to deterring Beijing in the book—which, read alongside repeated criticisms of U.S. militarism and the size of the Pentagon budget, certainly suggests a greatly reduced willingness to go to war in defense of Taiwan. And yet at face value, that line inevitably implies some commitment to using force. The result is nothing if not strategic ambiguity.

Needless to say, it is in the Middle East that the break with traditional Democratic foreign policy is clearest. The Biden administration’s consistent support for Israel’s genocidal policies in Gaza produced the starkest and most emotional division in the party. In the book, analysts Thanassis Cambanis and Zaha Hassan call for Washington to “end blank-check military support for Israel,” which increasingly seems like a demand that Democrats will make of any candidate in 2028.

Elsewhere in this section, though, their recommendations differ from past Democratic administrations more in degree than kind. They call for more diplomacy and fewer reckless wars, as well as supporting human rights over friendly autocrats. More pointedly, they call for reducing Washington’s military footprint in the region to “discourage unreliable partners and allies from starting wars they believe the US will finish for them.”

U.S. Army soldiers watch as Barack Obama is sworn in as the 44th president of the United States at a base near Baghdad on Jan. 20, 2009.Chris Hondros/Getty Images

Rhetorically, at least, these are all ambitions Obama and Biden shared. Obama ran on his opposition to the Iraq War, withdrew U.S. troops from Iraq, and resisted strong pressure to intervene in Syria. He also offered conditional support for the Arab Spring and negotiated a nuclear agreement with Iran, which he hoped would facilitate a “pivot to Asia.” Biden, for his part, campaigned on the promise to restore the Iran deal and make Saudi Arabia “a pariah” for its human rights abuses.

Both men, however, faced constraints in achieving these ambitions. Obama could have done more to push back against the military coup that ended any hope of a democratic transition in Egypt, but this was unlikely to change the ultimate outcome. In a similar vein, Obama was forced to send U.S. troops back to Iraq when the Islamic State upended the region, while Biden ultimately concluded that a fist bump with Saudi Crown Prince Mohammed bin Salman was the price to pay for the cooperation on oil prices he needed to keep his domestic agenda on track.

A more progressive administration is likely to face some of the same contradictions as their predecessors, even if they ultimately resolve them differently. Indeed, the challenge in evaluating this genre of policy writing, progressive or otherwise, is that, in the abstract, everyone wants their strategy to do everything. Whether you propose competing with rival powers while seeking areas of cooperation where possible or, alternatively, pursuing cooperation while competing where necessary, the real differences only emerge in the way policymakers negotiate the inevitable tradeoffs.

Of course, a constructive conversation about the tradeoffs Biden faced would be easier if members of his administration were more candid about where they fell short. So long as former officials continue to defend their record on Gaza, or proffer weak, equivocal, and mealy mouthed apologies, they will only make it harder to draw useful lessons from his tenure in other areas.

U.S. President Joe Biden (left) is welcomed by Saudi Crown Prince Mohammed bin Salman in Jeddah, Saudi Arabia, on July 15, 2022. Royal Court of Saudi Arabia/via Getty Images

More broadly, A World for All suggests that progressives, no less than other Democrats, are still struggling with two enduring tensions that will plague any future administration.

First, it is clear to everyone in the party that the health of U.S. democracy is intertwined with the fate of democracy worldwide. Yet the last few decades have also made it clear how limited Washington’s tools for democracy promotion can be. One approach that several authors highlight is a renewed focus on fighting corruption at home and abroad.

Progressives have led the way in insisting that authoritarianism and corruption are inextricably intertwined. A war against kleptocracy would be smart policy and smart politics, and it would certainly represent a concrete step toward making the world safe for democracy. Yet for the same reason, its impact would be limited so long as many of the countries the United States deals with remain authoritarian. Deploying Magnitsky sanctions against corrupt leaders implicated in bribing Americans or facilitating transnational repression is, in itself, an excellent idea. But it is also hard to imagine rebuilding ties with a new Russian government or pursuing a more cooperative relationship with China while simultaneously sanctioning some of their top officials.

U.S. President Donald Trump walks to Marine One at the White House in Washington on Oct. 6.Finn Gomez/Getty Images

The bigger contradiction is that in the very areas where both the traditional Democratic and progressive approaches to foreign policy differ from those of many Republicans—that is, diplomacy, cooperation, and multilateralism—they are uniquely susceptible to the kind of disruption Donald Trump has created. These all depend on trust and continuity, which is exactly what no one should expect from U.S. foreign policy in the coming decades. Among the many challenges Biden faced in restoring the Iran nuclear deal, for example, was Tehran’s demand for some reassurance that Trump would not simply tear it up again if he was reelected. This, of course, was something Biden could not provide and, as events proved, something that Iran was wise to ask for.

In their chapter on Europe, analysts Rachel Rizzo and Max Bergmann offer one clear-eyed example of what confronting this reality could look like. They argue that, rather than simply try to restore the traditional transatlantic relationship rooted in a strong U.S. commitment to NATO, the next administration should support Europe’s efforts to become “a stronger and more assertive global actor” with more independent defense capabilities.

Can Washington hope to find actors abroad who will support U.S. values more reliably than the United States itself? It’s hardly an ideal solution. But then, if Democrats win the presidency in 2028, they will hardly be inheriting an ideal foreign policy situation.

#5906. The Only Way to End the Netanyahu Era

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

AYMAN ODEH served in the Knesset from 2015 to 2026. He is the former leader of the Joint List and the Hadash party.

Arab Citizens of Israel Can Bring Change, but They Can’t Do It Alone

As the Israeli opposition leader Gadi Eisenkot struggles to assemble a governing coalition following last month’s elections, Israel finds itself in a familiar political predicament. The parties opposed to Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu and his far-right coalition command a clear majority in the Knesset, with 67 of its 120 seats. Yet the four Jewish-led parties in Eisenkot’s camp hold just 48 seats, leaving them unable to form a government on their own. The impasse echoes the prolonged political deadlock between 2019 and 2021, when Netanyahu’s supporters and his opponents remained locked in competing parliamentary blocs, and four consecutive elections failed to produce a stable governing majority.

But one thing is different this time. Israel’s Arab-led parties, the Joint List and Raam, are stronger than ever. Powered by extraordinary turnout among Arab citizens of Israel, they won a record 19 seats in the Knesset: 11 for the Joint List and eight for Raam. This political mobilization is important in itself, demonstrating Arab voters’ overwhelming desire for change and their recognition of the importance of participating in elections. But its significance goes far beyond the number of seats they have won. Together, the Arab parties now have the potential to be a decisive force in an anti-Netanyahu coalition, providing the parliamentary backing needed to form a government. The proof of this power has already been shown: on November 17, Arab support was crucial in the vote to replace the Likud speaker of the Knesset with a speaker from Eisenkot’s party. Now they have a historic opportunity to put the country on a different path.

As the first and former leader of the Joint List, I know how much is at stake. In 2019, I helped lead a groundbreaking effort to give Arab-led parties a direct voice in the Israeli political system. We took an enormous political risk by endorsing Benny Gantz, then a former chief of staff of the Israeli military and Netanyahu’s principal rival, as a candidate to form a government. We did so despite our profound political and ideological differences with him. Although that effort ultimately fell short, with Gantz choosing to pursue a “unity government” with Netanyahu instead, it established an important precedent: by showing a willingness to work with Jewish political forces with whom they had deep disagreements, parties representing the Arab public had a real chance to prevent a Netanyahu-led far-right government from holding power.

Today, after nearly four years of disastrous leadership by Netanyahu and his far-right allies, that possibility has become far more urgent. It will take political courage on both sides. Eisenkot must be able to offer something to the Arab-led parties, and the Arab-led parties will need to be comfortable offering support to a government that they may not agree with on many issues. But the remarkable Arab turnout and the sizable Israeli majority that is now against Netanyahu have pointed to a way forward. Not only Israel’s future but also its place in the region and the world may depend on the willingness of its leaders to engage with the country’s Palestinian minority. Ultimately, such a partnership will be essential to building the trust between Jews and Arabs that is a prerequisite to achieving any real progress toward a viable Israeli-Palestinian peace. This is the real choice now facing Israel: whether Israelis are prepared to replace the politics of supremacy and exclusion with a politics of equality.

The factors that drove record numbers of Palestinian citizens of Israel to the polls in October have been years in the making. Although the country’s Arab citizens make up more than 20 percent of the Israeli electorate and have long made vital contributions to Israeli society as doctors, lawyers, engineers, high-tech professionals, and in many other fields, the Israeli right has for decades demonized them as enemies: a fifth column and a threat to the state. In recent years, this hostility has increasingly manifested itself in harassment and open racism at universities and workplaces, in the media, in the parliament, and in everyday life.

Yet it would be a mistake to assume that Arab exclusion has been driven only by Netanyahu and the radical right. Zionist parties of the center and the left have also contributed, by keeping a distance from Arab voters or simply ignoring them. The belief that Jewish citizens should ultimately determine the country’s political direction while Arab citizens must remain within whatever limits have been set for them runs deep in Israeli politics. Netanyahu understood early on how to turn this contradiction into a political strategy. Since the 1990s, he has repeatedly used the same formula: inciting his supporters against Arab citizens, delegitimizing Arab representatives in parliament, and convincing Jewish Israelis that a government that relies on Arab political support would somehow be illegitimate.

These tactics proved to be among Netanyahu’s most effective political tools, especially given that Arab-led parties have long been some of his biggest foes. He didn’t need to convince everyone to support his Likud party or its various coalition partners. He only needed to convince enough people, including Jewish Israelis who opposed him, that working with Arab-led parties was dangerous and unacceptable. Netanyahu’s strategy also helped suppress Arab turnout. Since the Arab-led parties had little chance of changing the status quo, Arab voters had little incentive to vote: for many years, a significantly lower proportion of Arab citizens went to the polls than did their Jewish counterparts.

That began to change in 2015 with the creation of the Joint List, which for the first time brought together four parties representing the Arab public on a single electoral ticket and which became the third-largest faction in the Knesset. By embracing a broader vision for our society, grounded in Jewish-Arab solidarity and opposition to the racism of the right, the Joint List demonstrated that Palestinian citizens of Israel could play a new and more influential role in the country’s political system. Then, under my leadership in 2019, the Joint List decided to back Gantz’s bid to unseat Netanyahu. The decision was highly controversial, both within the Arab electorate and across Israeli society. Many Arab voters were deeply ambivalent about supporting a potential government led by a former army chief whose political positions, particularly on the status of Palestinian citizens of Israel and the occupation, differed sharply from their own. Nevertheless, we were prepared to take that political risk because we understood that defeating Netanyahu was more important.

In practice, our willingness to cooperate with the Zionist opposition was not, in fact, reciprocated. The Jewish opposition parties wanted our votes but could not bring themselves to accept us as equal political partners. And, ultimately, Gantz decided to form a government with Netanyahu instead. Nevertheless, the experience showed that Jewish-Arab political cooperation could open up new horizons for a large segment of the public. Since then, every time that Arab-led parties have recommended or supported the effort of a Jewish candidate to form a government, or whenever the Israeli center-left has depended on their support, additional segments of Israeli society have become more open to this kind of cooperation.

In 2021–22, an Arab party, Raam, joined the short-lived government led by Naftali Bennett and Yair Lapid. I had serious reservations about the model of partnership that emerged, which seemed to rest on the expectation that Arab political representatives would limit their involvement to civilian and domestic matters, while refraining from addressing political and national questions, particularly the occupation and the Palestinian issue. I reject this distinction. We are part of the Palestinian people, and our identity and political concerns cannot be separated from our citizenship in Israel.

But these kinds of slow and painful steps are how political change often happens in national struggles. It does not always begin with a moral or democratic awakening. Sometimes, it begins with pragmatic necessity. When there is no other option, people begin to reconsider what had once been seen as impossible. In the current election, that dynamic has become even more important. After the unending crisis of the past four years, many Israeli voters, including both Arabs and Jews as well as the leaders of most of the Zionist opposition parties, have concluded that getting rid of the Netanyahu government is more necessary than ever.

In recent past elections, voter turnout among Palestinian citizens of Israel has generally remained between 50 and 60 percent. What explains the unprecedented 20-percentage-point surge in turnout in the October election? Three developments stand out: the war of annihilation in Gaza and the killing of tens of thousands of Palestinians; the daily racism and hostility that Arab citizens now face in the streets, in public spaces, and in virtually every aspect of their lives; and the crime that has spiraled out of control in Arab communities under the national security minister, Itamar Ben-Gvir, whose policies have allowed it to flourish, and the relentless settler violence against Palestinians in the West Bank enabled by Finance Minister Bezalel Smotrich. All the grief and frustration accumulated over these years were brought to the ballot box.

But there was another crucial factor as well. In the run-up to the election, Arab citizens, following the nearly even polls between Eisenkot and Netanyahu, could see in black and white just how much the outcome would depend on them. Voting this time would be more than a political choice. It was for many a personal responsibility and a way of taking control of their own future. That is why hundreds of thousands of Palestinian citizens of Israel made their way to the polls, defying years of efforts to silence them and undermine their political agency. Many understood that the future direction of the country might be quite literally in their hands.

I saw elderly people, like my friend Jumah from Jisr al-Zarqa, taking to the streets and campaigning for the Joint List. Many of them had lived through the 1948 nakba, or “catastrophe,” which accompanied Israel’s establishment and involved terrible massacres, the destruction of hundreds of Palestinian villages and towns, and the displacement of around 750,000 Palestinians. I saw young people debating politics in the cafés and restaurants of my beloved Haifa, persuading their friends to vote. I saw women and men from Nazareth, Umm al-Fahm, Sakhnin, Rahat, Akko, Kafr Yasif, Majd al-Krum, and countless other towns and villages united by a shared sense of purpose. I saw political consciousness become a living, collective force. For a moment, the boundaries between generations and communities seemed to disappear.

The result marks a remarkable advance of the process of Arab participation in the Israeli political system that began in 2019. In this election, Arab citizens of Israel turned out in record numbers, dramatically narrowing the historical turnout gap with Jewish voters. And that has made another political reality increasingly clear: Israel’s liberal and centrist camp cannot form a government without the parties that represent the Arab public. If it excludes them, as well as the ultra-Orthodox and the far right, it will not reach the 61 seats needed. This is the demographic reality. Israeli Jews who oppose Netanyahu must decide whom they are willing to build a political future with.

The debate about what Eisenkot should do and whether he should ask for Arab support is now unfolding beyond the Knesset, in people’s homes, in cafés, in universities, and on the street. It is a deeply controversial question, and many Israelis disagree strenuously about it. Yet for the Arab-led parties, gaining sufficient importance to be able to provoke these conversations is an achievement. The fact that this debate is even taking place shows how the political process has caused Israelis to consider possibilities that until recently seemed unimaginable.

In the end, Eisenkot may have few other options if he wants to replace Netanyahu, as he has promised. In most cases, political parties support coalitions to advance positive changes and achieve concrete goals. But there are moments when political cooperation is necessary for a different reason: to prevent something far worse. The most immediate and overriding objective today is to bring an end to the far-right Netanyahu government. That is a priority on which both the Zionist opposition and the Arab-led parties can agree.

Beyond this shared basic goal, the Arab-led parties should use their political influence to work on two levels. First, they must seek tangible improvements in their constituents’ lives: combating organized crime in Arab communities, securing budgets, addressing the housing crisis, ending the brutal treatment of Palestinians living under occupation in the West Bank, and pushing for a permanent cease-fire and the reconstruction of Gaza. These are fundamental issues of human dignity and civilian life. On a broader level, they should continue to push for real progress on Israeli-Palestinian peace that is ultimately based on two states coexisting, side by side.

I do not expect the current Israeli political establishment to embrace these positions, but that does not mean that the Arab parties should not raise them at every opportunity. At the same time, the Arab parties can use their newfound political clout to build alliances between Jews and Arabs around shared political and strategic goals. The resulting partnerships must be about more than parliamentary arrangements; it must also create the foundations for a different future. That will require Jewish and Arab citizens of Israel to engage with one another on the basis of equality, rather than mere political convenience.

Thus far, Eisenkot has given few indications that he is prepared to go down this path. Since the election, it has been encouraging to see him condemn Netanyahu for fueling what he described as a “racist and violent” campaign against Israel’s Arab citizens. Yet many of the mainstream Zionist parties in Eisenkot’s camp have vowed not to work with the Arab parties. Still, Eisenkot must consider what that would mean. Thirty years from now, people will ask how Jewish supremacy became so normalized that even politicians who called themselves democrats accepted the exclusion of one-fifth of their fellow citizens. They will ask why Israel’s opposition could recognize the danger posed by Netanyahu’s government but not confront the assumptions that made his politics possible.

There is an irony in the fact that, in a country that seeks to keep political hegemony exclusively in Jewish hands, the Palestinian minority, after decades of struggle for political recognition and equal rights, has emerged as one of the most significant forces shaping the country’s political future. We have come to understand that we must be courageous and cooperate with the Zionist parties of the center and left in order to oust Netanyahu, Ben-Gvir, and Smotrich. The question now is: Do these parties, which call themselves democratic and liberal, have the same courage?

Only a Radical Course Change Can Win Back Its Most Important Ally

A Failing Peace Process Leads to Only One Outcome

Conflicts in the Middle East and the Horn of Africa Have Become Inseparable

Why a New Majority—Even in Gaza—Supports Compromise With Israel

Michael Robbins, Amaney A. Jamal, and Mark Tessler

The Netanyahu Doctrine Could Outlast Netanyahu

Why the Islamic Republic Can’t Make Peace

How China Exploited a Massive Loophole in U.S. National Security—and How Congress Can Close It

Democracy Can’t Be Saved at the Ballot Box

What America Should Give to Make Peace in Ukraine

#5905. Trump’s ‘Unprecedented Intervention’ in Nvidia

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Q&A: Trump’s ‘Unprecedented Intervention’ in Nvidia

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Prefer to listen? To hear this entire conversation, and more episodes in the weeks ahead, follow Ones and Tooze wherever you get your podcasts.

Jensen Huang was reinstated this week as CEO of Nvidia, a semiconductor manufacturer that’s a linchpin of the global artificial intelligence economy. That occurred only after U.S. President Donald Trump personally intervened to persuade Nvidia’s board—despite allegations that Huang’s affair with an alleged Chinese spy, and his efforts to sell Nvidia’s chips to China in contravention of U.S. policy, may have undermined U.S. national security.

Was Huang’s reinstatement initiated by Silicon Valley or D.C. elites? What levers did Trump use to pressure Nvidia’s board? Does Huang, or Nvidia more generally, qualify as “too big to fail”?

Jensen Huang was reinstated this week as CEO of Nvidia, a semiconductor manufacturer that’s a linchpin of the global artificial intelligence economy. That occurred only after U.S. President Donald Trump personally intervened to persuade Nvidia’s board—despite allegations that Huang’s affair with an alleged Chinese spy, and his efforts to sell Nvidia’s chips to China in contravention of U.S. policy, may have undermined U.S. national security.

Was Huang’s reinstatement initiated by Silicon Valley or D.C. elites? What levers did Trump use to pressure Nvidia’s board? Does Huang, or Nvidia more generally, qualify as “too big to fail”?

Those are just a few of the questions that came up in my recent conversation with FP economics columnist Adam Tooze on the podcast we co-host, Ones and Tooze. What follows is an excerpt, edited for length and clarity. For the full conversation, look for Ones and Tooze wherever you get your podcasts. And check out Adam’s Substack newsletter.

Cameron Abadi: Was Huang’s reinstatement initiated by Silicon Valley tech elites or by D.C. political elites—or an entwinement between the two?

Adam Tooze: Some reporting suggests that the rally around Huang really began in Silicon Valley itself. So it was a key group of people who were close to him: Uber founder Travis Kalanick, a bunch of venture capitalists like Gavin Baker, who’s been tweeting very effusively about this, Brad Gerstner and Marc Andreessen, who we’ve discussed on the show before. Heavy hitters. Apparently, there’s some Goldman Sachs interest as well. None of those people have really been willing to comment. But there was a rallying around Jensen Huang and the need to bring him back in. I think, basically, it was a combination of their influence plus a sell-off in the market that really prompted Trump to act. The venture capitalists have good connections to [Vice President] J.D. Vance, because he had that little foray in Silicon Valley finance by way of Peter Thiel. And they seem to then have been able to act on figures inside the Trump administration. Jensen Huang is, after all, very well known to Trump. The two have been seen together a lot in public.

And then there was some last-minute fig leaf-style conversations with the attorney general just to confirm that they’re not actually supporting a figure that is currently under investigation by the American judicial authorities. So apparently the attorney general, Todd Blanche, signed off on this. And that was really then the decisive moment when Trump then intervened with the board, which seems to be a remarkably weak institution. For a company of Nvidia’s size and significance, its corporate governance is schoolyard. It comes out of the era when it was a scrappy manufacturer of accelerator chips for video games rather than the biggest company in the world and the backstop to AI. So it seems to just be this incredible kind of jury-rigged, duct tape—you know, people refer to duct-tape empire—kind of setup that we’re dealing with here. And truly dramatic in its impact then on the market, though questions are being asked, I think, about how long the rebound can really last given the underlying questions about AI and the broader economy.

CA: What are the mechanics by which a leader of the U.S. government overrides a corporate board? And are there any precedents for a U.S. president personally intervening in a boardroom decision like this?

AT: I don’t think he has very many direct levers, other than his public sway. As corporate America has demonstrated across the board during his second term, no one wants to be on his wrong side. So if he told this weak corporate governance body at Nvidia that, you know, this is the way to go, and there really were no legal objections, at least at the level of espionage, then presumably they felt it was in the shareholders’ best interest to just do as they were told. It’s not entirely without precedent.

The most recent instance would be the Obama administration’s move against the leadership of General Motors in the spring of 2009 when the federal government did a bailout for General Motors. And they removed Rick Wagoner, who they didn’t feel that they could work with. Then beyond that, you have to go further back in history to the Cold War-era presidencies. So there’s action by the [Franklin] Roosevelt administration against the Ford Motor Company leadership when they were deeply worried during the wartime about the succession of Henry Ford and deaths within the Ford family. There’s Truman intervening in the steel industry in the early 1950s. There’s a moment when Kennedy also intervenes at U.S. Steel in ’62.

But, you know, the fundamental difference here is that we’re not in a state of war. This is not a matter of urgent national security or of macroeconomic pressure like in the early 1950s during the Korean War. This isn’t the aftermath of the 2008 financial crisis. Nvidia is not on the receiving end of a federal government bailout. You know, the analogies quite rapidly break down and leave one feeling that in some senses, this truly is an unprecedented intervention. This is the most valuable company in the world at various points. And it just basically doesn’t appear to have any serious corporate governance whatsoever.

Jensen Huang and the broader AI sector represent an enormous risk for the entire public.

The return of Nvidia’s Jensen Huang exposes a dangerous gap in corporate oversight.

CA: The intervention on behalf of Jensen Huang seemed to be motivated by a sense that he was somehow essential, not only to the success of the company, but through the company, to the success of the American economy overall. In what sense could Huang be considered to be “too big to fail”?

AT: Well, that term comes out of banking. And within the banking sector, since the Dodd-Frank reforms and other global financial stability reforms in the aftermath of 2008, designations as GSIB, so globally systemically significant banks and financial institutions, are formalized and have been formalized. And they do actually have a relatively formal or quantitative definition. I mean, you can look at bank balance sheets and just trace networks of interconnection and risks that arise from those. And there’s a quite serious study of those kinds of risks. And one could do the same for Nvidia. And there has been a great deal of speculation along these lines, or not just speculation, but investigation. And it’s quite clear that the circular financing mechanisms that we’ve also discussed on the show going back awhile now all center on Nvidia. And so questions have been asked about the stability of the Nvidia financing structure.

But I think the question was always, should they not be subject to some kind of oversight? Right now, there is no risk of failure, right? In some senses this invocation of failure in the worst-case scenario—you know, she’s a spy and Nvidia is going to fail and therefore, you know, we must do x or y, or not do x or y, or bring him back or shove him out or whatever—the whole discourse is massively exaggerated. I mean, they’re a very successful tech business, which right now is in the middle of a boom in AI, which does have some bubble characteristics. But the financing of this is extended and ramified, but it’s the cloud providers, not necessarily Nvidia, that anyone thought of as being immediately in trouble. And even those are some way away from outright failure. And so this, you know, this language of exigency and this language of crisis, you know, it has a historical root and there are some structural reasons why one might worry about Nvidia, but it doesn’t justify the current action one way or the other. And the fact that the company is as poorly structured as it is such that so much revolves around one man is prima-facie reason for, you know, some serious rethinking and some serious structural governance.

But that would require market pressure. And you know, the financial markets right now are bonkers. That they would reprice the company by 30 percent of a trillion dollars on the basis of this kind of news is, in itself, you know, profoundly delegitimizing. So to my mind, like, it’s an absolute disaster of American public discourse, because the allegations appear to be hugely exaggerated, the damage done to the market suggests that the corporate governance is terrible, the reporting by the American media has been shambolic, the president’s intervention is unprecedented and bonkers, and now the markets vindicate the whole thing by raising the value of Nvidia by 30 percent. I mean, it feels like everyone’s kind of lost their minds in some ways. And yet, as someone famously said, whilst the music plays, you dance. Because if you’re not on the Nvidia train, you’re not doing as well as other people. And all of these boosters are saying this is a vindication of the American success story and Jensen Huang is the latest character in that ongoing narrative. It all feels a bit delirious, to be honest.

Cameron Abadi is a deputy editor at Foreign Policy. X: @CameronAbadi

Adam Tooze is a columnist at Foreign Policy and a history professor and the director of the European Institute at Columbia University. He is the author of Chartbook, a newsletter on economics, geopolitics, and history. X: @adam_tooze

#5904. Trump, NATO chief push Russia to avoid any actions that would 'expand the war' with Ukraine

Fox News foreign correspondent Jeff Paul joins ‘The Story’ to comment on a study estimating that more than 700,000 Russian troops have been killed since the war in Ukraine began. He also reports on Poland’s accusation that Russia violated its airspace, prompting international concern.

President Donald Trump and NATO Secretary-General Mark Rutte are calling on Russia to avoid making any moves "that intentionally, or unintentionally, expand the war" against Ukraine.

The declaration came after the pair got together at Trump's Mar-a-Lago residence in Florida on Thanksgiving Eve for what was described as an "outstanding meeting."

However, Russia hit Kyiv with ballistic missiles overnight, according to the mayor of the Ukrainian capital.

"The meeting was very impactful, with the war between Russia and Ukraine and the conflict in Iran being the primary subjects, as well as all of the record setting achievements that we have made together at the summits in the Hague and at Ankara," Trump and Rutte said in a joint statement.

"We also discussed the importance of Russia avoiding any actions that intentionally, or unintentionally, expand the war, and the importance of obtaining a peaceful resolution to the war as rapidly as possible," the statement added. "Lastly, we talked about the importance of continuing to expand defense spending and investment in our industrial base, both in the United States and across NATO."

PUTIN'S WAR DEATH TOLL REPORTEDLY TOPS 700,000 AS RUSSIA SCRAMBLES FOR RECRUITS

NATO Secretary-General Mark Rutte meets with President Donald Trump during a visit to Mar-a-Lago in Florida on Wednesday, Nov. 25, 2026. (Reuters)

On Thursday, Trump said on Truth Social that "during my conversations in China with President Vladimir Putin, of Russia, I spent a lot of time talking about the fact that I would like to see President Putin pull back all Military aggression against territories throughout Europe, in the hopes that a Deal can be made between Russia and Ukraine, and made quickly."

Trump met with Putin recently at the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation (APEC) Summit in Shenzhen, China.

"I really believe this is a strong possibility — Thousands of people are being killed each and every month, A COMPLETE BLOODBATH — and will be a Great Tribute to everyone!" Trump added. " It was an Honor meeting with Secretary General of NATO, Mark Rutte, last night at Mar-a-Lago, where many topics were discussed, and in which I updated him on my talks with President Putin."

Rutte also said in a post on X following the meeting, "It’s clearer than ever that American leadership remains essential as we build a stronger Europe in a stronger NATO."

The Kremlin on Thursday accused Ukraine of trying to expand the war after Ukraine sank a Russian warship in Libya using drones and attacked a Russian military-linked cargo ship in the Gulf of Finland this past weekend, Reuters reported.

"As regards the escalation of the conflict in Ukraine, this appears to refer to attempts by the Kyiv regime to expand the geographical scope of this conflict. We are witnessing terrorist activity by the Kyiv regime in the Mediterranean and the Baltic," Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov was quoted as saying.

NATO SECRETARY GENERAL MARK RUTTE WARNS ON UKRAINE WAR RISKS AHEAD OF TRUMP MEETING

A fire burns in the Desnianskyi district following a Russian ballistic missile strike in Kyiv, Ukraine, on Thursday, Nov. 26, 2026. Russian forces attacked the district, causing multiple fires and injuring seven people. (Ukrinform/Ukrinform/Sipa USA/Reuters)

"This cannot fail to cause concern amongst coastal states, and most likely within the Alliance as a whole — the North Atlantic Treaty Organization. Of course, nobody would want the conflict to spread geographically, and these are potentially very dangerous actions on the part of the Kyiv regime," Peskov added, according to Reuters.

Kyiv Mayor Vitali Klitschko said at least seven people were wounded in Russian strikes overnight, hitting a shopping center and other buildings, Reuters also reported.

Russia's President Vladimir Putin and President Donald Trump shake hands at the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit in Shenzhen, China, on Nov. 19, 2026. (Vyacheslav Prokofyev/Pool/AFP via Getty Images)

Russia's Defense Ministry reportedly claimed that it struck two sites linked to the production of drones.

#5903. De Gaulle and the Art of European Defiance

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Review: De Gaulle and the Art of European Defiance

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

With year-end best-of lists about to arrive, one strong contender for film of the year might seem unlikely at first: a five-hour, two-part, largely French-language biopic of French general and statesman Charles de Gaulle that only recently reached U.S. screens. Directed by Antonin Baudry and starring a strikingly de Gaulle-like Simon Abkarian, this French blockbuster comes with an almost-American budget of 74 million euros, one of the largest in the history of francophone cinema. Perhaps unsurprisingly given its subject, it became the hit of the French summer, offering audiences a nostalgic vision of national greatness at a moment of paralysis and division.

More surprisingly, the de Gaulle diptych has won praise outside of France as well, helped by the fact that it works superbly as both action and entertainment. Epic in scale, beautifully shot, both wryly amusing and often touching, its twin installments pass far more quickly than their daunting runtime might suggest. There are clear contemporary echoes, too, in de Gaulle’s personal struggles and story of dogged European defiance. Indeed, it is not hard to see why viewers in France and elsewhere have warmed to this tale of an embattled European leader fighting to re-build sovereignty in the face of pressure from the United States as the moorings of a crumbling old order give way to something frightening and new.

With year-end best-of lists about to arrive, one strong contender for film of the year might seem unlikely at first: a five-hour, two-part, largely French-language biopic of French general and statesman Charles de Gaulle that only recently reached U.S. screens. Directed by Antonin Baudry and starring a strikingly de Gaulle-like Simon Abkarian, this French blockbuster comes with an almost-American budget of 74 million euros, one of the largest in the history of francophone cinema. Perhaps unsurprisingly given its subject, it became the hit of the French summer, offering audiences a nostalgic vision of national greatness at a moment of paralysis and division.

More surprisingly, the de Gaulle diptych has won praise outside of France as well, helped by the fact that it works superbly as both action and entertainment. Epic in scale, beautifully shot, both wryly amusing and often touching, its twin installments pass far more quickly than their daunting runtime might suggest. There are clear contemporary echoes, too, in de Gaulle’s personal struggles and story of dogged European defiance. Indeed, it is not hard to see why viewers in France and elsewhere have warmed to this tale of an embattled European leader fighting to re-build sovereignty in the face of pressure from the United States as the moorings of a crumbling old order give way to something frightening and new.

Each of the film’s two parts is just over two and a half hours long. The first, De Gaulle: Résistance (158 minutes; released in France as L’Age de fer), opens with the humiliation of the French collapse in June 1940. Refusing to accept the armistice, de Gaulle flees to England in a tiny plane. From there, he presses British Prime Minister Winston Churchill for greater support and builds a shaky government-in-exile from scratch, operating from a tiny office in central London.

The second, De Gaulle: Liberté (159 minutes; J’écris ton nom in France), covers 1943 to the end of 1944, following both the actions of the Resistance and de Gaulle’s power struggles with rival claimants to French leadership, before moving on to D-Day and the liberation of Paris. The epic closes movingly on Armistice Day, when Churchill visits the freed capital as de Gaulle’s guest.

For audiences outside France, some of this will be familiar, from de Gaulle’s defiant broadcasts on the BBC to his haughty and stubborn personal affect. Undeniably imperious and quite unable to forget a slight, Abkarian’s de Gaulle is both a model of determination in the face adversity and, to the Allied leaders who bankrolled him, a quintessentially French pain in the ass.

A still from De Gaulle: Résistance.Pathe Films

Other episodes are less well known in the English-speaking world, however, even as they remain central to France’s own story of the war, from the British destruction of the French fleet at Mers-el-Kébir in July 1940 (to keep its warships out of German hands) to the epic battle at Bir Hakeim in mid-1942. Perhaps now more familiar to tourists as the name of a Paris metro station, Bir Hakeim saw 3,700 Free French soldiers and their African colonial troops hold an isolated desert fort in Libya against German Gen. Erwin Rommel’s Afrika Korps for more than two weeks. Fighting against overwhelming odds, their bravery allowed Britain’s Eighth Army to fall back to El Alamein and regroup, marking an eventual turning point in the wider war. Providing the first film with a dramatic 15-minute denouement, the battle proved that de Gaulle’s ragtag movement could fight as a real army, provided a much-needed fillip to Free French morale, and strengthened the general’s position as a legitimate and potentially successful French leader.

Although this is very much a big-budget war movie, much of its power lies, paradoxically, in its intimate portrayal of the quiet complexities of leadership. In the public mind, de Gaulle can often come across as a cartoonish figure, all stubborn resolve, frosty self-regard, and Gallic hauteur. Abkarian’s ramrod-stiff performance is magnetic and provides plenty of evidence of this. But it also offers something more human, portraying with affection de Gaulle’s strange mix of grandeur and loneliness. The first film dwells in particular on his isolation and frequent despair. Arriving in London as a defeated tank division commander and former undersecretary of war, de Gaulle has no army, no money, barely any staff, and little claim to legitimate leadership. His situation is often tragi-comic, as when he tries unsuccessfully to win over members of the French expatriate community at a drinks party, or when he attempts to address a British audience in a packed theater, only to walk off ashamed without saying a word. In one especially memorable scene, he weeps alone in the bath of his small, dingy flat. In many others he is disconsolate, close to losing hope, and tempted to give up.

This most Gallic of films is in fact based on an equally epic book by a British academic: A Certain Idea of France, Julian Jackson’s 900-page biography, published in 2018. Reviewing it in the New Yorker, Adam Gopnik dwelt on the crippling weakness de Gaulle faced in 1940, and how he ultimately managed to think his way out of it via a mixture of imagination, self-belief, and obstinacy. De Gaulle depended “more on theatrical élan than did pretty much any other public man of his century,” as Gopnik put it. “No one has ever played a weaker hand more compellingly. His life was one long brilliant bluff.”

It is this vulnerability, rather than the more familiar image of pigheaded strength, that likely appealed to French audiences, prompting a reassessment of his traditionally unbending image and the controversial Gaullist legacy it inspired. For all that, the film is also huge fun. Its action sequences are slickly executed and, unusually for a European production, come with plenty of exuberant, Hollywood-style explosions. But it works as entertainment above all because at its center is the double act of de Gaulle and Churchill, played by Simon Russell Beale, easily the best of the many actors who have attempted the role.

A still from De Gaulle: Liberté.Pathe Films

The two men are endlessly frustrated by one another and an obvious study in contrasts: one reserved, austere, and abstemious, the other impulsive, theatrical, and alcoholic. Yet each recognizes in the other a wartime leader fighting to save his nation against massive odds, creating a certain fondness and clear mutual regard. Among their many fine scenes is a comic row involving translation. De Gaulle speaks poor English; Churchill’s French is comical but serviceable. As they argue, both men hear their respective interpreters diplomatically softening their words. Each leader throws his man out of the room in turn, preferring to fight out their differences in broken language. The grudging but sincere warmth between the two both humanizes the complexities of wartime leadership and provides the film’s emotional heart.

Some critics have accused director Baudry of playing fast and loose with history, mixing invented characters with historical leaders, among other sins against accuracy. Jackson, who acted as a consultant on the production, has written warmly in its defense: “My pavlovian historical pedantry – ‘no that is wrong, it didn’t happen like that’, ‘you have these events in the wrong order’, etc – was quickly slapped down with the reminder that this was a film not a documentary.”

The film’s very French portrayal of the United States, with a Machiavellian President Franklin D. Roosevelt cast as a chief villain, is one obvious area of potential historical dispute. But it also provides the film with its drama. Curiously, Adolf Hitler is never seen and barely even mentioned, while few German soldiers even appear. Instead, the real antagonist is Roosevelt’s Washington, and the U.S. president’s mounting frustration with an upstart French exile who contributes little manpower to the war effort yet irritatingly demands to be treated as an equal and a great power. In this sense it has something in common with zombie movies, where the greatest danger often comes less from the outside menace and more from potential betrayal within the group of humans struggling to survive.

A still from De Gaulle: Liberté.Pathe Films

In Roosevelt’s eyes, France had forfeited any claim to great-power status, while de Gaulle himself was merely an arrogant annoyance and obstacle to his plans. This broader threat to French survival is embodied in a plan to create the Allied Military Government of Occupied Territories (AMGOT), which the film presents as a U.S. scheme to permanently dismantle and divide the country. In this vision, a liberated France would be run by U.S. and British administrators, much as occupied Germany later was. That AMGOT was never the fully formed plan for France the film suggests is almost beside the point. In one memorable scene, Churchill is shown a future map of Europe in which France has vanished, carved up into a “New Wallonia” and other invented entities. In an act of covert friendship, he leaks it to de Gaulle; a rare act of British solidarity with the French over the Americans.

As exaggerated as the film’s version of the AMGOT plan may be—and Jackson concedes it is somewhat overplayed—the question of how to deal with a powerful United States runs throughout the film, giving it its most obvious modern resonance. In part this reflects a traditional and enduring Franco-British divide, with France seeking self-sufficiency and sovereignty while Britain prefers proximity and access to Washington.

To distract from runaway protests, the French president is making a show of independence on the world stage.

The film’s ideas have poisoned military thinking for centuries.

On the eve of D-Day in June 1944, for instance, de Gaulle’s demands over his forces’ role in the coming liberation of France prompt another giant bust-up with Churchill, and one especially memorable exchange, recounted in de Gaulle’s own highly readable memoirs. “”Every time we have to choose between Europe and the open seas, we shall always choose the open seas,”” he recalls Churchill declaring. “”Every time I have to choose between you and Roosevelt, I shall always choose Roosevelt.”” From Churchill’s point of view, this was entirely sensible; Britain had no obvious alternative to a superpower’s existential support, and it would have been foolish to side with an exile liberation army possessing no resources of its own. Nonetheless, it was a line de Gaulle would go on to quote throughout his life: an emblem of British perfidy and deference to Washington.

Late in the second film, in a scene set in November 1944, de Gaulle sounds out Churchill on a deeper Anglo-French alliance. Churchill declines because Britain must stay close to Washington. The portrayal feels both enduring but also clearly modern, depicting two exhausted European powers facing common adversity but still quite unable to choose to be full partners with one another.

More than anything, the film brings to mind the modern predicament of embattled and weakened middle powers, trying to find their way in a world dominated by far larger and more chaotic forces. During World War II, Britain and France both still thought of themselves as great powers—in de Gaulle’s case almost absurdly so, given his limited circumstances. Yet both clearly lived at the mercy of others, be they Hitler’s marauding armies or the caprice of an indispensable but overbearing U.S. ally.

Today European nations face a version of the same bind, struggling to rebuild sovereignty in a world dominated by superpowers increasingly willing to coerce and threaten them. The message of the film is clearly a comfortable one for French audiences: France was right all along about untrustworthy Americans, and about the need for strength and independence rather than reliance on potentially unreliable U.S.-led institutions like NATO. Nonetheless, here de Gaulle offers modern Europeans clear lessons on how to counteract weakness, via a mixture of political imagination, risk-taking, and—above all—a willingness to stand up to U.S. power from time to time.

What most people understand as “Gaullist” policies are increasingly common far beyond France as our geopolitical order frays, not least intrusive industrial policies aimed at technological self-sufficiency. Ben Judah, a British author and former political advisor, has gone further, proposing a new agenda of “Anglo-Gaullism” inspired by the French leader’s mixture of interventionism and political élan. “[T]he genius of de Gaulle was to re-enchant through storytelling a nation that had utterly lost confidence in itself,” he writes. Europe could clearly use a little more of that Gaullist enchantment today.

It is almost a cliche to contrast the giants of World War II with the slighter figures of our own generation. Yet watching de Gaulle’s struggles, it is hard not to think of the supplication of modern leaders like Mark Rutte, NATO’s secretary-general, whose approach to U.S. President Donald Trump was memorably described by Susan Glasser of the New Yorker as “strategic self-abasement.” De Gaulle is often fairly criticized for a grandiose vision of French leadership that far outstripped France’s actual power, leading to unhelpful delusions of national grandeur fed by the vision of its grandest of leaders. Nonetheless, de Gaulle’s obdurate refusal to accept decline—and his determination that France should stand, as he put it, as a nation “in the front rank”—should serve as an inspiration to Europe’s leaders today. This, too, is the lesson of the two excellent films that bear his name: to hold a vision of national power and to will it obstinately into existence, even if only by learning how to play a bad hand well.

#5902. America’s Bad Bargain in Venezuela

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

Javier Corrales is Dwight W. Morrow 1895 Professor of Political Science at Amherst College and the author of Autocracy Rising: How Venezuela Transitioned to Authoritarianism.

U.S. Dealmaking Is Strengthening the Chavista Regime

After the U.S. military captured then Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro in January, the United States set out on a mission to fix what President Donald Trump called a “dead country.” With broad authority over Venezuela’s agenda, officials in the Trump administration promised to free the country from socialism. As Secretary of State Marco Rubio said, when he was called before the Senate in late January, the administration’s ultimate objective was to establish “a friendly, stable, prosperous Venezuela, and democratic, in which all elements of society are represented in free and fair elections.”

Rubio laid out a three-stage framework for managing this democratic transition: stabilization, economic recovery, and, finally, transition. Washington is currently focused on the second stage, recovering the economy, but it is proceeding so quickly and with such little transparency that it is jeopardizing the stated end goal entirely. U.S. dealmaking with the country’s oil sector, in particular, is reifying rather than transforming Venezuela’s economic rentism: a model of governance in which the state controls important sectors of the economy and offers business contracts and market privileges to firms that support its agenda. This is precisely the system that facilitated the Venezuelan state’s rampant corruption over the past 25 years, allowing autocratic leaders to drown out democratic forces and win elections.

As it pursues its economic goals with dizzying speed, the Trump administration is also slow-walking democratization efforts. For months, Washington excluded opposition leader María Corina Machado and her party, Vente Venezuela, from any direct involvement in negotiations. Machado had left the country last December to receive a Nobel Peace Prize after nearly 20 months in hiding, and after Maduro’s removal, the United States did little to help her return. Venezuela’s pro-democracy forces have thus been left feeling like they are pushing the regime in Caracas to democratize with the acquiescence, but not necessarily the full enthusiasm, of the United States.

Last month, Machado returned to Venezuela, and now she has the potential to change the game. Machado could become the greatest asset or, possibly, the greatest disruptor to Venezuela’s democratic transition. If Machado coordinates with the faction of the opposition that is currently negotiating with the government, any outcome will be seen as legitimate. But if she opposes and speaks out against the result, Venezuelans will find themselves in a familiar and dangerous place: with a divided opposition, a government with few constraints on its power, and a transition in purgatory.

So far, Washington has squandered its immense leverage over Caracas. By dragging out the transition and pursuing questionable economic deals, Washington is endangering not only its own end goal but also the greatest opportunity Venezuela has had to overcome more than a quarter century of autocratic governance.

During Trump’s first term, his administration centered its criticism of Maduro on the country’s socialist model. In February 2019, weeks after recognizing the opposition leader Juan Guaidó as Venezuela’s interim president, Trump promised in a speech that the “twilight hour of socialism has arrived in our hemisphere.” That campaign to oust Maduro failed, but Trump’s diagnosis of what ails Venezuela was wrong to begin with. Over the past two decades, Venezuela has not been operating primarily under a socialist model but an extractivist one. And since January 3, the United States has seemed only to reinforce it.

Venezuela is an oil state. Under Chavismo, the ideology named for Maduro’s predecessor Hugo Chávez, the state used oil revenues as a political slush fund to reward allies. Little went toward investments in the oil sector or the rest of the economy. Secret deals with oil magnates at home and abroad guided most governance decisions. Whereas the government’s critics faced legal attacks, the well-connected received impunity. Chávez implemented elements of socialism, of course, with massive nationalizations and price controls, but the key priority was collusion and special deals. Many of the state’s allies were poor Venezuelans whose votes kept the autocrats in power, but less noticed were the economic elites. These private actors shunned opposition figures and refrained from any public criticism of the regime; in exchange, they accumulated regulatory favors, access to preferential exchange rates, or exclusive contracts with the state. This model even applied internationally; around 2007, Chávez began redirecting oil sales from the United States to China, both to reward China for being so friendly toward the regime and to preempt a possible U.S. divestment from the Venezuelan oil sector. Oil booms in the first decade of the century allowed the country’s populist leaders to build vertical patronage systems that rewarded loyalty and starved out dissent.

Eventually, however, this system ran its course, leaving Venezuela’s oil industry in shambles. In the 1990s, before Chávez took power, oil production hovered around 3.5 million barrels per day; by 2019, oil production had dropped to 700,000 to 800,000 barrels per day. The government has recognized this dismal state; acting President Delcy Rodríguez has said that the oil sector will need as much as $76 billion in external capital to recover over the next five years.

The United States is a plausible partner for Venezuela’s oil recovery. Washington is responsive to high domestic and global demand for oil, especially since the war in Iran has disrupted flows from the Middle East. American oil firms have the resources to rebuild Venezuelan production and, just as important, the refineries needed to process Venezuelan oil, which is among the world’s heaviest and most sulfurous.

But rather than replacing, or at least revamping, this Chavista system, the United States seems to have embraced its structure. In August, the U.S. government struck an agreement with North American Blue Energy Partners (NABEP), a private Venezuelan corporation, that Trump called “the biggest oil deal in the history of the world.” According to the White House fact sheet of the deal, the Venezuelan state granted NABEP a 100-year concession to operate 17 oil fields, which could cover one-fifth of Venezuela’s total proven reserves. The company is led by Alejandro Betancourt, a Venezuelan citizen being investigated in Spain and Switzerland for money laundering and widely regarded as having collaborated with Chávez and acting as an intermediary between Trump and Rodríguez. Meanwhile, the agreement gives the U.S. Defense Department a 35 percent stake in NABEP, and the State Department gets the right to buy 20 percent of NABEP’s oil at the cost of production—meaning NABEP makes no profit on that share—and has preferential access, at market prices, to the rest.

Outside of these details, little is known about the arrangement or how it came to be. There was no competitive public bidding on the fields—a practice that is generally recommended every time governments assign contracts to private and foreign actors—and there was no explanation of how Washington will manage the export proceeds or clarity on who will fund the necessary investments. The U.S. Congress has not reviewed the deal, although Democrats, with a new majority starting in January, have promised to investigate it.

This deal is also wildly unpopular across Venezuela’s political spectrum. Although Venezuelans have generally celebrated the return of U.S. capital to the country, nearly 80 percent of Venezuelans did not support the oil deal, according to a poll released in September by Meganálisis. Even Machado, who rarely criticizes the United States, has expressed reservations, saying that Betancourt has been responsible for “obscene corruption.” Hardcore leftists see the deal as capitalist robbery. Nationalists see it as neoimperialism. Democratic forces see it as a bailout for the dictatorship. Ordinary citizens see it as a missed opportunity to spread Venezuela’s wealth more equitably.

These oil and gas deals could generate an oil boom if investment comes quickly, production capacity is restored, competitive bidding expands, and government spending and welfare institutions are reformed. But as Trump said when celebrating the deal at the United Nations in September, “To the victor belong the spoils,” and the victors, in this case, are Washington, the ruling party, and a private businessman.

The Venezuelan state has also shown little interest in helping the Venezuelan people and directing petrodollars toward human capital or public welfare. Instead, it will likely do what Chavismo perfected in previous booms: bankroll patronage networks, buy off corporate allies, deny funds to competitors, and spend on electoral campaigns. These new deals could very well end up financing the ruling party’s political rebirth.

The rest of the Venezuelan economy’s recovery, meanwhile, remains a mystery. There have been no efforts toward establishing universal property rights, modernizing contract law, reforming the financial sector, or dismantling state monopolies. Without such inclusive economic rights and democratic rules, oil windfalls seldom produce equitable growth. They produce skewed gains for the state and its friends.

The White House sees the deal as locking Venezuela into a secure economic relationship with the United States for at least 100 years. This week, he referred to the country as “our new acquisition.” But this might be a false sense of security. The agreement is so distasteful to so many Venezuelans that, rather than inoculate Venezuela against anti-imperialist socialism, it is making a radical backlash more likely.

Almost a year after Maduro’s departure, very few concrete steps have been taken to organize a free and fair election in Venezuela. Instead, the Rodríguez government and an opposition delegation led by Dinorah Figuera, who is president of Venezuela’s exiled National Assembly in Spain, held a series of talks this fall. The parties did make progress, including agreeing to unblock certain banned websites and to allow the government to reclaim sanctioned gold reserves held by the Bank of England. Most significantly, after months of pressure from the Venezuelan opposition, the Venezuelan government agreed in August to replace all 32 judges in the Supreme Tribunal of Justice, accepting nominations from civil society that included the opposition. This is a gigantic baby step. In Venezuela, the Supreme Tribunal of Justice long served at the center of Venezuela’s famous autocratic legalism. It was the regime’s preeminent weapon to disqualify opposition candidates and elected officials, accepting laws that justified repression, refusing to investigate extrajudicial killings, and persecuting opposition figures on fabricated charges. An independent judiciary could invalidate current laws that criminalize dissent and establish new ones to promote fair electoral rules. Establishing even a modest framework for an independent judiciary is indispensable for any transition to minimal democracy.

And yet committing to reform the judiciary, however important, is not enough. A full transition to democracy will require far more: a new constitution with robust institutional checks and balances, new laws promoting civil liberties, and more oversight of intelligence and security organizations, which have been known to engage in arbitrary detentions and torture. This will also require retraining the law and order apparatus, namely the National Police and the military’s internal security forces, the Bolivarian National Guard, to respect civil rights; redesigning the electoral system and naming independent electoral authorities; and rebuilding civil society and a free press. Judicial reform in isolation will not dismantle an entrenched autocratic regime.

This week, Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth announced an expansion of U.S.-Venezuelan military cooperation, mostly targeted at fighting crime. But fighting crime effectively does not necessarily require more brute force but rather good intelligence, a robust criminal justice system, a professional and law-abiding police force, and investments in community building, namely in strengthening ties of trust between neighbors and police. It is not clear that these elements will be part of this new U.S.-Venezuelan security cooperation. Unless new agreements come with commitments for internal state reform, the United States risks helping a dictatorship acquire even more coercive power.

Most important, the transition process has ignored the will of the people. Most Venezuelans want a fair election, plain and simple. In a Meganálisis poll conducted in April, 87 percent of respondents agreed that the “solution for Venezuela is a presidential election in 2026.” Even with so much material deprivation—inflation is in the triple digits, and close to 70 percent of the population still lives in poverty—the people want to vote first.

Rubio and Rodríguez have argued that stabilization is necessary before elections can be held. But there is reason to believe that elections are not the result of stability but the source of stability. Elections produce legitimacy for authorities. The current authorities not only aided the suppression of the last election, in 2024, but they have also passed the limit of 180 days that an interim government can serve, according to Venezuela’s constitution. Any policy that it advances is, thus, also less legitimate than those put in place by an elected government.

Moreover, Machado’s absence from the process so far risks making any major deal between the government and the opposition fragile. She is the most popular politician in the country and the leader of the largest pro-democracy movement that Latin America has seen since the first decade of this century. Across multiple polls, including by Meganálisis and AtlasIntel, she has approval ratings between 57 and 76, compared with Rodríguez’s single digits, and multiple international accounts have shown that her substitute in the 2024 election, Edmundo González, easily won. This popularity makes Machado a threat to the Chavista regime, and her exclusion likely came at least in part as a request by the Rodríguez government.

Rather than inaugurating a democratic and economic renaissance, the United States is potentially enabling the survival of a rentist regime. During the oil boom of the first two decades of this century, the Chavista regime insulated itself from domestic accountability by exchanging oil rents for votes and elite support. China served as its primary geopolitical patron, unconcerned with accountability so long as the crude flowed eastward. The United States’ playbook today eerily imitates this model. Venezuela’s main external partner has now changed, but that partner has similar functions. By prioritizing narrow resource extraction over deep institutional reform, the United States has opted to become the senior partner in a rentier compact. Washington is Venezuela’s new toll collector.

Thus far, Venezuela’s democratic transition has delivered only a commitment to reform the Supreme Tribunal of Justice. Nearly a year after Maduro’s ousting, there is no election calendar, no clear plan to reform the electoral system, and no meaningful internal changes to how the government generates and spends its revenues. Despite all the leverage Trump claims to have over the Venezuelan government, outside of business deals, he has very little to show for it.

If this trajectory persists, the United States will not propel a transition to democracy, market principles, and the rule of law in Venezuela. It will instead re-create the very conditions that produced Venezuela’s autocracy in the first place.
