#5901. German Culture Could Soon Be Officially Anti-German

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Dispatch: German Culture Could Soon Be Officially Anti-German

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

In the small eastern German city of Dessau, if the far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD) party has its way, a century-old vision for the future may soon belong to the past.

A short walk away from the main train station, past typical German architecture of beige stucco and pitched, red-tiled roofs, flat-roofed, modernist structures of concrete and glass appear, leading to a building with “BAUHAUS” spelled down one gray side. Designed by pioneering modernist architect Walter Gropius, the building in Dessau opened as a school for architecture, craft, and industrial design on Dec. 4, 1926—one with ambitions to create a new aesthetic for the modern world. With its steel-framed windows and a bright red entrance door, the Bauhaus school building is an iconic symbol of early 20th-century modernism.

In the small eastern German city of Dessau, if the far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD) party has its way, a century-old vision for the future may soon belong to the past.

A short walk away from the main train station, past typical German architecture of beige stucco and pitched, red-tiled roofs, flat-roofed, modernist structures of concrete and glass appear, leading to a building with “BAUHAUS” spelled down one gray side. Designed by pioneering modernist architect Walter Gropius, the building in Dessau opened as a school for architecture, craft, and industrial design on Dec. 4, 1926—one with ambitions to create a new aesthetic for the modern world. With its steel-framed windows and a bright red entrance door, the Bauhaus school building is an iconic symbol of early 20th-century modernism.

It’s also a symbol of Nazi persecution. In 1932, the school closed under pressure from state Nazi officials that denounced the Bauhaus as un-German, internationalist, and a hotbed of “cultural Bolshevism.” (It briefly operated in Berlin, until it fell to Nazi pressure there, too.) Many of its members fled the Nazi regime and are now considered some of the last century’s most influential luminaries of art, architecture, and design.

Nazis burn anti-German literature in Berlin on May 11, 1933.

Clear, simple, and functional, Bauhaus’s influence has been a key German cultural export for more than a century. Yet Hans-Thomas Tillschneider, a culture policy spokesman for the AfD, considers it an “Irrweg der Moderne”—“a wrong turn of modernity.” For years, he has targeted the Bauhaus, arguing that it stands for “deracination” and “dislocation of architecture” in favor of a “globalist” architectural vernacular. The Bauhaus was not only an aesthetic movement, he suggests, but a deeply political—and dangerously anti-German—project that must be combatted for the sake of German identity today.

In September, the AfD swept the state elections of Saxony Anhalt, where Dessau is located, which means that Tillschneider—who’s been a member of Saxony Anhalt’s parliament for a decade—may soon be in a position to realize his ambitions for recasting German culture. And those ambitions extend far beyond the Bauhaus.

The AfD’s electoral victory put it within reach of forming Germany’s first far-right state government since World War II, with the power to determine educational and cultural policies for the region. Right now, its 39 seats in the 83-member parliament leave it three votes short of an absolute majority (although other parties have ruled out a formal coalition). The party is hoping its candidate for state premier, Ulrich Siegmund, will be officially confirmed by the state legislature in early December.

If the AfD successfully forms a government, there may be significant cuts to funding for a host of cultural institutions in the state, including the Bauhaus Dessau Foundation, which operates in the school’s original building (which is now mostly converted into a museum). The AfD has not only positioned itself as the hardline party on immigration, but more broadly as a party seeking to culturally reform Germany. And, for the AfD, broad swathes of what has long been recognized as German culture are simply “un-German.”

For a party rooted in the former East, much of the offending inheritance belongs to the postwar West. Germany’s self-image as an internationalist, socially liberal state and leader by example in reckoning with its Nazi past have defined reunified Germany. The AfD, by contrast, wants to treat Nazi Germany as a historical aberration. In this sense, the AfD’s cultural policies are not only restorative of neglected aspects of German history, as it likes to claim, but a revisionist effort to redefine substantial parts of the country’s recent past.

Asked in an email which major German cultural figures belonged in the national canon, Tillschneider had a ready taxonomy. Thomas Mann, the Nobel laureate who became a prominent opponent of Hitler from exile, yes. But Günter Grass and Heinrich Böll, both Nobel-winning novelists whose work helped shape West Germany’s critical examination of its past and present, would be forgotten along with “the whole postwar lot.”

German author Thomas Mann at his home in New York City in January 1943.Fred Stein Archive/Getty Images

He similarly dismissed much of the work of Rainer Werner Fassbinder—a leading figure in New German Cinema whose films examined the cruelties and compromises beneath postwar prosperity—as bound to its time. Leni Riefenstahl, the filmmaker who notoriously made propaganda films for the Nazi regime, received a more favorable assessment. The continuing interest in her work outside official channels, he argued, suggested that the filmmaker’s innovations would endure.

For Tillschneider, the distinction was not simply between artists who had opposed Nazism and those who had accommodated or served it. It concerned what their work could offer Germans now. “We don’t want to ban it,” he said of art he considered anti-German. “But art that turns against Germany cannot claim a right to continued support from the German taxpayer.”

Tillschneider’s hostility to modernism is not confined to Dessau. Berlin’s Neue Nationalgalerie, designed by the Bauhaus’s last director, Ludwig Mies van der Rohe, and opened in West Berlin in 1968, is “a hideous lump,” he said. The government quarter inspired a more physical response: “I regularly feel like throwing up when I walk behind the Reichstag and see those bland cubes of steel, glass, and concrete,” he wrote.

The Neue Nationalgalerie, a museum of 20th-century art, in Berlin on Aug. 29, 1969. Hedrich Blessing Collection/Getty Images

While these judgments represent the personal preferences of one far-right politician, they may also soon determine how government money is spent. This is of outsized importance in Germany, where cultural infrastructure is in large part sustained by public funding.

Since the September state election, the AfD has already sought substantial cuts to theater funding. In October, its city council faction proposed plans to reduce Theatre Magdeburg’s annual municipal subsidy by up to 7 million euros.

While the party presented the proposal as a response to the city’s financial difficulties, in the months preceding the election, the theater’s general director, Julien Chavaz, publicly rejected the idea that theater should enact a conservative program and warned that to attack theater is itself “anti-German.” The city council rejected the AfD’s motion in November, signaling the resistance the AfD’s cultural policies still face.

Tillschneider’s arguments echo a broader AfD campaign against the place of national self-criticism in German public life. In a speech in 2017, the AfD’s hard-right ideological standard-bearer, Björn Höcke, demanded a “180-degree turn in our policy of memory.” He called the monument to murdered Jews in Berlin a “monument of shame,” and attacked the framing of Germany’s defeat in World War II as liberation from Nazism.

Read More A supporter of the far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD) holds up a placard that shows AfD lead candidate Ulrich Siegmund just before initial results showed the party with 44 percent of the vote in Saxony-Anhalt state elections, in Magdeburg, Germany, on Sept. 6. A supporter of the far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD) holds up a placard that shows AfD lead candidate Ulrich Siegmund just before initial results showed the party with 44 percent of the vote in Saxony-Anhalt state elections, in Magdeburg, Germany, on Sept. 6. Why AfD Appeals to So Many Germans The far-right party surges in a regional election. Insider | Cameron Abadi, Adam Tooze

The far-right party surges in a regional election.

In 2023, AfD cultural spokesman Marc Jongen argued in the federal legislature that Germany increasingly reduced being German to “an exclusively negative self-image” and his faction proposed reorienting cultural policy toward “positive” sources of national identity. Tillschneider gives that ambition specific cultural targets, identifying the writers, institutions, and forms of remembrance he believes should lose public support or prominence: “We must finally arrive at an identity unburdened by the Nazi era,” he wrote.

And Tillschneider—who could have a say in setting cultural and educational policy in any AfD-led government in Saxony-Anhalt—is happy to provide reading lists to achieve that goal. In our email exchange, he called for state assistance for contemporary writers such as Jörg Bernig and Uwe Tellkamp. Bernig, an award-winning poet and novelist, and Tellkamp, whose novel The Tower won the German Book Prize, have become controversial for their criticism of immigration policy and allegations that German cultural life marginalizes conservative views.

He recommended renewed attention to Martin Heidegger, the influential philosopher who joined the Nazi Party and whose writings contain antisemitic passages, and praised projects that adapted German poetry and folk songs for contemporary audiences.

Tillschneider also gave Ernst Jünger, the author of the World War I memoir Storm of Steel and a leading voice of radical nationalism during the Weimar Republic, a stronger claim to the canon than either Grass or Böll, saying his work should be read in schools and he should be publicly commemorated. (Jünger himself refused to join the Nazi Party.)

German author Ernst Jünger at his home in Wilflingen, Germany, in February 1974.Sven Simon Archives/Getty Images

His recommendations were notably not exclusively literary; he praised a version of Goethe’s “Heidenröslein” set to a techno beat.

Asked whether the postwar tradition of national self-criticism was itself part of German culture, Tillschneider acknowledged that it was. But its German origins did not establish its value. “This tradition of self-criticism is, of course, German,” he wrote. “The question is whether it ought to belong to Germany, whether we can make use of it to sustain our identity.”

That position helps explain his dismissal of much of Germany’s postwar cultural inheritance. The capacity to question Germany, including the incongruities between its democratic present and its authoritarian past, has itself traditionally been understood as a national achievement of modern German culture. What Tillschneider considers exhausted or destructive in Germany’s culture of self-examination could equally be understood as one of postwar Germany’s enduring cultural accomplishments.

As for National Socialism, Tillschneider said it should continue to be studied in schools, universities, memorial sites, and among research institutions. But it should become a historical subject, rather than a tool for interpreting the present or future. The reality of National Socialism, he argued, should leave older German traditions untainted by association.

Tillschneider treats his own tastes as expressions of national identity, while treating art that challenges that identity as political. He’s quick to describe his aesthetic preferences as a neutral standard; departures from them become ideology. In our email exchange, he called for public funding to support predominantly “pure,” apolitical art. But greater attention to nationalist writers, or to art organized around German identity, did not strike him as a departure from that principle. “National identity is not a political subject,” he wrote.

For Barbara Steiner, director of the Bauhaus Dessau Foundation, the AfD’s political accusations against the Bauhaus today, like those of the Nazis 100 years before, are fundamentally mistaken. She emphasized that, for all its modernist ambitions, the Bauhaus had never been politically uniform, much less stridently anti-German.

During a visit to the museum in early October, I struggled to find the radicalism that so offends the AfD. The exhibitions I saw foregrounded materials and production, giving an institution that had sought to transform everyday life a surprisingly subdued presentation. The Bauhaus, however, remains indelibly political to Tillschneider. It should, he argued, have developed traditional building practices to meet new human needs instead of seeking a rupture with the past.

But according to Steiner, the Bauhaus Museum and foundation is an authentic expression of 20th-century German history. “I would say the Bauhaus is German and not German at the same time. It’s both,” Steiner said. “Without the Weimar Republic, there would have been no Bauhaus. Without [East Germany], it would not have been reopened.”

Moreover, Steiner insists, the movement is deeply rooted in German tradition at the local, national, and international level. “The Bauhaus had—and still has—a great many connections to Dessau, to the region, but also to the world,” she said. “I cannot separate them.”

This post appeared in the FP Weekend newsletter, a weekly showcase of book reviews, deep dives, and features. Sign up here.

Carol Schaeffer is a writer and journalist based in Berlin.

#5900. Congress and the Coming Crises

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

IVO H. DAALDER is Senior Fellow at Harvard’s Belfer Center for Science and International Affairs. He served as U.S. Ambassador to NATO from 2009 to 2013.

JAMES M. LINDSAY is Mary and David Boies Distinguished Senior Fellow in U.S. Foreign Policy at the Council on Foreign Relations.

How to Defuse Looming Threats to U.S. National Security

Democrats won a historic victory in the U.S. midterm elections by running on a key voter concern: affordability. They promised to lower gas and diesel prices, curb electricity and water bills, and cut borrowing costs. Now voters expect results.

Making progress will not be easy, given that the affordability crisis is inextricably linked to a set of thorny policy issues. Soaring gas and diesel prices stem from wars in Europe and the Middle East that have overextended the United States and provided China, Iran, North Korea, and Russia openings to exploit. Rising electricity and water bills are driven by the growing number of data centers powering an artificial intelligence revolution that is nearing a point of no return, when machines improve faster than humans can understand or control. And higher prices and borrowing costs are the result of an unsustainable U.S. national debt, now above $40 trillion, that requires exorbitant interest payments and has foreign governments questioning the safety of the dollar and U.S. Treasury bonds.

These problems not only strain Americans’ pocketbooks but also threaten the security and prosperity of the United States—and the world. If Beijing and Moscow try to take advantage of a weakened United States by escalating simultaneously in Taiwan and eastern Europe, those moves could be the first steps toward World War III. If AI escapes the bounds of human control, no one will be safe. And if the national debt continues to soar, Washington could trigger a fiscal crisis that devastates the American economy and leaves the United States far less able to meet the challenges it faces overseas.

Action to address the United States’ problems therefore cannot wait. But politics may stand in the way. The White House is more likely to obstruct than to cooperate with congressional Democrats over the next two years, as President Donald Trump’s appointment of the pugilistic Stephen Miller as the new White House chief of staff underscores. Moreover, one electoral victory did not erase the country’s deep partisan and policy divisions. Going into the 2028 presidential election, Republicans know that a successful Democratic Congress would endanger their chances of keeping the White House. Trump and congressional Republicans, therefore, have considerable motivation to derail Democratic plans.

Democrats are not without options, however. They can pursue legislation, seeking cross-party collaboration when possible and pushing ahead even in the face of presidential vetoes. Sometimes it is the president, not Congress, who blinks. When he doesn’t, voters will know who stood in the way. Democrats can also use public hearings and the investigative powers of Congress to shed light on the threats facing the country and to build a case for change—much as the Fulbright hearings did during the Vietnam War and the Church Committee did to highlight Cold War–era intelligence abuses. They can put the defense secretary and military commanders, AI executives, and Treasury and budget officials under oath to account for how they prosecuted the Iran war, raced ahead on AI without adequate safeguards, and exploded the deficit. They can also call on economists, scientists, and strategists to explain how to steer the country away from peril.

Congressional efforts may not be enough to meaningfully change Trump’s policies. But the risks of major war, unconstrained AI, and eroding economic competitiveness are too great to wait two more years to address. These problems, and the affordability crisis they have generated, will only get worse. If Democrats in Congress do not start advancing a serious agenda to address them now, these dangers may become impossible to contain.

The first order of the new Congress’s business should be to end the Iran war. Operation Epic Fury has sent energy prices soaring and put freedom of navigation at risk. It has also highlighted the United States’ vulnerabilities in ways that could prove dangerous—and costly—in the years ahead. Iranian missiles and drones wreaked havoc on American military bases in the Persian Gulf. U.S. efforts to defeat these attacks depleted supplies of critical munitions, showing that the U.S. military has become overly reliant on very expensive, hard-to-produce high-tech weapons. The economic, diplomatic, military, and intelligence support that China and Russia have provided Iran revealed the extent to which autocracies are cooperating to stretch U.S. capabilities and undermine American power. The U.S. Navy is effectively bottled up in the Arabian Sea, limiting its presence in the Atlantic and Pacific. In theory, U.S. allies could help fill the gaps, but Trump’s decision to launch the war without consulting them and his adversarial attitude toward them have strained those relationships, weakening deterrence of a Russia and China bent on securing strategic advantage.

Perceptions of weakness, even when they are wrong, are often why wars begin. Moscow and Beijing might see the next two years as a window of opportunity. Europe and Japan are rearming, but that buildup will take years. The American military is stretched thin, and political divisions make decisive action difficult. Together this could be an invitation for U.S. adversaries to engage in aggression in Europe and Asia. Russia is already pursuing a shadow war against NATO and could test the alliance’s unity further by probing the Baltic states. China could impose a blockade on Taiwan. It is not difficult to see how, if Moscow and Beijing take these steps in concert, the resulting conflicts could escalate into a global war.

The new Congress will have limited ability to forestall these events. The U.S. Constitution puts much of diplomacy beyond the reach of the legislature; only the president decides when to negotiate, what to discuss, and with whom. But Congress does have tools to wield. If the Trump administration remains in a standoff with the Iranian regime when Democrats take control in January, Congress should immediately invoke the War Powers Resolution to pass binding legislation demanding that Trump either secure congressional authorization for the war or end it. Congress can also use its power of the purse to condition or halt further spending on the war, preferably as part of must-pass legislation, such as a defense appropriations bill, to avoid a potential Senate filibuster. Trump would likely veto any such legislation, and Congress has a poor record on overriding presidential vetoes. But challenging a war that Americans clearly oppose would fulfill the promise Democrats made on the campaign trail, reduce the likelihood that Trump would escalate in dangerous ways, and mark a step forward in Congress’s reclamation of its war powers.

There is more the Democratic Congress can do to lower the risks of global conflict, too. It can start by taking a stronger line on Russia. Moscow has repaid Trump’s conciliatory policies—including his waiving of sanctions Congress authorized earlier this year with the bipartisan Lindsay O. Graham Sanctioning Russia and Iran Act—with aid to Tehran and an intensified war on Ukraine. Only greater pressure can force the Kremlin to stand down. Congress should amend the Graham Act to give itself the right to review and overturn any presidential waivers of sanctions mandated under the law. It should also reaffirm the current ban on energy purchases from Russia and prohibit the administration from using government funds to follow through on its proposal to restart Russian gas and oil exports to Europe through a U.S. intermediary. Congress should resume military aid to Ukraine, ensure the availability of Starshield communications to Ukrainian forces, and refuse to fund any reduction in the permanent U.S. military presence in Europe or Asia that it has not approved itself.

Finally, to help dissuade China from threatening Taiwan, Congress should use defense spending bills to speed up the sale of arms to Taiwan. And Congress should legally codify the 1982 Six Assurances, which include a promise by Washington not to consult Beijing on arms sales to Taiwan. Many of these measures could garner sufficient Republican support to pass with veto-proof majorities, forcing Trump’s hand.

Additionally, Congress should use its oversight powers to assess the vulnerabilities that the Iran war has revealed. It should evaluate whether the U.S. intelligence agencies and the Pentagon adequately anticipated Iran’s response to U.S. attacks, the scale and causes of the shortages of high-tech weapons, and the risks that AI-aided drone warfare poses to U.S. overseas bases. Congress can also review the extent of Chinese and Russian support for Iran; assess NATO’s defenses against Russia, including its response to the escalating shadow war; determine U.S. readiness for Chinese efforts to coerce Taiwan; and evaluate proposals to make Pentagon acquisition more flexible and less expensive. An essential step toward enhancing U.S. deterrence is to identify what is broken in the U.S. defense posture and what needs to be fixed.

The new Congress will also need to rein in the AI industry. Opposition to AI data centers and other infrastructure galvanized voters in the midterms. Across the country, 46 of 47 ballot measures passed, placing new restrictions on new data centers, which have brought rising power and water bills and explosive land costs. Many Americans fear what AI will do to jobs today and for the next generation. They distrust the tech executives who control it and question who will truly benefit from its development.

Those concerns are justified. AI is advancing with increasing speed and introducing unknown risks. Five months ago, AI generated one percent of Anthropic’s research and development; today, it accounts for more than a quarter. Lab leaders predict that AI will become self-generating within a year. That is a scary prospect, not only for displaced computer scientists but also for society at large. AI could pose an existential threat to global computer networks; rogue AI agents have already colluded to evade human control. Leading firms have warned of even greater dangers. Alphabet said recently that its products could lead to “severe or catastrophic security and societal incidents,” and Anthropic has cited “catastrophic or existential risks to humanity.”

AI also poses profound national security challenges for the United States. The Trump administration and the tech industry have invoked an innovation race with China as a reason not to regulate the technology. That race is real, but many of the most important stages are not the ones Washington is fixating on. They are also ones Beijing is winning. Perhaps U.S. firms will be the first to develop artificial general intelligence. But in the meantime, the rest of the world and many U.S. companies are adopting Chinese open-weight AI models. Alibaba’s Qwen model now accounts for more than half of all open-source model downloads worldwide. As cheap, easy-to-adapt Chinese models become the global default, Beijing gains leverage over the digital infrastructure on which other countries’ economies and militaries will run. The companies that build these models are legally bound to cooperate with Chinese intelligence services. And because these models spread with few safeguards, they are easier to turn to malicious use.

Meanwhile, AI is transforming war. Autonomous weapons are essential to Ukraine’s defense against a superior military, shooting down long-range drones and targeting Russian forces, supply lines, and strategic industries. This year, the Pentagon quietly revised its doctrine to let AI select and initiate strikes while humans merely monitor, which risks errors and escalation at a speed no human can catch. And as AI lowers the barriers to entry for launching cyberattacks and developing new biological weapons, every country is in danger.

The Trump administration is clearly reluctant to address any of these threats. It is beholden to figures such as Jensen Huang, Elon Musk, and Mark Zuckerburg who have wanted to keep the AI industry unregulated and unconstrained. Huang and Nvidia, the company he led, had long pushed the administration to relax U.S. export controls that restricted the sale of AI chips to China and other countries; the firm’s current scandal has revealed just how loose those restrictions had become. Now, the administration wants China to report serious AI-related incidents through a new bilateral channel yet opposes requiring American companies to do the same. Given how rapidly AI is advancing, Congress cannot afford to stand idly by. There is a slew of legislative proposals already circulating, many of them with bipartisan support, that would require incident reporting, auditors in AI labs, and mandatory safety testing before deployment. Other AI industry leaders, such as Anthropic’s Dario Amodei and Alphabet’s Demis Hassabis, have supported these ideas, but the Trump administration has ignored them, asking instead for the industry to police itself.

Finding the right regulations and safety mechanisms, and then codifying them in legislation, will take time. In the meantime, Congress should use its convening power to seek input from industry leaders, experts, and the public about how this technology can be controlled. The leaders of frontier AI labs should be asked, under oath, to explain not just the upside of AI but also its risks. Elon Musk, for example, told The Economist this summer that within ten years, work will be “optional” because of AI—but he has also said there is a ten to 20 percent chance that AI will lead to the extinction of humanity. He and others who share this belief should be forced to justify why this risk is acceptable and why tech leaders are the ones to decide on behalf of humanity. They should also explain what it would take to reduce the ten to 20 percent chance to zero.

Public hearings that expose both the dangers and the opportunities of AI will make it easier to galvanize action to harness the technology’s promise safely while preventing the worst possible outcomes. And Congress has some levers it can pull now to address threats to national security. It can use the defense authorization bill to require that humans retain meaningful control over lethal force and to demand an accounting of how AI is reshaping military doctrine. It can also fund efforts to make American models, rather than Chinese ones, the default choice for countries around the world. If legislation takes too long to pass, hearings can at least lay the groundwork for a future administration to act.

Finally, Congress must address threats to the United States’ economic power. With $40 trillion in public debt, an annual budget deficit of $2 trillion, and annual interest payments topping more than $1 trillion, the U.S. economy is living on borrowed time. China and Russia are working to “de-dollarize” their economies by encouraging the use of cryptocurrency and other means of settling international transactions and by creating alternatives to the U.S.-dominated global banking system.

Even U.S. allies are reducing their reliance on that system: Norway’s sovereign wealth fund has cut its holding of U.S. dollars, the Dutch and French central banks have pulled their gold holdings from New York, and European Central Bank President Christine Lagarde has called for a “global euro” that would serve as an alternative to the dollar. If the dollar were to lose its status as the world’s premier reserve currency, Washington would be forced to spend even more money servicing the debt than it does now, intensifying the country’s fiscal problems, slowing economic growth while driving up inflation, and diminishing its ability to use economic and financial sanctions to pressure adversaries.

An immediate objective for the Democratic Congress will be to rein in Trump’s tariffs, because they raise prices, unsettle the markets that finance U.S. debt, and make U.S. producers less competitive globally without making a meaningful dent in the deficit. Congress will be operating from a disadvantage. Over the past century, it has delegated to the White House considerable tariff-setting authority, which Trump has eagerly exercised. Clawing back that power will be difficult. Significant disagreement exists among Democrats and between Democrats and Republicans about how to rewrite tariff law, and Trump will likely fight to defend his favorite foreign policy tool. Yet one area where concerted congressional action might be possible is in walking back Trump’s unilateral tariffs on Canada, which not only run counter to the U.S.-Mexico-Canada trade agreement that Trump negotiated and Congress approved in his first term but also hurt Republican voters in Maine and across the Midwest. Congress can aim to repeal Section 338 of the Tariff Act of 1930, which allows the president to impose tariffs of up to 50 percent on countries that treat U.S. goods differently from those from other countries. The provision was a forgotten relic until Trump invoked it in August to impose a fresh round of tariffs on Canada. And legislators should narrow the reach of other presidential tariff authorities by mandating expedited congressional review, instituting joint resolutions of disapproval that cannot be filibustered, and adding sunset provisions to emergency tariff authorities so that the president needs congressional approval to keep them in place.

Tariffs, however, are only part of the problem. A comprehensive solution to the country’s fiscal troubles would require raising taxes or cutting entitlements, which neither party has been willing to do. But Democrats have other cards they can play to start undoing the damage of the past two years. Trump’s 2025 One Big Beautiful Bill Act will cost the Treasury some $4.5 trillion over the next decade; Congress should repeal the law’s tax breaks for corporations and the wealthiest Americans and restore the Internal Revenue Service’s ability to collect taxes that are already owed. It should also write the Federal Reserve’s independence into law, so that no president can force interest rates down to make government borrowing cheaper. And Congress should establish a bipartisan commission modeled on the 1981 Greenspan Commission, which resolved a Social Security funding crisis, to recommend steps the United States must take to put its finances on a sustainable path and to ensure its global economic competitiveness.

In his 1948 campaign, Harry Truman salvaged his presidency by running against what he called the “do-nothing” Congress. That history should be a warning to Democrats. Voters handed them a congressional majority to bring down costs, and they must start right away to address the forces driving those costs—wars stretching American power to the breaking point, an AI revolution racing beyond human control, and a fiscal hole eroding the foundations of U.S. economic power. If the Democratic majority spends the next two years relitigating the administration’s misdeeds but failing to make progress on the issue voters care most about, Trump and the next Republican presidential candidate will be happy to brand it the do-nothing Congress of 2028.

Passing bills that can become law, forcing the president to veto those that cannot, and using hearings to show Americans what is at stake may still fall short. Ending and averting war, regulating a powerful tech industry, and protecting the United States’ economic competitiveness are difficult for Congress to do without presidential cooperation. But two years without any progress would only leave the affordability problem in place and the United States in a more dangerous global position than it is in now. A Congress that confronts these problems directly can at least make headway. And if it does its job right, it will hand the next president an agenda equal to the moment.

#5899. Tariff Man Strikes Again

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

World Brief: Tariff Man Strikes Again

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at new proposed U.S. tariffs on China and other trade partners, tensions between Ukraine and Libya over a drone attack, and the end of a long deportation battle in the United States.

World Brief will be off Thursday and Friday for the U.S. Thanksgiving holiday. We will return on Monday, Nov. 30.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at new proposed U.S. tariffs on China and other trade partners, tensions between Ukraine and Libya over a drone attack, and the end of a long deportation battle in the United States.

World Brief will be off Thursday and Friday for the U.S. Thanksgiving holiday. We will return on Monday, Nov. 30.

The Trump administration on Wednesday proposed a fresh batch of tariffs on more than a dozen major trading partners, including China, Japan, South Korea, and the European Union.

The proposed tariff rates, which could be implemented early next year, range from 15 percent for the EU, Taiwan, and South Korea, 18 percent for most others, and rates of as much as 25 percent on India or 35 percent on Vietnam. China faces a fresh tariff rate of 20 percent, which will be stacked on top of duties still lingering from U.S. President Donald Trump’s first term—and that’s only if the trade truce between Beijing and Washington holds. (It expires in January.)

The latest tariffs are the culmination of an investigation launched this spring into alleged excess industrial capacity among nearly all U.S. trading partners. U.S. trade officials allege that other countries artificially prop up their manufacturing sectors with subsidies, promotions, tax breaks, and other measures that distort trade and disadvantage U.S. exports. U.S. President Donald Trump, meanwhile, has taken equity stakes in dozens of private companies and is actively promoting state involvement in the manufacture of everything from chips to ships.

China’s Foreign Ministry said the new tariffs are based on a “false proposition,” though China is one of the few countries among the 14 economies targeted that demonstrates genuine excess capacity that is harming the U.S. and global economies. Even trade specialists who are skeptical of the Trump administration’s affinity for tariffs believe that higher duties on Chinese exports are warranted.

The European Commission also hit back at the tariffs, arguing that Europe is a victim of overcapacity, not a source of the problem.

The Trump administration is using Section 301 of the 1974 Trade Act to levy the new duties—the same law that it used earlier this year to slap tariffs on 60 economies on the grounds that they do not adequately police forced labor in their supply chains. (Those tariffs were challenged in court.) The proposed new tariffs would essentially replace (and slightly raise) the rates levied earlier, implying that the question of forced labor has been solved in just a few months.

The measures proposed on Wednesday are just the latest attempt by the Trump administration to find a formula to recreate in the aggregate the sweeping global tariffs that the U.S. Supreme Court struck down earlier this year. The administration has used at least four different trade authorities dating back almost a century to continue raising barriers to trade, despite polls showing that the widespread unpopularity of tariffs contributed to the Republicans’ historic midterm election wipeout.

Toxic explosives. The U.S. Army on Wednesday blew up nearly five tons of toxic lead explosives at the Iowa Army Ammunition Plant in Middletown, Iowa. The intentional detonation of 68 barrels of lead azide, a sensitive chemical compound used by the military in explosives, was originally scheduled to take place last Friday. The controlled explosion was delayed, however, amid public pushback and legal challenges from environmental groups.

Destroying the bomb-making material, which had been stored in the Iowa plant since 2009, was deemed necessary by the military after inspectors in March discovered that it had partially crystallized—increasing the risk of a spontaneous explosion. But the plan to detonate the material raised concerns over the potential for toxic lead dust to spread over a large area, and many locals also said they weren’t given enough notice to prepare.

Residents within five miles of the blast were urged to evacuate ahead of the detonation, while others in the vicinity were told to remain indoors.

After the detonation, the Environmental Protection Agency wrote on X that its real-time air monitors were reporting “ZERO detection of airborne lead throughout the region.” Adam Telle, the Army’s acting secretary, also wrote on X that the “controlled, mitigated detonation exceeded all expectations and was a HUGE success” and said the threat of an “uncontrolled spontaneous detonation has been eliminated.”

Ukraine and Libya. Kyiv has seemingly angered the primary military force in eastern Libya, the Libyan National Army (LNA), after attacking a Russian warship that was refueling at the Benghazi Naval Base on Sunday. Two Libyan naval officers died from wounds sustained in the Ukrainian sea drone attack. The LNA has vowed to retaliate “at the appropriate time and place, and by the appropriate means.”

Ukraine offered no immediate public response on Wednesday, though officials said the previous day that Ukrainian forces hadn’t targeted Libyans in the operation.

The incident is yet another example of how the Russia-Ukraine war has spread to other parts of the globe. Russian missiles crashed in Poland last week, and there have been repeated Russian drone incursions on NATO’s eastern flank. Ukraine also carried out a drone attack on a Russian cargo ship in the Gulf of Finland this past weekend.

A ‘free man.’ Kilmar Abrego Garcia arrived in Spain on Wednesday as part of a negotiated agreement between the Salvadoran immigrant’s attorneys and the U.S. government after a prolonged, highly publicized deportation battle with the Trump administration. “I’m happy, and I’m celebrating my freedom,” Abrego Garcia told reporters in Spanish after he landed.

Amid the administration’s controversial crackdown on immigration, Abrego Garcia was arrested last year by federal agents in Maryland, wrongfully deported, and placed in a notorious prison in El Salvador before he was ultimately brought back to the United States. The Trump administration continued to target Abrego Garcia for deportation and aimed to send him to Liberia, but that was blocked by the courts.

Simon Sandoval-Moshenberg, an attorney for Abrego Garcia, said that his client left the United States a “free man” and signaled that Abrego Garcia would attempt to return once Trump is no longer in office. “Kilmar Abrego Garcia is going to outlast Donald Trump, and he’ll be back in the United States as a lawful permanent resident,” he said.

On Wednesday, Muhammad Zeeshan Ali became the first Pakistani national to reach orbit and the first foreign astronaut to board China’s Tiangong space station. Ali, who will return to Earth in a few days, told reporters that he carries “the name of Pakistan on my shoulder, the hopes of 250 million Pakistanis.” We officially feel like underachievers.

Keith Johnson is a staff writer at Foreign Policy covering geoeconomics and energy. Bluesky: @kfj-fp.bsky.social X: @KFJ_FP

John Haltiwanger is a staff writer at Foreign Policy. Bluesky: @jchaltiwanger.bsky.social X: @jchaltiwanger

#5898. Ukraine’s Shadow War in Africa

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Africa Brief: Ukraine’s Shadow War in Africa

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Welcome to Foreign Policy’s Africa Brief.

The highlights this week: Ukraine attacks Russian vessels in Libya in “response to Russia’s war,” Gambia wins a landmark case at the International Court of Justice, and African nations respond to a disappointing United Nations climate summit.

Welcome to Foreign Policy’s Africa Brief.

The highlights this week: Ukraine attacks Russian vessels in Libya in “response to Russia’s war,” Gambia wins a landmark case at the International Court of Justice, and African nations respond to a disappointing United Nations climate summit.

Kyiv claimed responsibility on Monday for a recent attack on two Russian vessels off the coast of Libya, including the corvette Merkury, one of Russia’s newest warships. The vessels had been docked at the Benghazi naval base when the attack occurred on Sunday.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky has described the operation as “exemplary” and noted that it was in “response to Russia’s war.”

Yet security experts warn that conducting drone strikes within a region that is not involved in the Russia-Ukraine war could set a dangerous precedent, especially as they fear Africa is becoming a proxy theater in the conflict.

The United Nations mission in Libya, known as UNSMIL, has called for “an immediate, independent and transparent investigation” into the drone strikes. “Libya cannot afford, and must not become, a battleground for foreign conflicts,” UNSMIL wrote on X.

Kyiv has confirmed that seven Ukrainian-made naval drones had been launched from a “mothership” in the region, though it did not clarify how they were transported there. Some unverified Libyan sources have alleged that Tripoli’s U.N.-recognized government may have aided Ukraine, which Tripoli strongly denied.

Both of Libya’s rival administrations—the government in Tripoli, led by Prime Minister Abdul Hamid Dbeibah, and a Russia-backed government based in Benghazi, controlled by Gen. Khalifa Haftar—have denounced the attacks.

Following the 2024 fall of the Assad regime in Syria, where Russia operates a naval base, Moscow has sought alternative military footprints in Africa, including by pursuing airbases in eastern Libya. Syria served as a logistical hub for transferring troops and arms to landlocked Sahel nations, where Russia’s mercenary Africa Corps helps fight jihadists.

With Syria growing closer to the United States and Europe, Libya now serves as that hub, explained Ulf Laessing, the head of the Syria/Iraq program at the Konrad-Adenauer-Stiftung and the author of Understanding Libya Since Gaddafi.

“Ukraine is trying to hit Russia wherever they can. It’s difficult for Ukraine to hit Russian targets where the Africa Corps is based in the Sahel,” Laessing said. “It’s more efficient and easier to do it where Russia is basically bringing goods.”

Zain Hussain, a researcher at the Stockholm International Peace Research Institute, said that “through this attack, Ukraine is showing that it can target Russia’s geopolitical interests as well as fighting a war with Russia at home.”

The strikes come amid increasing concern that the war in Ukraine is spreading to other African countries. Ukrainian drone specialists have been deployed to Libya, Mali, and Sudan, according to a September CNN report. A source also told CNN that troops from Chad, Niger, and Burkina Faso had traveled to Mali to receive drone training from Ukrainian soldiers, though several experts told me that the evidence for this is speculative at best.

Meanwhile, in the Sahel, multiple media reports have accused Ukraine of supporting and training northern Mali’s Tuareg separatists in recent years as a means of wearing down Russian forces there.

Experts suspect Ukrainian drone assistance in the Tuareg ambush that killed dozens of Russian Wagner mercenaries and Malian soldiers in 2024. At the time, Andriy Yusov, a spokesperson for Ukrainian military intelligence, said that the rebels had been given the “necessary” information to carry out the attacks. Yusov’s comments prompted Mali to sever relations with Ukraine, accusing Kyiv of supporting a “barbaric” attack by terrorists.

Analysts also suspected Ukrainian involvement in the brief insurgent takeover of Mali’s strategic town of Kidal earlier this year by Tuareg and al Qaeda-linked jihadists. Ukraine’s Foreign Ministry has officially rejected the allegations.

Ukraine’s latest attacks are unlikely to disrupt eastern Libya’s ties with Russia, Laessing said, especially as Moscow needs “a country on the way to the Sahel.” Still, they add a layer of complexity to long-standing violence and drone warfare in Sahelian nations, said Virginie Baudais, the director of the Stockholm International Peace Research Institute’s Sahel and West Africa program.

As Baudais put it, the “internationalization of the conflict” in the Sahel will only contribute to “the escalation of violence, with the highest cost for civilian populations.”

Monday, Nov. 23, to Friday, Nov. 27: The Africa Centres for Disease Control and Prevention’s public health conference is held in Addis Ababa, Ethiopia.

Friday, Nov. 27: The deadline for councils in South Africa to elect their leaders.

Somalia-UAE rift. Last week, Somalia expelled the United Arab Emirates’ ambassador and recalled its own envoy from Abu Dhabi, citing ​violations of the country’s sovereignty that amounted to an “open invasion.”

The dispute stems from growing Emirati ties with Ethiopia and the Somalian breakaway regions of Somaliland and Puntland. Somali President Hassan Sheikh Mohamud also accused the UAE of having played a role in Israel’s recognition of Somaliland last December in a statement to Somalian state news agency SONNA.

The UAE’s state-owned DP World and its subsidiaries operate ports in those breakaway regions. They have expanded the Berbera port in Somaliland and are developing a road corridor connecting it to landlocked Ethiopia. In January 2024, Addis Ababa signed a memorandum of understanding to eventually recognize Somaliland’s independence in exchange for naval and commercial access to Berbera.

A New York Times report using satellite imagery recently found that the UAE has turned airports in Berbera and Bosaso, a city in Puntland, into military facilities.

In response to Somalia’s latest move, the UAE said its embassy in Somalia had been closed since January, after Mogadishu canceled bilateral agreements, and it strongly denounced the “escalatory and hostile approach” by the Somali government amid the latest diplomatic spat.

ICJ ruling. Gambia, the smallest nation in mainland Africa, won a landmark case last week when the International Court of Justice (ICJ) ruled that Myanmar committed genocide in its 2017 military campaign that killed at least 10,000 members of the Rohingya Muslim minority community and displaced more than 700,000 into neighboring Bangladesh.

A Muslim-majority nation, Gambia brought the case to the court in 2019. Now, the ruling has possible implications for South Africa’s ICJ genocide case against Israel for its operations in Gaza.

Both Myanmar and Israel have defended their actions as counterinsurgency operations. But in its decision, the court considered a pattern of mass killings, the razing of homes, and forced exodus. That finding “gives South Africa more avenues ​to argue for genocide” in Gaza, Israeli legal expert Yuval Shany told Reuters.

COP31’s failures. This year’s U.N. climate summit, known as COP31, in Turkey ended on Sunday without an agreement after references to phasing out fossil fuels were removed from the draft text. Despite being the most attended COP to date, some observers have called it the “worst” one ever.

At the summit, African delegations voiced discontent with financial commitments for climate adaptation. Wealthier nations pledged only $50 million in total to the climate adaptation fund, far short of the $300 million annual target. They also largely offered loans rather than grants. The United States, the United Kingdom, Canada, France, Italy, and Australia did not make any funding commitments.

Africa, which is home to 17 percent of the world’s population, is responsible for just 4 percent of global carbon emissions. Yet the continent faces disproportionate impacts from climate change.

The African Group of Negotiators (AGN), chaired by Ghana, rejected attempts to weaken both finance targets and the Paris Agreement’s goal of limiting warming to 1.5 degrees Celsius. It said that the debate on fossil fuels had “overshadowed” broken promises on adaptation finance.

“Those who were arguing about whether we should get fossil fuels in the cover decision or not were producers, exporters, importers and users of fossil fuels,” AGN chair Antwi-Boasiako Amoah said. “It was hypocrisy.”

AFCON verdict. Senegal was reinstated as the 2025 Africa Cup of Nations champion in a much-anticipated ruling by the Court of Arbitration for Sport on Tuesday.

Senegal initially won the final against Morocco 1-0 in extra time in January. Two months later, however, the Confederation of African Football (CAF) stripped the country of its title, awarding it to Morocco because Senegalese players temporarily walked off the pitch to protest refereeing decisions.

The court overturned CAF’s decision because the match was allowed to conclude. “By blowing his whistle to restart play, the Referee considered that the conditions for resuming the match had been met,” the court said in a statement. Morocco plans to appeal the decision.

Sudanese excavations. Last week, archaeologists unearthed the original floor of the largest medieval church in Nubia, in northern Sudan’s ancient city of Old Dongola. It was part of Makuria, one of three Christian Nubian kingdoms that existed from the sixth to 16th centuries.

The church was first discovered in 2021, but excavations were suspended two years later following the outbreak of Sudan’s civil war. Operations resumed last year in the abandoned city, which has managed to escape widespread destruction from the conflict so far.

UNESCO estimated that more than 100 cultural sites have been damaged, looted, or destroyed since fighting began in Sudan in April 2023.

Troll farmers. In HumAngle, Shade Mary-Ann Olaoye reports on U.S.-based social media influencers exploiting false narratives of a Christian genocide in Nigeria for engagement and donations. These accounts have posted artificial intelligence-generated content and videos from elsewhere in Africa, including Somalia, as supposed evidence of persecution in Nigeria.

As the creators “continue to spread misinformation, concerns are growing that their online influence could have real-world consequences,” Olaoye writes. “Campaigns like these can deepen ethno-religious fault lines, shape political decisions, and fuel reprisal attacks.”

AI bioweapons. In Rest of World, Danai Nesta Kupemba reports on researchers at a small AI lab in Ghana who “jailbroke” their own drug-discovery AI model, which allowed them to quickly design thousands of toxic molecules, to warn about the potential for AI bioweapons misuse.

“Scholars have long warned about AI’s bioweapons threat, but it is only recently that AI systems have truly outperformed Ph.D.-level biologists,” Kupemba writes.

Nosmot Gbadamosi is a multimedia journalist and the writer of Foreign Policy’s weekly Africa Brief. She has reported on human rights, the environment, and sustainable development from across the African continent. X: @nosmotg

#5897. It’s Time to Settle With Russia

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

MICHAEL C. DESCH is Packey J. Dee Professor of International Relations at the University of Notre Dame and founding Brian and Jeannelle Brady Family Director of the O’Brien Notre Dame International Security Center.

What America Should Give to Make Peace in Ukraine

In October, U.S. President Donald Trump sold his eleventh-hour deal to secure diesel from Russian President Vladimir Putin—pausing sanctions that his own administration had imposed—as a boon for the negotiations to end the war in Ukraine. The arrangement, he wrote on Truth Social, was intended to keep Russia at “the bargaining table.” Of course, it was always obvious to observers that the gambit had as much or more to do with bolstering the U.S. economy and stemming a massive victory for the Democrats in this month’s midterm elections, and in that, it failed.

Cynics have grounds for questioning all of Trump’s moves when it comes to Russia and Ukraine. He clearly dislikes Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky and shows a marked preference for illiberal leaders, especially those willing to give him easy wins. Still, a stopped clock can be right twice a day. And concessions such as the fuel deal are exactly what might move Russia and Ukraine closer to peace, even if they are not wholly motivated by public-spiritedness.

Ukraine and its backers in Europe and the United States have long maintained that Russia only responds to sticks. The problem with their argument is that since 2014, and especially since 2022, Moscow has been subjected to nothing but sticks. According to Kaja Kallas, the European Union’s foreign policy chief, the more than 25,000 sanctions imposed on Russia after its full-scale invasion of Ukraine had cost its economy almost $1.5 trillion as of June. And although Trump has backed Ukraine less consistently than U.S. President Joe Biden did, especially in his rhetoric, his administration has still provided significant financial, military, and intelligence support; his CIA has been more aggressive than Biden’s in assisting Ukraine’s offensive and defensive air operations. All of these coercive measures have proved insufficient to compel a change in Putin’s policy or turn the tide in Ukraine’s favor on the battlefield.

European leaders may fret that Trump, now a lame duck and focused on his legacy, will push for a deal on Ukraine that suits the Kremlin’s interests more than their own. But ultimately, this remains a war in which Russia has the advantage. Europe should recognize that its pressure tactics have not worked and welcome the U.S. president’s willingness to throw in a few carrots among the many sticks used to try to beat the Kremlin into submission. Washington, meanwhile, should continue to pursue a concessionary strategy by encouraging Ukraine to surrender territory, freezing NATO’s expansion, and offering sanctions relief to Moscow. Putin has shown a willingness to sign favorable deals in the past, including the Minsk agreements in 2014 and 2015, which promised an end to the civil conflict in Donbas only to collapse under the duress of Kyiv and European capitals. A more enduring peace is desperately needed in Ukraine, however unfair the price may seem.

Ukraine has had some recent success on the battlefield. Early in 2026, Kyiv launched offensives in western Zaporizhzhia that erased a year of Russian gains there. During the summer, the Ukrainian military embarked on a major counteroffensive, Operation Vivaldi, that made significant progress near Liman, delaying Russia’s campaign to take the Donbas fortress cities of Kramatorsk and Sloviansk. And after four years, Ukrainian forces have finally inflicted serious, albeit not irreparable, damage to the bridge connecting Crimea and Russia, disrupting a military transport route and tarnishing a potent symbol of Putin-era expansionism. Kyiv has also brought the war home to segments of the Russian public with its attacks on oil infrastructure and commercial targets such as warehouses for Wildberries, Russia’s largest online retailer. Supporters in Western capitals have trumpeted these achievements as evidence that Ukraine has turned the tide in a war that previously seemed to be moving in Moscow’s favor.

But tactical wins do not necessarily add up to strategic victory. And as Undersecretary of Defense for Policy Elbridge Colby reminded the Ukraine Defense Contact Group at a meeting earlier this month, the war will be decided on the ground. Casualties remain a huge problem for Ukraine. The few times Zelensky has addressed them, he has offered implausibly low figures for his side and cited astronomically high losses on the other. A recent study by Alexander Grable, a King’s College London war studies researcher, dissects these figures and concludes that Ukraine is suffering far higher casualties than Russia in absolute numbers—findings that, if true, refute the canard that Russia is uniquely careless with the lives of its soldiers. These days, while Ukraine pursues costly offensives to temporarily recover territory, Russia is taking cities and densely fortified areas by encirclement rather than frontal assault, reducing the risk to its fighters.

Although Ukraine’s strikes on Russian oil infrastructure have disrupted refining and strained domestic fuel supplies, Moscow has managed to cushion the blow through emergency measures and rapid repairs. In May, the U.S. Defense Intelligence Agency concluded that Kyiv’s long-range drone attacks did not “substantially affect Russian energy production,” which may explain why the Russian civilian population has not felt the full bite of them. The average Russian seems to be weathering this pain with something between resigned stoicism and increased determination to see the war through to victory. If Ukraine’s deep strikes were designed to encourage the United States to support Zelensky’s proposal for an energy truce, its effects have been counterproductive: higher oil prices have refilled the Kremlin’s war chest and greater energy costs are undermining allied support for Ukraine, especially in the United States.

Many Western battlefield analyses suggest that Russia has recently lost a bit of ground. In fact, the battle lines in Ukraine remain ambiguous. According to the widely followed Military Summary Telegram channel, which applies a nuanced metric of territorial control to geolocated videos from Russian and Ukrainian sources, Russia has continued to make inroads through October and November. In the north, its troops have carved out a buffer zone on the border, making gains around Sumy and Velykyi Burluk. The see-saw battle for Kupyiansk—a fiercely contested gateway to Kharkiv and northeastern Ukraine—once again favors Moscow. In Donetsk, Russian forces have largely taken the city of Dobropillia, which could enable them to creep north behind the Donbas fortress cities and encircle them. And Russia continues to gain ground in eastern Zaporizhzhia, where its units are now edging closer to the region’s administrative capital. Operation Vivaldi may have slowed the Russian advance north of Kramatorsk, but only at a significant cost to some of Ukraine’s best units. Much like the Kursk campaign in 2024, the counteroffensive seems more like a temporary media victory than a real change in the ground war.

In the air, Russia has commenced a deep strike campaign across Ukraine, using drones and ballistic missiles to turn the tables in key regions. Russian forces have severely disrupted Kyiv’s Black Sea operations, intensified strikes against data centers and electricity infrastructure, attacked locomotives, and hit targets ever closer to Ukraine’s western borders, and are now going after major bridges across the Dnieper, which could literally cut the country in two. Russia’s quantitative and qualitative advantage in jet-powered drones allows these weapons to function as effectively as cruise missiles, and recent air operations have been more sustained and systematic as a result. Ukraine’s dearth of American-made PAC-3 interceptor missiles—a result of the United States’ own dwindling defense stockpiles—means that Russian ballistic missiles are mostly unstoppable.

In October, Trump angrily wrote on social media that Zelensky “doesn’t want to end this war, and probably never did.” This was unfair: undoubtedly, Zelensky does want to end it, just on his own terms. The aid and support Europe and the United States have lavished on Ukraine—initially necessary for preventing a catastrophic defeat—now gives its president little incentive to make peace any other way. Early in the current war, when Ukraine was unexpectedly prevailing on the battlefield, it passed on two promising peace tracks that might have ended the fighting soon after it began.

But negotiations are now the only way forward for Ukraine, and they will not progress by targeting Russia exclusively with sticks. To overcome the current impasse, Ukraine and its supporters have to put their chips on the table. That means forgoing any hope of NATO accession—or of a sustained NATO military presence in Ukraine—and relinquishing territory to Russia. If Kyiv remains unwilling to make the concessions demanded of losing parties in war, then Washington can take steps to encourage better judgement. The United States could taper down or cut off certain services and munitions it provides the Ukrainian armed forces, including Starlink and Starshield satellite connectivity, Patriot missile defense systems, and intelligence support for air defense and deep strikes on Russian targets. Any of these deprivations would be felt immediately on the battlefield.

Washington should offer carrots of its own accord, too. Trump can lift some sanctions, such as those banning Russian commercial flights from U.S. airspace, without much controversy. Lifting others, such as those restricting fossil fuel exports and financial transactions, will be less palatable but more attractive to Russia and thus more effective. Russia’s readmission to the SWIFT interbank messaging service and other global commercial networks must be pursued accordingly.

Pro-Ukrainian hard-liners argue that no amount of carrots will be enough to satisfy Putin’s voracious appetite for territory. They warn that if Russia is not stopped in Ukraine, it will continue its campaign of conquest farther west through Poland, Lithuania, Latvia, Estonia, and beyond, with no end in sight. As Zelensky warned European leaders at the Berlin Security Conference in early November, the Russian military buildup is not just directed at his country, “it is directed against you.” But although Russia is making grinding progress on the fronts in eastern Ukraine, at the rate of its advances in 2025 and this year, it would take three decades just to capture the rest of Ukraine east of the Dnieper. And the idea that Russia, with a military of under 1.6 million men and women in uniform, could defeat a NATO alliance boasting 3.65 million soldiers is implausible, especially considering NATO’s overwhelming advantages in major weapons and military spending.

Even if Russia could take on NATO directly, it is far from clear that Putin wants to. The Kremlin’s war aims in Ukraine may not seem just, but they are narrower than many analysts allege. Since his 2007 speech in Munich excoriating the United States’ global hegemony—and after the 2008 NATO summit declaration that welcomed eventual membership for Georgia and Ukraine—NATO expansion has been Putin’s idée fixe. He has been similarly preoccupied with the status of the pro-Russia citizens of Eastern Ukraine and devoted substantial effort to ensuring protection of their linguistic rights and political influence through the Minsk agreements. Only when that failed did Moscow recognize the two regions of the Donbas, Luhansk and Donetsk as independent states eligible for membership in the Russian Federation, paving the way for direct Russian intervention in the region. Nonetheless, Putin continued to pursue a negotiated solution to the conflict through Turkish President Recep Tayyip Erdogan and Israel’s then Prime Minister Naftali Bennett. As with the Minsk process, it was Ukraine and its backers who walked away from these negotiations when their aims—the complete liberation of all Russian-occupied territory—still appeared achievable on the battlefield.

In a round of U.S.-led peace talks earlier this month in Abu Dhabi, Russia made maximalist demands, asking for the pullback of NATO forces from Poland and the Baltic states, limits on European nuclear deterrence, and restrictions on the size and capabilities of Ukraine’s military. But read in the broader context of Putin’s singular and consistent fixations, these demands look more like negotiating ploys than dealbreakers. It has long been clear what the Kremlin will settle for: control over Ukraine’s eastern border regions and a largely demilitarized Kyiv. Whatever the ultimate terms of this deal, some basic facts are clear. There is no going back to a Ukrainian Donbas, or a Ukrainian Crimea or Novorossiya. And whatever security guarantees Europe and the United States give Ukraine in the future, they cannot include NATO membership.

The status quo in Ukraine is unsustainable. Four years of war have lulled many of Kyiv’s supporters into complacency about the risk that continued pressure on Russia could create an escalatory spiral. There is increasingly cavalier talk in some newer NATO capitals, for example, about undertaking aggressive NATO exercises near Kaliningrad and overcoming the city’s air and missile defenses. But those steps may only further widen and deepen the war. The clear and present danger of vertical and horizontal escalation must not be dismissed.

Cracks are already showing in the West’s united front against Russia. France and other western European states are experiencing heightened social and political unrest, fueled in part by the costs of the war. In late October, Finnish President Alexander Stubb and Norwegian Prime Minister Jonas Gahr Store made a rare phone call to Putin to “convey messages directly regarding future negotiations” toward peace, according to a statement released by the two leaders. In the United States, a once bipartisan hard line toward Russia is starting to fall out of favor, even among Democrats. In mid-October, Democratic Representative Jim Himes told the Warsaw Security Forum that a future Democratic president will have to acknowledge the “tectonic fact” that the United States can no longer afford to play its historic leading role in confronting Russia.

There is reason to think that the Trump administration’s more conciliatory approach has already opened the door to diplomacy. The Oleshky truce brokered by First Lady Melania Trump and the Vatican successfully evacuated civilians trapped in a Russian-occupied city south of Kherson. And the president’s comments in his 60 Minutes interview this month suggested that he is ready to ply Russia with the incentives necessary to get a deal over the line. These are steps in the right direction, but in addition to altering the balance of carrots offered to Russia, the Trump administration must consider providing Ukraine security guarantees outside of the NATO framework.

Now in the twilight of his political career, Trump has made clear his desire to leave a legacy in the form of Nobel Peace Prizes and triumphal arches. Ending a major war in Ukraine would be a crowning achievement, which is why he has waxed lyrical about a “big, beautiful agreement” since he entered his second term in office. And if Trump still believes that China is the pacing threat of the twenty-first century, reintegrating Russia into the global economy and weakening the axis between Putin and Chinese leader Xi Jinping will be paramount for advancing his long-term political vision.

No one has lost money betting against peace in Ukraine since 2014, and it would be foolhardy to expect Trump to pursue diplomacy solely for altruistic reasons. But perhaps if he sees peace as being in his own interest, as well as that of the United States, he might, despite himself, broker an end to a bloody and increasingly fruitless war. Doing so could be one of the few positive legacies of his second term, even if it does not get him to Oslo.

The Kremlin’s New Mental Map Is Changing the War in Ukraine—and the Confrontation With the West

How a True “Energy Cease-Fire” Can Curb Putin

And the West Has Done Far Too Little to Deter It

How Technology Empowers—and Imperils—Dictators

America and Its Allies Must Scale Together for a New Era of War

Why the Islamic Republic Can’t Make Peace

How China Exploited a Massive Loophole in U.S. National Security—and How Congress Can Close It

Democracy Can’t Be Saved at the Ballot Box

What America Should Give to Make Peace in Ukraine

Only a Radical Course Change Can Win Back Its Most Important Ally
