#5891. 2026's biggest political turkeys: Scandals, gaffes and other memorable unfortunate moments from this year

From Eric Swalwell to Tim Walz, several political figures are looking back on 2026 with regret after making the list of biggest political turkeys.

As 2026 comes to a close, several political blunders, missteps, and serious allegations have left prominent politicians and notable figures presumably looking back with regret. Here are the top political turkeys who made headlines for unfortunate reasons this year.

Heading into 2026, Eric Swalwell was a well-known member of Congress and the leading Democratic contender in the race for governor of California. By March, he had become the frontrunner in the race and looked poised to take control of the nation’s largest economy in a state where Democrats rarely lose in statewide general elections.

Shortly after, that all changed when allegations of sexual assault and other misconduct surfaced in April, prompting prominent Democrats to swiftly withdraw their endorsements and call for Swalwell to abandon his gubernatorial bid.

SWALWELL’S FORMER DEM RIVAL UNLOADS ON HIS ‘LIGHTWEIGHT’ CAREER AS SEXUAL ASSAULT ALLEGATIONS EMERGE

Several prominent political figures faced controversy, public missteps and serious allegations throughout 2026, landing them on this year's list of political turkeys. (Kevin Carter/Getty Images; Drew Angerer/Getty Images)

Swalwell denied the sexual assault allegations, but within days he suspended his campaign and resigned from Congress amid bipartisan calls for his ouster, which effectively ended his political career and put him in potential legal jeopardy.

Swalwell faces a federal investigation and separate probes in New York and Los Angeles into sexual assault allegations, which he denies. FBI agents seized his electronic devices and searched his Washington home in August, and in October searched a Beverly Hills mansion where he had stayed. No criminal charges have been filed.

In 2024, Walz was elevated to the top of the national scene when he was chosen by former Vice President Kamala Harris to be her running mate for president.

Since then, Walz’s political fortunes have taken a downward turn after the Harris-Walz ticket lost the presidential election, leading some pundits to criticize the decision to add him to the ticket.

In January 2026, Walz’s political career took another hit when he announced he was dropping his bid for re-election as governor amid a massive fraud scandal that is estimated to have cost the state billions.

Polling in June showed that Walz’s approval rating had dropped to its lowest level since he took office.

ANGER SWELLS AFTER SWALWELL'S 'OPEN SECRET' WITH WOMEN WENT UNREPORTED FOR YEARS

Republicans faced historical headwinds heading into the midterm elections and were dealing with significant voter frustration on issues like gas prices and affordability. While many expected Democrats to have a good night on Nov. 3, the GOP ended up losing control of both chambers of Congress ahead of President Donald Trump’s final two years in office.

Additionally, Democrats picked up control of the majority of governorships in the United States as well as control of the majority of state legislatures, giving the party a dominant presence it hasn’t seen in decades.

Axios reported that 75% of Americans now live in a state with a Democratic governor and 53% live in a state where Democrats control the legislature and the governorship.

TIM WALZ SAYS HE TAKES RESPONSIBILITY FOR MINNESOTA FRAUD BY NOT RUNNING FOR RE-ELECTION

After years as one of the most recognizable and outspoken Democratic members of Congress, Crockett will be out of a job on Capitol Hill in 2027 after opting against House re-election to pursue an unsuccessful Senate bid.

Her decision followed Texas Republicans’ redrawing of the congressional map, which reduced the number of Democratic-leaning seats in the Dallas-Fort Worth area from three to two and scrambled the plans of three Democratic incumbents. Crockett’s redrawn 30th District remained Democratic-leaning, but she chose a statewide campaign rather than seeking another House term.

Crockett lost the Democratic primary to state Rep. James Talarico in March, then reignited tensions by describing the contest as a "racist race" and pushed back against expectations that she would campaign alongside him. "People keep trying to say, ‘Well, Jasmine has to go and hold his hand.’ No, I don’t," she said during a July podcast appearance, arguing that supporting Black candidates further down the ballot would help increase turnout.

Although Crockett and her campaign said she had endorsed Talarico through her post-primary call to support the Democratic ticket, her reluctance to campaign with him and his omission from her political action committee’s endorsements fueled questions about her enthusiasm. Talarico defended her, saying he welcomed her endorsement, while Crockett maintained that elevating lesser-known candidates would benefit Democrats on the ballot.

Talarico ultimately defeated Republican Ken Paxton by about 12 percentage points, becoming the first Texas Democrat to win a U.S. Senate seat there since Lloyd Bentsen was re-elected in 1988.

The socialist Wisconsin gubernatorial candidate was dominating in the polls heading into the August Democratic primary, but her campaign stumbled in its final days as she struggled to explain past calls to cancel Thanksgiving before narrowly losing to Milwaukee County Executive David Crowley, a candidate who had exited and then returned to the race.

Progressive firebrand Francesca Hong sought to topple the Democratic establishment in a closely watched Wisconsin showdown. (Talia Sprague/Bloomberg/Getty Images)

The controversy centered on a resurfaced 2020 social media post in which Hong wrote, "Cancel Thanksgiving. Should have done this in 1621," and criticized celebrating colonialism. Asked on CNN in August whether she still wanted the holiday canceled, Hong initially emphasized that Thanksgiving was painful for some communities rather than directly rejecting her earlier position. She later reversed course, saying she loved Thanksgiving and did not want to cancel it. After losing the primary, Hong acknowledged that she regretted the CNN interview.

The Republican senator lost her seat in deep-red Mississippi to Democrat Scott Colom after an answer about rising beef prices became damaging campaign fodder.

Asked by WLOX anchor Hugh Keeton about rising beef prices during a local television interview, Hyde-Smith responded, "You know, you have so many proteins to choose from." Colom later used the exchange in a campaign ad featuring voters saying they would rather pick another senator.

Colom ultimately defeated Hyde-Smith by just 8,031 votes, a margin of less than 1 percentage point — close enough that her remark, amplified by her opponent’s advertising, could have made the difference in a race that cost Republicans a seat many believed was safe.

Former House Speaker Newt Gingrich singled out the remark while discussing Republican losses with Larry Kudlow on Fox News before the race was called, arguing that suggesting cheaper food failed to address voters’ economic frustrations.

"I mean, it’s not the job of a senator to explain her failure by suggesting a new diet," Gingrich said.

Other honorable mentions include Democratic Fayette County Council candidate Jaime West, who was ridiculed by critics who said she appeared to be drunk and slurred her way through attacks on Republicans during a televised candidate forum; Ohio Republican gubernatorial candidate Vivek Ramaswamy, whose controversial ad featuring his opponent’s mother drew fierce blowback before he lost his race; and Maine Democratic Senate candidate Graham Platner's fall from grace as he suspended his campaign after being accused of sexual assault.

#5890. The Chaos Theory of International Relations

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Infographic: The Chaos Theory of International Relations

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

The enemy of my enemy is my friend. This appears as close to a truism as there is in international relations. But what happens when your friend might also be friends with your enemy? Global politics offers all too many examples of how regularly and unexpectedly simple realpolitik calculus can boomerang back on countries in ironic ways.

Consider Israel’s violation of U.S. export agreements, transferring advanced missile and fighter jet technologies to China.

The enemy of my enemy is my friend. This appears as close to a truism as there is in international relations. But what happens when your friend might also be friends with your enemy? Global politics offers all too many examples of how regularly and unexpectedly simple realpolitik calculus can boomerang back on countries in ironic ways.

Consider Israel’s violation of U.S. export agreements, transferring advanced missile and fighter jet technologies to China.

The accumulated leakages of American know-how have boosted China’s aerial capabilities, threatening the U.S. posture in the Pacific…

…and have also improved the quality of China’s missile exports to Iran…

…which have then been aimed and fired back at Israel. Oops.

Consider Israel’s violation of U.S. export agreements, transferring advanced missile and fighter jet technologies to China. The accumulated leakages of American know-how have boosted China’s aerial capabilities, threatening the U.S. posture in the Pacific and have also improved the quality of China’s missile exports to Iran, which have then been aimed and fired back at Israel. Oops.

Now recall Pakistani nuclear scientist A.Q. Khan’s smuggling of centrifuge technology from the Netherlands in the 1970s, accelerating Pakistan’s nuclear program but also those of North Korea and Iran. Fast-forward to 2026, and Saudi Arabia, Pakistan, and Turkey signed the Mecca defense pact, ostensibly to protect Saudi Arabia against the nuclear threat from Iran—whose nuclear blueprint came straight from Khan.

Realist international theory takes anarchy (the absence of central authority) as its principal point of departure. It then goes on to identify patterns of behavior that order the system, such as balancing and bandwagoning. But all too often in contemporary geopolitics, these patterns don’t hold. Indeed, the frequency with which realist theory falls short, as in the examples above, suggests we live in a world dominated by chaos rather than anarchy.

Contrary to conventional usage, chaos theory does not imply no rules. In fact, agents in chaotic systems follow rules precisely. It is the consequences of their collective behavior that make chaotic systems so unpredictable. In two words: butterfly effects. In 1951, historian Arnold Wolfers famously analogized international relations to a set of billiard balls, colliding as they pursued power maximization. Today’s billiard table is a lot more crowded in every sense. Along the way, the system has graduated from complicated—with mechanistic but determinable patterns—to complex, with feedback loops among a wide range of actors playing out at regional and global scales over multiple time horizons and generating novel outcomes.

The principle that best explains how each unit behaves in this chaotic system is multialignment. In multialignment, all actors, large and small, put themselves at the center of their own calculations rather than occupying a fixed position in a given hierarchy. They seek to connect with and build ties with as many other states and in as many ways as can be useful to them. This is not so much “the enemy of my enemy is my friend” but rather, “How many friends can I have and get away with?”

In 2006, when I first coined the term “multialignment” in an essay with C. Raja Mohan, I was in the midst of traveling through 40 countries, from Brazil to Saudi Arabia to Kazakhstan, to observe how they practiced this diplomatic technique. At the time, Washington was consumed with neo-unipolar triumphalism after the invasions of Afghanistan and Iraq. But none of the leaders I met during my journey gave the impression of feeling trapped by George W. Bush’s famous “with us or against us” mantra. Each was building ties with Europe and China, Russia and India. Specifically, every country that Washington labeled a “rogue state”—Venezuela, Libya, Iran, North Korea, and others—was also receiving lifelines from China, with many seeking investment and trade with Europe as well.

With formal alliances replaced by dalliances of unpredictable duration, the real action shifted to new functional groupings such as BRICS and the Shanghai Cooperation Organisation (SCO). As these organizations grab the headlines today with their security initiatives and de-dollarization efforts, it’s worth reconstructing how multialignment evolved.

America’s “unipolar moment” carried the seeds of its own destruction. The acceleration of globalization that the United States itself underwrote drove rising powers’ economic growth and confidence, giving them the capacity to seek out the best deals for themselves. Power diffusion is the genie that can’t be put back in the bottle.

From left: Egyptian President Gamal Abdel Nasser, Indian Prime Minister Jawaharlal Nehru, and Yugoslav President Josip Broz Tito meet on Brioni Island, Yugoslavia, in July 1956, prior to the establishment of the Non-Aligned Movement.AFP via Getty Images

During the Cold War, postcolonial nations were weak and deferential. The Non-Aligned Movement first convened in 1961 in Belgrade, in then-Yugoslavia, to promote anti-colonialism and south-south solidarity. Its economic counterpart was the Group of 77, which coordinated positions on trade and debt relief. Other than the right to give speeches at the United Nations, their members were price-takers. In today’s lingo: NPCs, not main characters.

The end of the Cold War changed that. Increasingly, countries want to become agents rather than cogs in someone else’s strategic map. The desire for agency—like the human desire for recognition—runs deep among the nearly 100 postcolonial nations born after World War II. After centuries as colonies and decades as Cold War pawns, none of them want to be inert targets of great-power machinations again. They remember the past and don’t plan to let it repeat itself. The message should be clear: It’s not about you (America)—it’s about us (everyone else) seizing the moment.

The transition isn’t just from a U.S.-dominated unipolar world to a multipolar one but to a system in which power rests not in static units but in contextual relations and networks—in other words, fluid formations that instantiate when the need arises. It matters less what the latent capacity of the power is; the fundamental principle is that all of them are multialigning to the best of their ability.

Chinese Finance Minister Lan Fo’an (left) and Brazilian Finance Minister Fernando Haddad pose for a photo as Chinese Premier Li Qiang and Brazilian President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva applaud after the signing of a raft of agreements on the sidelines of the BRICS summit in Rio de Janeiro on July 5, 2025. Pablo Porciuncula/AFP via Getty Images

In 1848, Lord Palmerston declared in a speech to the U.K. House of Commons: “We have no eternal allies, and we have no perpetual enemies.” Now, every state is following his dictum by hedging, multialigning, or realigning to suit its immediate and long-term needs.

For some states, multialignment is a matter of survival. Israel’s export of reconnaissance drone technology to Russia in the early 2010s inadvertently stimulated the offensive drone capabilities that Russia has been deploying to batter Ukraine—which in turn is competing with Israel to acquire sufficient munitions from the United States. And with U.S. public sentiment turning against unconditional support for the Gaza war, Israel has doubled down on procurement from its second-largest arms supplier—Germany. Now, a converted Volkswagen plant is manufacturing components for Israel’s Iron Dome defense system while the United Arab Emirates helps Israel shoot down incoming Iranian missiles.

In other cases, multialignment is about both identity and economics. NATO member Turkey may apply to join the SCO. And why not? It no longer aspires to join the European Union, and the Asian Infrastructure Investment Bank has sponsored nearly 50 projects across Turkey involving manufacturing, transportation, water, and other areas. As the country most associated with spanning Europe and Asia, Turkey rightly wants to dictate east-west energy corridors.

SCO core members—China and Russia—are also core BRICS powers and have recruited dialogue partner states from West, Central, South, and Southeast Asia.

Turkey, Kazakhstan, Uzbekistan, and Mongolia are geographically vital states associated with both SCO and NATO —of which Turkey is a full member.

Saudi Arabia, the United Arab Emirates, and India are practicing shrewd multialignment by associating with all major strategic bodies.

The Gulf countries have also been adept at multialignment. Saudi Arabia has hosted large U.S. military bases while expanding its arms deals with China. The UAE also has deep defense ties with the United States but now counts China as its largest trading partner. Both Saudi Arabia and the UAE are SCO dialogue partners, and the latter is a member of BRICS as well. They both also support Moscow’s sanctions evasion efforts, including by routing payments from Russia to India. And speaking of multialignment, India remains an exemplar—it is part of the U.S.-led Quadrilateral Security Dialogue but also BRICS and the SCO.

Around the world, this kind of “forum shopping” has become the single dominant mode of international behavior. Indeed, it has come to characterize not just emerging power logic but the strategy of all states.

Even the superpowers are playing the multialignment game. The United States wants Europe to shoulder the burden in Ukraine while it seeks to woo Russia with resource investments. Moscow, for its part, is happy to have Washington bypass Kyiv (and Europe) in making offers that may one day reduce its dependence on China. And with U.S. President Donald Trump imposing wanton tariffs on Canada, Ottawa is being fast-tracked into “associate member” status with the EU. At the same time, U.S. tariffs on Indian exports helped the EU accelerate its efforts this year to conclude a free trade agreement with New Delhi.

Managing the global order with an antagonistic Washington.

Self-help is becoming more vital in a vicious world.

China, in turn, is ramping up investments and joint ventures in European markets to convince the continent’s leaders to keep tariffs lower than those the United States imposes. And in region after region, Beijing aims to maintain friendly ties with both parties in local rivalries: Saudi Arabia and Iran, India and Pakistan, Kazakhstan and Uzbekistan, and so on.

To a striking extent, the United States is actually bringing to life many of the new diplomatic networks that enable multialignment. The Biden administration launched the Indo-Pacific Economic Framework as a “high-standard” alternative to Asia’s Regional Comprehensive Economic Partnership. It also convened the first Quad leaders’ summit together with partners India, Japan, and Australia in 2021. The Trump administration, for its part, has launched the Pax Silica network to coordinate semiconductor supply chains and the “Mine to Magnet” initiative to diversify critical mineral extraction, processing, and distribution. Such opportunistic, functional, and often nonexclusive partnerships are what multialignment is all about.

Pundits have consistently predicted that, in conflicts such as Ukraine and the current Iran war, middle powers will eventually be forced to “choose sides.” Yet they don’t. Instead, they play multialignment to gain leverage over great powers rather than the reverse. This makes multialignment a powerful antidote to the notion of an inevitable “new cold war” between the United States and China. Washington and Beijing don’t get to carve up the world if the world doesn’t play along. And it isn’t. Indeed, nothing inspires countries to pursue multialignment more than being told they can’t.

Multialignment, then, is how the “rest” keep the superpowers honest. The United States has lost ground to China where its trade deals feel too constrictive. But, for the same reason, it can also poke at Beijing by offering lifelines to countries where Chinese debt-trap diplomacy and product dumping have gone too far.

A decade ago, the entire African continent was portrayed as a Chinese neo-colony. Sub-Saharan Africa in particular was considered China’s economic playground. But now the U.S. International Development Finance Corp. (DFC) has helped underwrite more than $4 billion in the Lobito Corridor, spanning Angola, the Democratic Republic of the Congo, and Zambia, to extract and process critical minerals outside of China’s supply chains. Similarly, Pakistan, long China’s most reliable ally, has received U.S. Export-Import Bank loans to accelerate critical mineral production and exports to the United States and is seeking additional U.S. financing from the DFC and other vehicles to potentially buy Boeing aircraft and upgrade oil refineries.

Through multialignment, countries find ways to cooperate even when no great power is at the wheel. Regional groupings from the Association of Southeast Asian Nations (ASEAN) to the Gulf Cooperation Council (GCC) to Mercosur are premised on bringing down internal barriers and harnessing complementarities. This enables participants to reduce their dependence on distant superpowers and elevate themselves in the process. Cross-regional axes such as the defense procurement network spanning Ukraine, Turkey, and the GCC, and the GCC’s food and energy security partnership with ASEAN, similarly illustrate how functional cooperation doesn’t require a great-power patron. The current Cambrian explosion of international partnerships embodies the flourishing of multialignment—warts and all.

Many continue to lament the end of the liberal international order and the return to a world of naked self-interest. But in doing so, they miss the new and productive strategic networks emerging in place of the old, moribund multilateralism. Multialignment paves the way for geopolitics to evolve from the bottom up rather than just the top down. If international relations now seems more unpredictable than ever, that chaos isn’t a bug—it’s a feature.

Parag Khanna is the founder and CEO of the geospatial analytics software AlphaGeo and the author of Connectography, The Future Is Asian, MOVE, and other books on geopolitics and globalization. X: @paragkhanna

#5889. China Is Preparing for the End of Détente

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

YUN SUN is Director of the China Program at the Stimson Center.

After three cordial meetings in six months between U.S. President Donald Trump and Chinese leader Xi Jinping, Beijing is trumpeting optimism about U.S.-Chinese relations. At last week’s Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation forum in Shenzhen, China—where Xi personally met Trump at the airport and the two embraced—Xi said that U.S.-Chinese relations were on a “stable and positive trajectory” and that both sides were making efforts to sustain “current positive momentum.” Buoyed by Trump’s unprecedented friendliness toward China, Beijing is amplifying a narrative that relations between the two superpowers have entered a more amicable phase of détente—what the Chinese ambassador to the United States, Xie Feng, has hailed as a “new chapter” in the countries’ relationship.

But this sentiment is more aspiration than accepted reality. Many foreign policy scholars and strategists in China remain largely pessimistic about bilateral relations with the United States. Great-power competition might have been put on hold temporarily because of Trump’s personal desire for warmer ties, but the fundamental national security challenges China poses to the United States remain. There is a tacit consensus in Beijing that heightened competition and hostility are likely to reemerge as a U.S. priority once Trump is gone, if not sooner. Indeed, these tensions are already beginning to appear: the ouster of Jensen Huang, the NVIDIA co-founder and CEO, was read in Beijing as a coordinated attack by members of the Washington establishment to take China policy in a more hawkish direction. And it was only because of Trump’s personal intervention that Huang was reinstated.

Beijing is thus wasting no time making the most of the last two years of the Trump administration. The goal is twofold: to preserve a positive direction for U.S.-Chinese ties to show Americans that better relations are worth maintaining and to consolidate any gains from dealing with Trump to build a higher floor under the relationship in anticipation of an inevitable downturn. For the next two years, China’s strategy is to pursue trade deals and investment opportunities, use public diplomacy to win hearts and minds in the United States, set a new normal on limiting U.S. arms sales to Taiwan, and take a harder line toward U.S. allies in the region, especially Japan and the Philippines, to punish perceived defiance of China’s vision for order in the Asia-Pacific. In many areas, what matters are tangible markers of progress and practical gains that future U.S. policymakers may want to preserve. Then, if and when relations sharply deteriorate, as most assume they will, such actions will ensure that even a significant setback will still leave Beijing in a stronger position than it would otherwise be in—and remind Washington of what is possible when ties are stable.

The Chinese policy community has been watching closely as Washington’s strategy has shifted. Under the first Trump and the Biden administrations, it was “strategic competition”—a form of great-power struggle centered on divergent values and interests. Under the second Trump administration, it has become “constructive strategic stability,” an idea that originates in the “mutually assured destruction” doctrine of nuclear deterrence but actually goes much further. Although there is no formal definition, strategic stability between the United States and China implies managing frictions in bilateral relations through accommodation and reciprocity on issues as varied as trade, Taiwan, and technology, especially artificial intelligence.

For American observers, the driving force of this change is obvious: Trump. China policy under the second Trump administration has been directed by the president himself, who, as analysts describe it, has acted as his own China “desk officer.” It was Trump’s decision to abandon the great-power competition narrative, to pursue warmer ties with Xi, and to avoid friction with Beijing over the sensitive issue of Taiwan’s status. And the Republican-dominated Congress of the past two years has rarely pushed back against the White House’s decision-making on foreign policy.

Despite this obvious explanation for the changes in U.S.-Chinese relations, leaders in Beijing have instead opted to embrace a structural argument rather than one based on Trump’s personal preferences. In this telling, China’s forceful retaliation against the Trump administration’s escalation of tariffs in April 2025 and its “50 percent rule” imposing export controls on subsidiaries of blacklisted Chinese entities five months later effectively demonstrated China’s ability to disrupt the American economy. Beijing’s immediate adoption of equivalent tariffs on U.S. exports that April and its export controls on rare earths in September 2025 forced the Trump administration to roll back its policies and seek reconciliation. Beijing’s public posture suggests a belief that what is driving the détente is that the United States and China are now in a state of “mutually assured disruption” in which neither side can overwhelm the other without significant damage to itself.

Beijing outwardly favors a structural explanation because it conveys China’s power and boosts internal support for the path the country has taken under Xi. The problem is that this narrative cannot explain why Trump did not choose a more aggressive approach from the start of his second term. Even before the tariff war escalated, Trump had eased U.S. diplomatic and political pressure on China. He adopted a more skeptical position on Taiwan, accusing it of stealing American jobs, while avoiding any public commitment of support for the island. He also abandoned the Biden administration’s framework of pitting democracy against authoritarianism. Washington had—and still has—many of its own points of leverage over Beijing; from the start of his second term, however, Trump has chosen not to use most of them.

Privately, therefore, many members of China’s policy community remain deeply pessimistic about the future of bilateral relations after Trump. They believe that Trump’s second-term approach to China is a deviation in U.S. policy, rather than a sustainable course correction. Although Chinese officials and diplomats praise the stabilization or even improvement of bilateral relations, the expectation among policy thinkers in Beijing is that the current moment is ephemeral and that the next U.S. president will resume more aggressive competition. The Democratic Party’s sweeping victory in the midterm elections has deepened the belief that a gloomy future awaits after 2028. After all, the Biden administration not only inherited the framing of U.S.-Chinese relations as a great-power competition from the first Trump administration but also escalated the U.S. response, including by adopting more stringent export controls. Echoing how Mao Zedong once told Nixon that he “liked rightists”—that is, Republicans—in the United States, many strategists in China today see Democrats as less pragmatic and more likely to focus on ideological differences with Beijing.

But rather than withholding cooperation, Beijing is leaning into positive engagement with Trump. This is strategic: the goal is to reinforce the framing of U.S.-Chinese relations in the United States as constructive and productive. Even if the expectation is that relations will return to being more hostile, competitive, and confrontational soon after the 2028 U.S. elections, Beijing sees its best option as trying to take advantage of the current upswing. The hope is that shifting the way the two countries define their relationship and maximizing positive interactions will gradually change the entrenched views of policymakers in Washington who view China as a mortal enemy.

This is why the strategic framing of the leadership summits this year is much more important to Beijing than any agreement that could be reached at the working level. A positive reframing is the crown jewel that Chinese leaders have been aiming for. Any concrete deliverables—including commitments to purchase soybeans and planes and to set up boards of trade and investment—are not the end goal, but the means to showcase and solidify a belief that the United States and China are not predestined to destructive competition. Whether the two sides completely follow through with any of their commitments is secondary; what matters is that these deals and the posture they represent reinforce and encourage further cooperation from the United States down the road. The logic is that once Washington realizes the benefits of a positive framing of bilateral relations, it will want to maintain the momentum into the future.

For China, such an approach has few downsides. The worst-case scenario is that the United States and China return to great-power competition. But the belief is that Washington’s default is to pursue this path anyway—and that at the very least, improved relations can set a higher baseline for the next phase of competition. China has everything to gain and nothing to lose.

Beijing sees hope that this strategy will work. Already, U.S. public opinion has been tending more favorably toward China. According to polls conducted by Gallup, although more Americans still hold negative views of China than positive ones, favorable sentiment has ticked upward from record lows in 2023. Favorable views of China climbed from 15 percent of those surveyed in February 2023 to 34 percent in February 2026. For China, this uptick is linked with the Trump administration’s policy approach, reinforcing the belief that further positive ties will expand public support in the United States for a less confrontational approach to China.

This is China’s theory of success. And it is why Beijing continues to promote grassroots interactions, such as people-to-people exchanges. During his state visit to Washington in September, Xi invited 100,000 young Americans to visit and study in China over the next five years. Beijing sees this as the path to winning U.S. hearts and minds and building irrevocable support for warmer ties.

The bad news for Beijing, however, is that there is no guarantee that such efforts will reverse U.S. skepticism or hostility toward China within the next two years. If anything, in line with the theory that public opinion follows the views of political elites rather than the other way around, it is likely Trump’s about-face on China that has softened U.S. public attitudes toward China. That means that if the next president takes a harder line on China, U.S. public opinion might harden, too—and any efforts by Beijing to win hearts and minds could even backfire if they are viewed in Washington as an attempt to manipulate American citizens.

But in Beijing, the feeling is that there is no better alternative to trying to move the goalposts with positive framing. It just might be the best China can do to manage a very uncertain and unsustainable détente.

#5888. The Top 10 Reasons to Be Thankful in 2026

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Argument: The Top 10 Reasons to Be Thankful in 2026

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

When the world is looking especially bleak—as it was after Election Day in 2024—making a list of the things I’m thankful for is a challenge. But that isn’t the case this year. Although the state of the world in 2026 contains much to regret and many things to worry about, there are some salient developments that have me feeling especially grateful as I contemplate the upcoming holiday. Without further ado, here are the top 10 things that I’m thankful for this year.

1. The U.S. Midterms. It turns out U.S. democracy is healthier than many people thought—which is not to say that it doesn’t need some serious improvements—and the Trump administration was as inept at rigging things as many of us had hoped. And make no mistake: This election was far more than the usual setback that incumbent parties face in the midterms. It was a drubbing of historic proportions. It took real political ineptitude to turn Texas and South Carolina blue, for example, and to produce a nine-seat swing in the Senate. Full points to the successful Democratic candidates and to party stalwarts like Stacey Abrams who laid much of the groundwork, but you have to give Trump and his minions credit for enacting policies that a clear majority of Americans really hated. Because I like the rule of law, the separation of powers, a competent and noncorrupt government, disciplined policy-making, separation of church and state, and other quaint traditions, I’m grateful that most of my fellow citizens agree (along with a dislike for foolish wars, cockeyed trade policies, predatory hegemony, oligarchic excess, sexual misconduct, rising living costs, and deliberate and probably illegal efforts to gut public services).

When the world is looking especially bleak—as it was after Election Day in 2024—making a list of the things I’m thankful for is a challenge. But that isn’t the case this year. Although the state of the world in 2026 contains much to regret and many things to worry about, there are some salient developments that have me feeling especially grateful as I contemplate the upcoming holiday. Without further ado, here are the top 10 things that I’m thankful for this year.

1. The U.S. Midterms. It turns out U.S. democracy is healthier than many people thought—which is not to say that it doesn’t need some serious improvements—and the Trump administration was as inept at rigging things as many of us had hoped. And make no mistake: This election was far more than the usual setback that incumbent parties face in the midterms. It was a drubbing of historic proportions. It took real political ineptitude to turn Texas and South Carolina blue, for example, and to produce a nine-seat swing in the Senate. Full points to the successful Democratic candidates and to party stalwarts like Stacey Abrams who laid much of the groundwork, but you have to give Trump and his minions credit for enacting policies that a clear majority of Americans really hated. Because I like the rule of law, the separation of powers, a competent and noncorrupt government, disciplined policy-making, separation of church and state, and other quaint traditions, I’m grateful that most of my fellow citizens agree (along with a dislike for foolish wars, cockeyed trade policies, predatory hegemony, oligarchic excess, sexual misconduct, rising living costs, and deliberate and probably illegal efforts to gut public services).

2. The Global Backlash Against Autocracy. Here’s something more to celebrate: This trend wasn’t just confined to the United States. I’m also thankful that voters in Brazil, Hungary, and Serbia made similar choices, rejecting would-be (or in some cases, incumbent) autocrats and their self-dealing schemes. To be sure, strongman rule continues in many places, and there is reason to worry about the upcoming election in France, the enduring popularity of right-wing parties in Germany, and the authoritarian trends that have returned to parts of Latin America. But I’ll give thanks that what looked like a cresting autocratic wave has been halted, at least for now.

3. Being a Jerk Lost Its Political Appeal. There is one final aspect of the political landscape that deserves a separate mention: The apparent rejection of asshole-ry in the body politic and a renewed desire for principled but civil behavior by political leaders. In recent decades, acting like a complete jerk—lying, engaging in vulgar name-calling, embracing misogyny and racism, trolling relentlessly, and such—had been a surprisingly effective political tactic. U.S. President Donald Trump is the leading example, of course, but a similar approach was embraced by the likes of Pete Hegseth, Vivek Ramaswamy, Laura Loomer, Ken Paxton, Lauren Boebert, Nancy Mace, and Greg Abbott, among others. It was part of the standard playbook of Fox News and provocateurs like Ben Shapiro. By 2026, in fact, acting like a jerk had become a central part of the MAGA/GOP brand. This approach was successful in part because it made Republicans look and sound like real fighters (as when Trump told a rally crowd that “I am your vengeance”) at a moment when mainstream Democrats seemed unclear about what they believed and terminally hamstrung by wokeness.

But over the past year, Democrats produced a bunch of candidates who made it clear that they had strong beliefs that they were willing to stand up for, even if their views were controversial. But at the same time, people like Zohran Mamdani, James Talarico, Abdul el-Sayed, Annie Andrews, and Jon Ossoff come across as likeable folks who want to focus on policies rather than personalities, and they don’t engage in a lot of nasty personal attacks. Notably, Democrats dumped toxic candidates such as Graham Plattner in Maine, while Republicans (and especially Trump) doubled down on some of theirs. Surprise, surprise: It turned out that American voters have grown tired (if not disgusted) by political figures are chronic liars, selfish, corrupt, and generally mean-spirited jerks, especially when they are also incompetent. One may therefore hope for the return of civility to American public discourse, which would help heal some of the country’s current divisions. And as one watches the British electorate sour on faux-populist Nigel Farage, one may even hope that this trend extends beyond the United States.

4. Balancing Behavior. I ’ll confess to a certain vested interest here, but I’ve been pleasantly struck by evidence of balancing behavior in a wide variety of contexts. Balance-of-power politics often gets a bad rap, as a world of competing powers is seen as one that exacerbates tensions and eventually leads to greater conflict. But as we saw during the unipolar era, unchecked power can be equally (if not more) dangerous and result in a bunch of pointless wars. One of the many problems with “liberal hegemony” is that it made global security overly dependent on U.S. engagements almost everywhere, which made peace dependent on Washington showing good judgment and consistently doing the right thing. Even before the arrival of Trump, there were good reasons to question that assumption.

If states balance readily against potential threats, and if leaders recognize that this is the case, then states that might be tempted to challenge the status quo will be deterred by the prospect of facing combined opposition. The knowledge that others will also face similar constraints also reduces every state’s need to make gains to be secure. Thus, efficient balancing behavior can be a powerful cause of peace, as it was during the Cold War.

For this reason, I’m heartened by the evidence of renewed balancing, even if it is currently less efficient than one might wish. Europe is rearming after years of neglect, Middle Eastern states are forging new arrangements that are less dependent on unilateral U.S. protection, and Asian countries continue to coordinate responses to rising Chinese power and assertiveness. Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney’s call for joint efforts to counter the Trump administration’s predatory policies is consistent with this logic, as well. Let us hope that the next U.S. administration recognizes the danger of provoking ever-growing resistance, rediscovers the value of constructive ties with allies, and adjusts its behavior accordingly.

5. The Gulf on Israel Widens and Deepens. Last year, I expressed some thanks that U.S. public attitudes toward Israel were shifting, and that trend has accelerated over the past year. The electoral victories by candidates who were openly critical of Israel’s behavior is an obvious sign of this development, as is Senator Chris Van Hollen’s challenge to Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer’s efforts to become majority leader next year. Van Hollen may fall short, but the fact that he could pose as a credible alternative is evidence that we are living in a very different world.

My thanks that the American body politic is shifting to a more defensible position is tempered by my fear that it is too little and much too late. Had this shift occurred a couple of decades ago, it might have salvaged the two-state solution, marginalized Hamas politically, bolstered liberal forces within Israel and halted its own spiral toward extremism, opened the door to gradual détente with Iran, and left the Middle East far better off than it is today. While I’m thankful that the United States is finally coming to its senses on this issue, I cannot look upon the current situation with any sense of pleasure.

6. Tech Bros Brought Down to Earth. A year ago, tech bros like Elon Musk, Mark Zuckerberg, Sam Altman, Peter Theil, Jensen Huang, and others were riding high. Not only were they amassing unreal amounts of wealth, but they were pouring millions of dollars into the political system and performing end runs around efforts to rein them in.

I don’t want to overstate this trend—the latest developments at Nvidia underscore that tech leaders still have the ear of the president—but I’m glad to see public opinion and public officials begin to demand greater accountability from these self-designated masters of the universe. There’s a growing pushback against data centers, Musk’s millions didn’t prevent the blue tsunami, the saga of Nvidia’s Jensen Huang has also exposed potentially corrupt dealings by U.S. officials and revealed that some of the corporate titans care more about their own bank accounts than they do about national security. Looking ahead, it seems likely that politicians in the United States, Europe, and elsewhere are going to start regulating artificial intelligence. It’s about time.

Yes, I know: Huang has been reinstated at Nvidia, SpaceX and Anthropic had record-setting IPOs, and all these companies will likely play an outsized role in the economy and in politics for years to come. But for now, I’ll be thankful that some of their hubris has been checked. Heck, even Musk is now trying to deny that he was ever MAGA—despite the abundant evidence to the contrary. Either he’s lying or he has no principles at all—there’s no third option.

7. Dogs That Didn’t Bark. As I said at the outset, there’s no shortage of things to worry about if you’re looking for reasons not to feel thankful. Efforts to reduce climate change are failing; global public health issues are rising; the wars in Ukraine, Sudan, the Persian Gulf, etc., are still raging and causing vast suffering; measles is back in the United States (give thanks to the Trump administration and especially U.S. Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. for that dubious “achievement”); and we are all wondering where the AI revolution will take us. If you want to have a gloomy Thanksgiving, therefore, that option is available.

Nonetheless, we should also pause and reflect on the bad things that could have happened but didn’t. The world economy didn’t suffer a new financial panic or depression. We didn’t face another global pandemic. Humanity marked the 81st consecutive year since Hiroshima in which nuclear weapons were not used and none of the great powers fought each other. The European Union didn’t collapse, the United States didn’t invade Greenland, China didn’t blockade or attack Taiwan, and the war in the Persian Gulf didn’t escalate in truly catastrophic fashion (e.g. large-scale attacks on desalinization plants).

8. Guilty (and Not So Guilty) Pleasures. As usual, I’m grateful for the people who educated, amused, inspired, and entertained me over the years. I enjoyed nonfiction works by Eli Clifton and Ian Lustick, Stephen Kinzer, Chad Levinson, Kimmo Rentola, and Daniel Walker Howe, as well the commentaries by David French, Paul Krugman, Lydia Polgreen, David Rothkopf, and most of the regular columnists at the Financial Times. I don’t get to read as many novels as I’d like, but Colson Whitehead, Alessandra Ranelli, Curtis Sittenfeld, Barbara Trapido, and a few others brought me a lot of enjoyment this past year. I never grow tired of rereading Raymond Chandler or Rex Stout, and I finally got around to reading Ursula K. Le Guin’s The Left Hand of Darkness, a mere 57 years after it was first published. My wife and I tapped our feet to big band jazz at Birdland, enjoyed Don Cheadle and Ayo Edebiri in Proof, and were blown away by Paul Giamatti’s performance in Eugène Ionesco’s Rhinoceros at the American Repertory Theater. The Red Sox resurgence fell short in the playoffs but their record-setting winning streak was great fun. The Yankees didn’t make the World Series, and this lifelong Dodgers fan found himself rooting for the Brewers instead. (I mean, seriously, how could one not?) And given my earlier predictions, it’s hard not to feel a bit smug about the demise of the Saudi-sponsored LIV Golf tour. I don’t watch much TV apart from sports, but I’m thankful they brought Ted Lasso back for one more season (but really, enough is enough), and I hope Apple TV renews Sugar for a third season.

9. The Golden Age of Academia. This item is bittersweet, as the world in which I have spent almost all my career is slowly vanishing. I feel enormous gratitude for having been part of it, yet I am filled with apprehension and sadness at its present trajectory. I’ve been a full-time academic for the better part of 50 years, and whatever success I have enjoyed is only partly due to my own efforts. In addition to a whole lot of lucky breaks, I was fortunate to be part of a golden era in American higher education, when society placed a high value on research and teaching, professors were generally respected, and both state and federal officials understood that a well-funded, unconstrained, and diverse academic community made the whole country stronger and wealthier.

Sadly, that world is now imperiled. A small part of the problem lies within higher education itself (excessive bureaucratization, overemphasis on intercollegiate sports, self-imposed limits on free inquiry, reluctance to police genuine misconduct, etc.), and the emergence of AI is almost certain to transform the entire enterprise in ways that are still hard to predict. But most of the trouble arises from a concerted attack on universities from the political right, a campaign that began long before Trump but has been supercharged under him. In addition to defunding key lines of research and damaging institutions once universally acknowledged as the world’s best (and a magnet for talented young people from all over the world), government efforts to impose ideological conformity on universities guarantees that the brightest minds will pursue other careers, important questions will receive inadequate attention, truth will get censored, and society will know less and misunderstand more. I remain deeply thankful for the life that I’ve been privileged to lead, and I’m alarmed by the obstacles that current and future scholars will face.

10. A Personal Note. I’ll spend a fair bit of the next three weeks reviewing the page proofs for a new book, which means I can spend Thanksgiving Day giving thanks that the damn thing is finished. The title is The Five Keys to a Safer World: A Foreign Policy Primer, and it will be published by Princeton University in late spring 2027. I’m immensely grateful to the institutions and individuals who made it possible (you can read about them in the acknowledgements), but I’m also greatly relieved that I’m not still writing it. I hope each of you can look back on some important achievement or milestone in 2026 and look forward to 2027 with anticipation and hope.

Stephen M. Walt is a columnist at Foreign Policy and the Robert and Renée Belfer professor of international relations at Harvard University. Walt is the author of The New Geopolitics, a five-part newsletter masterclass breaking down the biggest concepts you should use to navigate today’s world. #5887. Jensen Huang returns to Nvidia. And, Rep. Gomez resigns amid alleged sexual misconduct

Nvidia reinstated Jensen Huang as CEO yesterday, causing the company's stock to soar more than 30%. President Trump claimed credit for facilitating Huang's return. The CEO's comeback follows his resignation just two weeks earlier due to reports of extramarital affairs, including involvement with a woman U.S. intelligence agencies suspected of being a Chinese spy. Huang, a close ally of the president, had previously donated $25 million to the new White House ballroom and security complex. His return boosted Nvidia's stock and triggered a rally in the entire stock market, revealing that one man's comeback is worth about a trillion dollars. Some individuals advocating for diversity and inclusion in Silicon Valley argue that Huang's return signals that profit is the only priority.

#5886. The Global Antiauthoritarian Wave Is an Illusion

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

LARRY M. BARTELS is University Distinguished Professor of Political Science and Law and May Werthan Shayne Chair of Public Policy and Social Science at Vanderbilt University.

Democracy Can’t Be Saved at the Ballot Box

In 2022, many observers celebrated the results of the United States’ midterm elections as a victory for democracy. The Republican Party underperformed at the ballot box, and some of the candidates most outspoken in support of former President Donald Trump’s historic effort to overturn the 2020 election outcome were among the casualties. President Joe Biden concluded that the American people “have proven once again that democracy is who we are.” Reporters for The New York Times concurred, writing that the midterm outcome demonstrated “the resilience of the country’s democracy” as the electorate rejected “openly extremist Republican candidates.”

But that rosy interpretation was more the result of wishful thinking than careful analysis. Most of the Republicans who lost were simply bad, inexperienced candidates. In fact, Republican members of Congress who had flouted the democratic process by voting to decertify electoral votes on January 6, 2021, suffered no discernible penalty at the polls, while the minority who had stood up for the rule of law faced stiff backlash from their party’s establishment and from primary voters. And, of course, two years later, Trump stormed back into the White House.

In this year’s midterms, Trump’s party took another, even bigger beating. And again, there has been a rush to celebrate the result as a triumph for democratic values. NPR reported that “one of the big, clear winners of the midterms is American democracy itself.” Amanda Taub, an international affairs columnist at The New York Times, upped the ante, writing that “An anti-authoritarian wave appears to be crashing over elections around the world.” Just as in 2022, however, there is reason to fear that this optimistic account seriously misconstrues what happened at the polls on November 3 and what the result means for the future. History shows that voters in the United States, as in other democracies, are much more focused on the competence of their political leaders and their own well-being than on adherence to democratic procedures. To imagine that they have suddenly become guardians of democratic norms is to misunderstand the electorate’s role in the democratic process—and to underestimate the chronic susceptibility of the American political system to democratic backsliding.

It is natural to “feel pride,” as the New York Times editorial board put it, in the notion that ordinary citizens “stood up” for democratic principles at the ballot box. Democracy is the United States’ civil religion, and the voice of the people is the closest thing Americans have to the voice of God. But as the British jurist and historian Henry Sumner Maine observed in 1885, the oracle of democracy is often “not as intelligible as might be desired.” Electoral behavior is complex, and interpretations of election outcomes constructed in the hours and days after a vote often turn out to be far off the mark. Voters in democracies are less ideological than analysts generally assume. They are also less committed to abstract democratic norms than high-minded observers might hope. In a 2020 study, the Yale political scientists Matthew Graham and Milan Svolik used survey experiments to test the impact of democratic norm violations on Americans’ support for hypothetical candidates. They concluded that “only a small fraction of Americans prioritize democratic principles in their electoral choices when doing so goes against their partisan identification or favorite policies.” In 2022, real voters demonstrated the same point with their willingness to support Republican members of Congress who had participated in Trump’s bogus “stop the steal” effort.

This year, the exit polls run by a consortium of American news organizations provided superficial support for the notion that the midterm “blue wave” was an explicit rejection of Trump’s continuing assault on democratic norms. These surveys asked voters which of six issues was the most important facing the country. A third of respondents chose “the state of democracy,” and an overwhelming proportion of those who did, 79 percent, voted for Democratic congressional candidates. Taken at face value, these data suggest that more than a quarter of the electorate voted Democratic because they opposed Trump’s efforts to subvert American democracy.

Alas, people’s explanations for their own behavior are often misleading. Responses to list choices of this sort are particularly sensitive to the specific options presented and their favorable or unfavorable connotations. Given the choice, most people would probably prefer to attribute their votes to principled support for democracy than to pique at the price of gas. But when a separate YouGov poll conducted just after the vote asked an open-ended version of the same question, only five percent of voters spontaneously cited government corruption or the undermining of democracy as the most important factor motivating their choice.

A closer look at the exit poll data offers additional grounds for skepticism regarding the importance of democratic principles for the 2026 midterm outcome. The largest swing away from the Republican Party, more than 30 points, was among young voters—who are generally much less supportive of democracy than other age cohorts. (A 2025 Kettering Foundation survey, for instance, found that although 89 percent of Silent Generation respondents agreed with the proposition that “democracy is the best form of government,” only 54 percent of Gen Z respondents did.) To suppose that young people are now the vanguard of a sudden urge to defend democracy is implausible.

Finally, a comparison between the 2026 and 2024 exit polls is telling. In 2022, well before the depredations of Trump’s second term, voters were presented a similar list choice of issues that “mattered most in deciding how you voted for president.” A similar share of voters cited “the state of democracy” that year as did this year, and a similar overwhelming majority of them reported voting for Kamala Harris. The key difference between the two elections was not in the importance that voters assigned to democratic principles but in the impact of economic grievances. In 2024, 32 percent of exit poll respondents said the economy mattered most for their vote, and 81 percent of those respondents reported voting for Trump. In 2026, 39 percent said that the economy was the most important issue facing the country—and most of them voted for Democrats.

What about the notion that “an anti-authoritarian wave appears to be crashing over elections around the world”? As with many “waves” identified by political analysts, this antiauthoritarian trend mostly amounts to a short list of cases in which autocrats (or would-be autocrats) lost elections. Taub, the New York Times commentator, pointed to Brazil, Hungary, and Serbia, along with the U.S. midterm elections, as evidence for a wave. But it is a stretch to see antiauthoritarian sentiment as the common thread explaining these outcomes.

In Brazil in October, the incumbent president, Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva (known as Lula), defeated Flávio Bolsonaro, the son of his right-wing populist predecessor, by less than four percentage points. The result was unremarkable, except for the fact that Bolsonaro had led Lula in the first round of voting. The most salient event separating the two votes was a televised debate in which Bolsonaro seemed to mock Lula for having lost a finger in a factory accident. “I was a minor and working at night,” Lula responded, “because either I worked or I did not eat. I will not accept a posh guy calling me ‘nine-fingers.’ ” It was a dramatic moment, but hardly a test of Brazilian voters’ commitment to democracy.

The April election in Hungary provides a clearer parallel with the U.S. midterm race. Viktor Orban’s Fidesz party was defeated after 16 years of autocratic rule—but Hungarians’ attitudes toward democracy likely played a smaller role in both Orban’s rise and his fall than many observers imagine. His triumph in 2010 was widely but erroneously portrayed as part of a global populist wave. In fact, data from the European Social Survey (ESS) show that his supporters were not more distrustful of politicians, more dissatisfied with democracy, more opposed to immigration or the EU, or more eager for order and tradition than other Hungarian voters. What they were, mostly, was fed up with their incumbent government.

Orban used his sweeping election victory as a pretext to substantially erode Hungarian democracy, pressuring judges and the press and fiddling with election rules to entrench Fidesz’s dominance. Those moves no doubt contributed to his long tenure in office. But the more important factor was that, from 2009 to 2019, a series of ESS surveys recorded substantial increases in ordinary Hungarians’ satisfaction with their economy, their government, their lives as a whole, and, ironically, even “the way democracy works in Hungary.” When that sense of well-being faded, Orban’s autocratic machinations proved unable to protect him. As the Christian Science Monitor reporter Mark Sappenfield put it, “The story of his political demise is, in the end, not so different from countless democratic elections everywhere. The economy stagnated, highlighting levels of corruption that became intolerable to voters, who then lost trust in Mr. Orbán’s ability to guide them to better lives.”

Much the same could be said of Trump in 2026. One of the oldest and best-supported generalizations in political science is the law of the pendulum. It asserts that the political costs of governing—stemming from, for example, ideological overreach, complacency, scandals, or economic shocks—tend to erode support for incumbent political leaders and their parties, resulting in the restoration of competitive balance and periodic alteration of parties in power. Of course, these political costs do not accumulate with anything like the uniformity implied by the image of a pendulum swinging consistently from left to right and back again. Some governments are beset by problems and succumb in short order, while others face smoother seas and survive—for a time. But no democratic government lasts forever.

When incumbents use state resources—sometimes including legal and physical intimidation—to entrench themselves in power, it may feel like the law of the pendulum has been suspended. Nonetheless, even the most dominant political machines succumb eventually to hard times, economic and social change, internal divisions, and mounting pressure from those excluded from power. As the Vanderbilt political scientist Jessica Trounstine noted in a 2008 study of the rise and fall of political “bosses” in U.S. cities, the result may be chaotic: “In many cases the fall of monopolies set in motion periods of intense factionalism during which gridlock was common.” Turnover may result in protracted turmoil or in the emergence of a new machine, perhaps under the banner of reform. The fall of a strongman or boss does not automatically produce a well-functioning democratic system.

If characterizing the midterm elections as a triumph for democratic norms misconstrues why voters made their choices, it also risks misrepresenting what comes next. The New York Times editorial board envisioned quick progress to halt democratic backsliding: “The outcome creates a crucial check on Mr. Trump’s autocratic behavior. . . . The country already feels changed, and it will in fact be changed in January.”

Changed, certainly; but the president and his allies are not going anywhere, and they are far from powerless. A glance around the world provides examples of the intense factionalism and gridlock that may be in store for the United States during the next two years. In Poland, an autocratic government fell in 2023 after eight years in power. But the holdover president, Karol Nawrocki, has battled Prime Minister Donald Tusk’s institutional reform efforts. Tusk’s attempt to remake the constitutional court—packed with friends of the former regime—precipitated a constitutional crisis pitting the prime minister and the EU against the president and mobs of right-wing protesters. Tusk’s election was just one step in what will be a long struggle to restore Poland’s liberal democracy.

The American president is much more powerful than Poland’s, providing much more scope for bitter political conflict over policies and democratic procedures. In one election-night interview, Trump claimed that he would “look forward to working with” the victorious Democrats. But earlier in the day, to CBS News’s Robert Costa, he noted his victory in the Supreme Court case Trump v. Slaughter (which upheld his right to fire a Federal Trade Commission member) and claimed that “the Slaughter case gives the president tremendous powers that it never had before.” Another person Costa described as “close to the president” told him that “while Democrats may win” the election, the president would “keep the power to do almost everything he wants.” The post-election appointment of Stephen Miller as Trump’s chief of staff does not bode well for cooperation.

Of course, Democrats hope to win back the White House in 2028. But even if they do, it would be naive to expect Trump’s legacy of democratic backsliding to be erased. He has done substantial, durable harm to the federal government and the American political system. Some of his most egregious procedural and policy changes will be rolled back, but only by diverting attention and political capital from new priorities. Others will be harder to undo. Millions of years’ worth of governing experience cast aside in Elon Musk’s ham-handed purge of the civil service will not be restored with the stroke of a pen. The massive expansion of the Immigration and Customs Enforcement agency, its extensive network of detention facilities, and its culture—which the legal scholar L. Ali Khan described as “performative cruelty”—will not be quickly or easily dismantled. The Supreme Court will not revert to the ideological mainstream any time soon.

Perhaps the most significant damage of the Trump era has been to long-standing, informal democratic norms that supposedly constrained the behavior of political leaders. Observers who expected Trump to pay a stiff political price for his abusive rhetoric, incessant lying, self-dealing, and disdain for court rulings, Congress, foreign allies, and the electoral process have learned that, as the University of Denver political scientist Seth Masket put it, “a lot of the things we think voters just won’t tolerate turn out to be tolerable to them.” That lesson, once learned, is very hard to unlearn. If future political leaders refrain from these behaviors, it will be because they want to, not because they think they have to.

Trump’s trashing of political norms accelerated a process that was already underway before he turned his sights on the White House. In 2012, the veteran political analysts Thomas Mann and Norman Ornstein argued that hyperpartisanship was eroding the compromise and self-restraint necessary to facilitate power sharing in the United States’ peculiarly decentralized national government. The logic of partisan tit for tat has encouraged each party to respond to the other’s norm violations by pushing even more aggressively to weaponize the filibuster, bypass Congress with executive orders, and gerrymander congressional district boundaries. Each successful norm violation has encouraged further probing of legal and political redlines.

Hyperpartisanship will not disappear when Trump departs the political scene; he is its consequence more than its cause. Most important, the passion that has fueled the Make America Great Again movement will not vanish. Millions of Americans still see the waves of social change that have washed over the United States since the 1960s as a cultural disaster, and the looming prospect of a majority-minority country as a threat to be battled, by undemocratic means if necessary. They will find new champions.

A survey commissioned by NPR and PBS to mark the United States’ 250th birthday found that 82 percent of Americans agreed that “the issues that divide the nation” constitute “a serious threat to the future of our democracy.” If that seems alarmist, it is worth noting that 37 percent of the same respondents in the same survey—and 58 percent of those under the age of 30—agreed that “Americans may have to resort to violence in order to get the country back on track.” When people view political conflict in existential terms, they are unlikely to feel constrained by high-minded democratic theory.
